0,2026-11-14,"On January 6th, 2025, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang got his rockstar moment at last. He strode triumphantly onstage, hand raised in the air, to the claps and cheers of a 14,000-person arena absolutely packed with influencers and fans, their faces awash in blue glow…"
1,2026-11-14,"Andy Burnham became prime minister in July
The prime minister has told members of his party that Welsh Labour may have lost sight of some of the principles of devolution before it was thrown out of power in a historic election defeat.
On Saturday morning Andy Burnham addressed the first Welsh Labour conference since May's Senedd election.
He suggested the previous Labour government did not do enough to pass powers down to communities, and said it was ""hard"" to accept the party was no longer in power after 27 years of running the Welsh government.
He spoke before a review into what went wrong concluded that former First Minister Eluned Morgan's political strategy of calling out Westminster played a role in the party losing power.
The report by Prof Anand Menon, of PF Global, said Morgan's calls for constitutional change like the devolution of policing and the Crown Estate did not get through to voters.
A source close to the former first minister said it was ""ludicrous"" to suggest that Morgan was focused on constitutional issues.
Meanwhile, Welsh Labour leader Ken Skates has criticised his party for focusing too much on powers for the Senedd.
In a subdued speech, Burnham said he wanted to see ""one Labour team"" where ""everyone"" was ""signed up and on the same page.""
He spoke ahead of the publication of a review of what went wrong at the election.
Since he became prime minister he has been lobbying the Welsh government to devolve more powers to local councils.
""Somewhere along the way perhaps we lost sight of some of the principles that drove devolution in the first place.
""We probably stopped listening as much as we should have, and actually stopped driving power down into every Welsh postcode which is what the vision to me really should always have been about - about people and places.""
He said the party would accept its defeat and ""come back stronger"" with a ""vision for a stronger Wales in a fairer United Kingdom.""
Burnham said he wanted to achieve the sense of a ""one Labour team for Wales"" where the party is ""pulling in the same direction"".
""Where I'm pulling with you and not creating problems for you or going against what Ken or colleagues want to achieve.
""We will put forward a vision for communities up and down Wales, as well as giving Wales a stronger national voice.""
Prime Minister Andy Burnham said he wanted to achieve the sense of a ""one Labour team for Wales""
Addressing the event at Venue Cymru in Llandudno, Burnham said he loved ""everything about Wales"".
He said his last visit to Wales - to Aberfan - left the ""biggest mark"" of his visits to the country.
Referencing the fight for justice in the years after the disaster, Burnham said he wanted a ""country where all people and all places are treated fairly and with dignity"".
He said his vision for the country is a ""reindustrialised nation with revitalised high streets"".
Burnham said Labour should be ""immensely proud"" of the things it achieved in Wales - including free school meals, free prescriptions and the creation of Transport for Wales.
The prime minister said he wanted to show how ""being in the UK is good for Wales because of the partnership that we can develop together"".
Burnham said he had put the full devolution of employment support on the table with the Welsh government.
It was announced at the Budget that standing charges for gas will come to an end. Burnham confirmed that the charges will be scrapped in the new year.
Menon's criticism of the Red Welsh Way was one of several findings in his review of what went wrong at the May election.
It included that the party suffered a long-term decline in support to other left wing parties, laid bare as Labour took flak for dissatisfaction with public services at both UK and Welsh levels.
It said ""weaknesses in political communication"" also left the party without ""clear answers when voters wanted them"".
The review says Labour should set itself against Plaid Cymru's ""constitutional project"", by seeking more powers based on what they would deliver rather than ""debating powers in the abstract"", prioritising the NHS, the economy and the cost of living.
It said voters felt they had not delivered on all three issues.
Eluned Morgan made history in May's Senedd election, becoming the first leader of a government in the UK to lose her seat while in office
Morgan sought to differentiate herself from Sir Keir Starmer with a flagship speech in 2025, where she set out her approach called the Red Welsh Way.
Morgan said she was willing to ""call out"" Westminster Labour, and demanded the devolution of the crown estate to the Senedd.
Menon's report said the strategy ""struggled to land on everyday policy, instead tied up in constitutional positioning and the relationship with Westminster"".
Naming Morgan, the report said her policy recalled Rhodri Morgan's ""clear red water"" approach but ""was not its equivalent"".
""Demands such as transferring the Crown Estate and devolving policing did not speak to voters on the doorstep, who found themselves more concerned with the economy, cost of living and the NHS.""
The Labour campaign shifted from Red Welsh Way to ""power in partnership"" - attempting to send signals about the relationship with UK Labour in government. The report said that ""still did not provide clear policy objectives"".
Labour has shared most of Plaid's constitutional demands, including the devolution of policing, the Crown Estate and rail devolution, with the exception of independence.
Plaid beat Welsh Labour in the May election, and is now running the Welsh government.
Menon's report said the result was ""not necessarily"" about Welsh identity, saying that ""has been flat for a decade"".
But, it added: ""Welsh Labour voters who switch to Plaid do score more strongly on their Welsh identity than those who stayed.""
It said voters ""tend to judge whether a party stands up for Wales mainly on delivery: The NHS, the cost of living and the economy.""
A source close to Morgan said it was ""ludicrous"" to suggest she was focused on constitutional issues, adding that she had concentrated on the NHS and bringing quality jobs to Wales.
The source claimed Welsh Labour had run a ""terrible campaign"" which was ""lacklustre"", and said the biggest issue was the unpopularity of Sir Keir.
On Morgan's resignation as Welsh Labour leader, which came within minutes of her failure to retain her seat in the Senedd, the source accused the Welsh party of trying to ""control"" her, even ""down to the words of her resignation speech"".
Former First Minister Mark Drakeford said of the suggestion that calls for more devolution to the Senedd did not cut through to voters: ""It was less it seemed to me that it didn't cut through to voters, that it hadn't cut through to our colleagues in Westminster.""
""We needed evidence from our colleagues in London that that was still the case and unfortunately that evidence just wasn't there in a way that allowed us to convince the Welsh population of that.""
The review said that NHS and access to healthcare were the biggest concern for 79% of voters who left Labour for Plaid and 80% who left Labour for Reform.
The report said only 8% of Labour to Plaid switchers named devolution or independence as a top issue. 
However, 49% wanted a stronger Senedd, while 26% wanted independence.
It said 73% of adults preferred more power for regions, councils and local areas to more power for the Senedd.
PF Global polled 1,387 adults from August to September 2026.

2,2026-11-14,"Thursday, November 5, was among Jake Woodson's more chaotic days as a trader at Goldman Sachs. AI stocks were plummeting after the midterms, all three benchmark indexes were falling, and, in the middle of it all, Woodson's phone was ringing with one of the most important calls of his career.
Everyone on the trading floor went quiet as Woodson, 34, said hello to firm president John Waldron, who congratulated him on being promoted to partner, Goldman's highest-ranking title outside the C-Suite. Woodson, who oversees trading in leveraged corporate debt and loans and was previously one of Business Insider's Rising Stars of Wall Street, is the second-youngest among the 109 executives who earned the promotion this year.
""It was high-fives and hugs, and then right back to trading for the rest of the day,"" Woodson said early the next week, adding that, though his memory of the moment is foggy, he thinks colleagues on the leveraged finance credit desk applauded.
Woodson said he took a few minutes during the work day to call his mom, dad, and brother. He rang his teary-eyed mom first, partly since he'd flown her to New York from his hometown outside of Vancouver, Canada.
""I flew my mom out because I figured either I'm not going to make it and I could use a shoulder to cry on, or I really want her there to be a part of it,"" Woodson said. ""I've lived a six-hour flight from my family for 12 years, so it's nice to be able to show them it was worth it.""
His mom joined Woodson and Goldman's larger credit business that evening in the private room of Monk McGinn's, a pub near the office. She mingled with around 50 internal colleagues and 20 clients who came, meeting some of the people who have defined her son's life for more than a decade. None of Woodson's family works in finance, and he said his mom doesn't know much about his job beyond the fact that it happens at Goldman, so it was special to bring her into his world.
The two of them ducked out of the pub earlier than most to have a quiet dinner at Woodson's apartment, FaceTiming his dad and brother in Canada. Woodson celebrated again at a dinner with friends on Friday night, before his mom flew back to Vancouver and he drove to the Catskills alone. He spent Saturday by a lake, breathing air more similar to that of the mountains in British Columbia than of Tribeca.
Woodson didn't always expect to end up at Goldman, even though he studied finance in college. He grew up in a suburb 45 minutes outside of Vancouver and went to the University of British Columbia rather than the Ivy League schools that tend to dot Wall Street resumes.
""If you were a betting person, you probably wouldn't bet that there would be a Goldman partner coming from that suburb,"" Woodson said.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
He was accepted into UBC's Portfolio Management Foundation, a program that provides hands-on training in investing and finance to a small group of students. That program, with its strong alumni network and ties to Goldman, changed his life more than anything else.
Woodson joined Goldman in 2014, starting in investment-grade private credit. He was a distressed credit trader during the pandemic, and, as the head of US distressed credit trading, steered his team through the 2023 banking crisis, before becoming the head of leveraged finance trading this fall.
The trader feels some responsibility for representing those who don't start as interns, or come from a target school, or tick most of the other stereotypical finance-kid boxes. He said he's one of many at Goldman who want to ensure that the firm continues to invest in a range of young talent.
When he was one of those younger people, Woodson probably would have expected making partner to feel like a final, grand accomplishment.
""It's almost the opposite. It's just the beginning of growing into this role and continuing to operate at a high level,"" he said, adding that most focused on strengthening his team.
Balancing the daily demands of trading and managing people ""how they deserve to be managed"" will likely become a bigger challenge, Woodson said, and may just demand working more hours.
Woodson is one of the rare leaders who is simultaneously respected and liked, a former boss told Business Insider last year. Part of that, Woodson said, comes from treating people, whether internal colleagues or clients, kindly and well, even when it seems disadvantageous in the short term.
""It was instilled in me by my mom and maybe Canadian culture that treating people the right way over time is the right thing to do for a number of reasons. It's the right thing to do morally, but it also tends to lead to better outcomes,"" he said.
Beyond setting a good example, his mom also gave him one of the best gifts he received: appropriately cheesy, multicolored, six-foot-tall balloons.

3,2026-11-14,"The search for Nvidia's new CEO is officially underway.
The company's board of directors announced that CEO and founder Jensen Huang resigned ""effective immediately"" in a statement on Wednesday. The board tapped Colette Kress, executive vice president and chief financial officer, as interim CEO until a permanent successor is found.
Huang resigned three days after he admitted to having an affair, and Nvidia's board opened an investigation into whether he was the ""target of a foreign intelligence effort.""
As the fallout around Nvidia continues, analysts, investors, and prediction market bettors have begun to wonder who could helm the company next. Here's who they're pitching.
The CEO of Arm Holdings, a semiconductor manufacturing company, is one name that's been bandied about. On Wednesday, The Financial Times reported that Nvidia has approached Rene Haas as it searches for a successor. Sources told the outlet that it's not guaranteed Haas will be offered the role or accept it.
Haas previously served as vice president and general manager of Nvidia's computing products business unit for 7 years.
Reuters reported that analysts and investors view Ian Buck, Nvidia's vice president of hyperscale and high-performance computing, as a possible candidate for the top job.
""The permanent job is a different story, though, because Nvidia's edge comes from its technology, and the board would likely want an engineer in the chair, Josh Gilbert, a lead analyst at eToro, told Reuters. ""Someone like Ian Buck, who led the software platform that ​keeps developers tied to Nvidia's chips, is the sort of internal name you'd expect to come up.""
Prediction market users are also banking on Buck. He's at the top of the list on both Kalshi and Polymarket.
Reuters reported that analysts and investors also view interim CEO Colette Kress as someone who could do the job long-term. Armina Rosenberg, a cofounder at Minotaur Capital, which owns Nvidia stock, told Reuters that finding a new permanent CEO won't be quick.
""The more realistic route is an interim leader from the long-serving inner circle, possibly as a co-CEO structure: one leader to steady the business and the ​finances, such as Colette Kress, alongside a product and technology leader, such as Ian Buck,"" Rosenberg told the outlet.
President Donald Trump also weighed in on the search for Nvidia's new CEO, recommending former Apple CEO Tim Cook. Cook stepped down as CEO in September, but remains executive chairman.
""NOW THAT Nvidia CEO JENSEN HUANG, who has done a fantastic job, has resigned, there is only one man to take his place - TIM COOK of Apple. A Legend replaces a Legend!"" Trump wrote in a Truth Social post on Thursday.
Dan Ives, a partner and senior managing director at Yorkville Ives & Co., had a few ideas of his own. During an interview with Fortune, Ives named Google Cloud CEO Thomas Kurian as a promising candidate.
Kurian joined Google in 2019 after working at Oracle and McKinsey.
Palo Alto Networks CEO Nikesh Arora is getting swept up in the speculation, too. An Nvidia shareholder told The Economic Times that Arora is among his top picks to replace Huang. Ives also said Arora is on his list.
Johny Srouji, the senior vice president of hardware technology at Apple, is another name popping up in conversations. A person familiar with the matter told Semafor that Srouji is being considered for the CEO role.

7,2026-11-14,"Cars are expensive. So, why not drive more two-wheeled options?
That's what Also, a spinoff from EV-maker Rivian, is trying to convince customers. After months of delays, the company has begun delivering its $3,500 to $4,500 TM-B e-bikes to customers.
""The bike is almost like a Trojan horse for what's coming,"" Chris Yu, the cofounder and president, told Business Insider during a sit-down interview.
As cheap new cars keep disappearing from the US market and the average vehicle transaction price hovers above $51,000, several companies see an opportunity with small vehicles.
Toyota, for example, is bringing a tiny kei truck to the US market with a max speed of 25 mph. Fiat's golf cart-like Topolino launched this summer. Business Insider has also spoken to several Slate Auto early adopters who plan to use their $24,900 trucks as around-town cars.
Also thinks you can accomplish the same trips on a bike.
Yu's main argument: Around-town transportation should take a page out of the aviation sector's book. There's a wide array of plane types that perform very different tasks — but on American roads, DoorDash deliveries, school drop-offs, and hourslong trips are all accomplished with the same vehicles.
That's ""like we're using a 747"" to go shopping, he said.
Yu's vision isn't a replacement for the ever-popular SUV. Instead, 10 years in the future, the perfect garage will have a family SUV for long-haul trips, an e-bike for school drop-off, and a yet-to-be-announced Also product that could accomplish a family's grocery run autonomously.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Also began as a skunkworks project inside Rivian before rolling out as a stand-alone company. It's promising software-heavy products, including the TM-B, which gets a touchscreen, removable battery, and pedal-by-wire drivetrain.
The company partnered with DoorDash this year to develop and deploy autonomous delivery vehicles, and said its recent $150 million funding round would help it develop multiple autonomous vehicle types for moving people and goods.
Getting that first proof point on the road has not been easy. Also missed its original spring delivery target for the TM-B, and customers only began receiving the bike this fall.
Yu said the company was squeezed by surging demand for memory, chips, and high-performance circuit boards — all components used in AI servers and robotics. He said the delays taught ALSO to communicate more regularly with its reservation holders.
""Our customers called us out, and rightfully,"" he told Business Insider. ""People care. I remind employees all the time, it's an incredible privilege for us to have a bunch of people care; there are so many companies that don't have a Reddit audience.""

8,2026-11-14,"Author of the AI Insider newsletter
I'm not a conspiracy theorist. But sometimes new information emerges that makes a wild theory worth considering.
That's where I find myself with the extraordinary downfall of Jensen Huang, the cofounder and longtime CEO of Nvidia, America's most important AI company.
Huang resigned this week after revelations that he had an extramarital affair with a Chinese businesswoman suspected of spying for Beijing.
The woman, Alice Huang, was also an executive at a company that bought billions of dollars worth of Nvidia's powerful AI chips and has been investigated over concerns that some of those products may have been illegally diverted to China.
The allegations were explosive. Nvidia's board moved surprisingly quickly; Huang was out, and the company's stock plunged, wiping about $300 billion off its market value.
But now some of America's most respected news organizations are publishing well-sourced reports that raise serious questions about the central allegation in this scandal.
And those reports point toward another possible explanation for Huang's abrupt departure. What if the spy allegations were exaggerated to achieve a political goal?
That might sound like a conspiracy theory. Until a few days ago, I would have been reluctant to give it much consideration. Now I'm not so sure.
On Thursday, CNN reported that US intelligence agencies have not established that Alice Huang was actually spying for China.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
There is disagreement among officials about whether she was recruited by China's intelligence services. Some CIA officials are unconvinced that intercepted communications prove she knowingly passed information to Beijing.
The lead reporter on this explosive piece is Natasha Bertrand, an accomplished national security reporter whose investigations have scrutinized both Republican and Democratic administrations, and whose reporting on Russia's invasion of Ukraine helped earn her team an Emmy.
The possibility that Alice Huang was a Chinese intelligence agent was likely the key reason Nvidia's board ousted Jensen Huang so quickly. (Business Insider has not confirmed whether Alice Huang was or is a Chinese spy.)
Now, there's an open letter calling for the board to reinstate him as CEO until this is cleared up. It has over 100,000 signatures, including top executives and current Nvidia employees.
""We have not been shown evidence substantiating the allegation that the woman at the center of this controversy was a spy,"" the letter reads. ""Suspicion or association alone is not a sufficient basis for ending Jensen's leadership.""
There's more.
On Friday, The Washington Post reported that Secretary of State Marco Rubio, a prominent China hawk, took a less conclusive CIA intelligence report and used it to write his own memo warning President Donald Trump about Huang's relationship with an alleged Chinese spy.
According to a person familiar with the matter, Rubio recast the CIA's findings in more assertive language. The memo's findings eventually leaked, setting off the chain of events that ended Huang's 33-year reign at Nvidia, according to the Washington Post.
The Post's reporting doesn't prove Rubio deliberately misrepresented the intelligence or set out to remove Huang. But it raises a question I think deserves serious attention: Was intelligence about a possible Chinese spy amplified to help remove a powerful obstacle to tougher restrictions on China?
There's an uncomfortable historical precedent here.
In 2003, the US invaded Iraq after the Bush administration repeatedly warned that Saddam Hussein possessed weapons of mass destruction. Secretary of State Colin Powell famously presented intelligence to the United Nations to support that case.
After the invasion, investigators found that Iraq did not possess the stockpiles of weapons that had been alleged. The intelligence was badly wrong.
There has been extensive debate ever since about how much of that failure reflected flawed intelligence, political pressure, selective use of evidence, or deliberate exaggeration.
But the broader lesson is clear: Intelligence assessments can be presented with more certainty than the underlying evidence supports, particularly when powerful people have a policy goal they want to achieve.
The Bush administration wanted to invade Iraq. Claims about weapons of mass destruction helped build support for that decision.
So, if intelligence about Alice Huang was exaggerated, who stood to benefit?
To understand the possible motive, you need to understand a bitter fight inside Washington over China's access to Nvidia technology.
For years, the US government has tried to stop China from obtaining Nvidia's most powerful AI chips, which are used to develop advanced artificial intelligence. The concern is that China could use this technology to strengthen its military and challenge America's technological leadership.
But enforcing these restrictions has proved surprisingly difficult. One reason is cloud computing. Here's how it works.
An American company sells AI chips to a data center operator. Those chips stay physically outside mainland China. The operator then rents that computing power to customers over the internet, including Chinese companies.
This kind of remote access can be legal under existing US rules, depending on the circumstances. It's also one of the main benefits of cloud computing: You don't need to own a powerful computer or even be in the same country as one to use it.
But some Washington officials see this as a loophole that undermines the whole point of restricting chip sales to China. And they want to close it.
Jensen Huang was one of the most powerful voices arguing against completely cutting China off. He argued that America is better off if Chinese AI companies continue using American technology. That gives US companies revenue, keeps China dependent on American products, and potentially gives Washington more visibility and influence over Chinese AI development.
Cut China off completely, Huang argued, and Chinese companies will simply accelerate efforts to build their own technology.
Of course, Huang also had an obvious financial interest. Nvidia makes money selling chips, and China is an enormous potential market. And he had direct access to Trump and had repeatedly pushed the administration toward a more accommodating approach.
Now he's gone. And the people who wanted tougher restrictions have shed their biggest opponent.
There's another detail about all this that bothers me. Aspects of this relationship and the concerns about Alice Huang are not new.
On October 9, 2025, more than a year ago, The New York Times published a detailed investigation into Megaspeed, the Singapore-based company where she was an executive.
The Times reported that the company had purchased nearly $2 billion of Nvidia technology and was under investigation over possible violations of US export restrictions.
It also described Alice Huang's close access to Jensen Huang, including photographs of them together.
US officials had been investigating the company since 2024. The FBI has also been investigating the relationship for at least a year, according to CNN.
So why did all this suddenly become an existential crisis for Nvidia's CEO in November 2026?
One possible answer is that the political environment changed.
Axios reported this week on tensions inside the administration over efforts to tighten restrictions on China's access to Nvidia technology.
Meanwhile, lawmakers are pushing legislation that could restrict Chinese companies from remotely accessing powerful American chips located in other countries. The Remote Access Security Act has already passed the House and is awaiting action in the Senate.
Nvidia has opposed efforts to impose sweeping restrictions on this kind of access.
On Thursday, SemiAnalysis, a respected AI research firm, published a provocative assessment of Huang's departure.
""We aren't ones for conspiracy theories, but a few events around the same time seem to create a narrative that is hard to ignore,"" they wrote.
""The US intelligence community is a large part of the push to get Jensen out of Nvidia,"" the firm added. ""AI and chip restrictions on China are going to ramp now much faster with the biggest defender Jensen, gone.""
SemiAnalysis offered this as a theory, not proof. But it deserves examination, especially after CNN's reporting and the Washington Post's article about Rubio's memo.
I'm not here to tell readers whether China should have access to America's most advanced AI chips. There are serious arguments on both sides. And I do not condone Huang's other behavior, including CEOs having relationships with subordinates or customers.
What concerns me is the possibility that US intelligence was overstated to help bring down the leader of one of America's most important companies.
If that's what happened, officials may have damaged America's lead in AI in the name of protecting it.
A week ago, the idea that Washington's China hawks helped bring down Huang sounded like a conspiracy theory. Today, it's a possibility worth considering.
And given America's history of intelligence failures, I don't think we should simply take the government's word for it.

Stories you might be interested in"
10,2026-11-14,"Nov 14 (Reuters) - Microsoft said on Saturday that services had fully recovered across Teams, Outlook and its other products, ending a global outage that crippled online communications for businesses and governments for over 24 hours.
""We have completed our efforts to address the underlying issue of the incident and restored service to the remaining impacted customers and regions,"" the company posted on ‌X.
Microsoft had said server issues were behind the incident that also affected its Copilot AI assistant, and that it was not aware of any malicious activity, ruling out AI as the cause.
Outlook ‌and Teams are two of the most widely used workplace applications with hundreds of millions of users. The outage prevented people from performing essential functions such as sending emails, managing calendars and hosting virtual meetings.
It affected government agencies from Australia and Japan to the ​United States, caused delays at trading firms, hindered some companies from publishing earnings and prompted airlines to warn customers of communication lags, according to public statements.
Organizations that host Microsoft services on their own servers, rather than in the cloud, were somewhat spared.
ANOTHER OUTAGE FOR MICROSOFT
The outage, reports of which started climbing around 1725 GMT on Thursday according to Ookla, is one of the longest in Microsoft's history and the second disruption to its services this year.
Microsoft said the outage specifically hit features tied to Exchange Online — its cloud-based email and scheduling hub — leaving unconnected applications operational.
The incident deepens questions about the global reliance on a handful of Big Tech companies that supply both the essential software ‌and the cloud infrastructure that underpins it.
It also highlighted the dependence on ⁠Microsoft's workplace software, with outage reports spiking as employees began their workdays across Asia and Europe, according to Downdetector's parent company, Ookla.
Downdetector logged more than 7.4 million reports worldwide, peaking at nearly 20,000 a minute, more than 1,500 times normal levels.
""It shows how much of the working day now runs through one provider's email and collaboration tools,"" ⁠said Luke Kehoe, lead industry analyst at Ookla.
Microsoft shares closed flat at $523.88 on Friday. The stock has gained roughly 8% so far this year.
Disruptions and financial losses from the outage could result in numerous insurance claims, but the overall hit to insurers is expected to be limited, said Mario De Cicco, vice president of European financial institutions at Morningstar DBRS.
De Cicco estimated global insured losses from the incident at between $300 million and $500 million, well below the $400 million to $1.5 billion financial ​toll ​from the 2024 CrowdStrike outage that crashed more than 8 million Windows-based computers and caused widespread disruptions.
Microsoft also faced an outage with ​its 365 services in January this year and in October last year. Last month, ‌it reported an outage of its Azure cloud platform that impacted its suite of productivity software and a range of industries worldwide.
""The bigger worry is that this is not the first Microsoft 365 outage this year,"" said Josh Gilbert, a market analyst at trading platform eToro.
""If reliability starts to look like a recurring problem, it hands every IT department a reason to think twice before putting even more of their business in one provider's hands.""
Still, some analysts have questioned whether such incidents can push customers on to rivals due to the steep switching costs of replacing tools embedded so deeply in daily operations.
TRADES HAD TO BE CONFIRMED BY FAX
A spokesperson for the UK government said it was working with Microsoft to find a solution for the outage that hit organizations such as the NHS and London's Metropolitan Police.
Lack of access to emails and other services was impacting ‌the Metropolitan Police's ability to report some crimes online, though its ability to respond to calls was not affected.
The NHS said ​its local services were turning to alternate measures such as using IT systems or paper records.
For businesses, the fallout was widespread.
European ​exchange operator Euronext reported email disruptions, British insurer Aviva activated backups for its earnings calls and ​Danish shipping giant Maersk warned customers of delayed updates.
American AI giants OpenAI and Anthropic also faced issues with Microsoft connectors that were later resolved, while Indian airlines IndiGo, Akasa Air and ‌Air India said their email support services were impacted.
The outage also disrupted fund management ​at Taiwanese securities firms, life insurers, and investment trust ​companies. One of the companies went as far as cancelling a bond repurchase transaction, a source familiar with the situation told Reuters.
Another Taiwanese financial company resorted to using fax to confirm trades, a second source said.
In the US, electric truck maker Workhorse Group rescheduled its results on Thursday to November 16, while Switzerland's Richemont briefly delayed its first-half results announcement on Friday.
In Australia, where the spy agency ​and federal police were among organizations caught in the outage, Cyber Security Minister ‌Tony Burke said Microsoft does not suspect a cyberattack.
""The impact that they are dealing with ... is very much on email systems,"" the minister added.
(Reporting by Aditya Soni, Harshita Mary Varghese, Anzar ​Mehraj, Katha Kalia, Preetika Parashuraman, Shubham Kalia, and Akanksha Khushi in Bengaluru, Stefica Bikesh in Sydney, Chris Thomas and Mrinmay Dey in Mexico City, Liang-sa Loh in Taipei; Additional reporting ​by Jeffrey Dastin and Rhea Rose Abraham; Editing by Stephen Coates, Elaine Hardcastle, Devika Syamnath and Leroy Leo)

16,2026-11-14,"Millions of office workers got an unplanned break from email Thursday. Their bosses' favorite new AI tool got one too.A Microsoft (MSFT) outage that began midday Thursday knocked out Outlook and other workplace apps worldwide, with Downdetector logging report…"
17,2026-11-14,"By 


      Barbara Sprunt
    


                Texas Senator-elect James Talarico speaks at the Mission Ballroom during the What's Next Denver political convention on Nov. 12, 2026.
                
                    
                    David Zalubowski/AP
                    
                
hide caption

DENVER — Democrats descended on Denver for a two-day conference that was half victory party, half sober look at the road ahead on delivering for the voters who elected them.
The ""What's Next"" conference, hosted by the group Majority Democrats, was scheduled long before Democrats outperformed expectations with their best electoral performance since Watergate. It brought together rising stars — like incoming Gov. Rob Sand in Iowa and Sen.-elect James Talarico in Texas — along with strategists, organizers, state and federal lawmakers and potential presidential contenders.
Held at a concert hall, with colorful artwork of party heavy hitters dotting the venue, Democrats attended sessions like ""What Voters Want"" and ""What Needs To Change."" The overwhelming emphasis from speakers was that Democrats have to earn back trust.

                A compilation of posters on display at the What's Next conference in Denver on November 12, 2026.
                hide caption

""We won races in places where we haven't won in a long time, even in some places where we've never won. And the message shouldn't be that [voters] are fully embracing the Democratic Party,"" said Colorado Rep. Jason Crow in an interview.
""It took us decades to dig ourselves in a hole and to push people away from us, and it's going to take us more than one cycle to earn that trust,"" said Crow, who is one of the party's recruitment co-chairs.

                Attendees cheer during the What's Next summit. Following the midterm elections, prominent Democrats and political insiders gathered for the event to evaluate election results, and debate party strategy.
                
                    
                    Mark Makela/Getty Images/Getty Images North America
                    
                
hide caption

Anderson Clayton, chair of the North Carolina Democratic Party, took the party to task for not investing more in the South and in Black voters.
""When we say that Black voters are the base of our party, then I ask why are we not investing in a region of the country that on Tuesday night just proved everybody wrong of what is possible,"" said Clayton to much applause. ""Rural America deserves the investment from the Democratic Party and we must run towards communities that our party has run away from.""
Former Transportation Secretary Pete Buttigieg said Democrats shouldn't rest on their laurels.
""I think we are adequately politically traumatized by the last few cycles, so that we will not draw the wrong lessons from all of this,"" Buttigieg said. ""Because here's the thing: it is yet to be written whether what we just experienced is one more oscillation from one side to the other and back, or whether it is the beginning of a tectonic movement changing American politics.""
He said Democrats can learn from the current White House on two fronts, by having: ""a bias towards action and a readiness to get things done coming out of the gate and at the very same time a readiness to plan for the long-term,"" adding Democrats should come out of the gate strong, with a rapid-fire series of bills to lower the cost of living.
Presumptive next Speaker of the House Hakeem Jeffries told the audience he has plans to do just that, with a robust agenda that includes reversing Medicaid cuts, banning congressional stock trading and mid-decade redistricting, establishing a statutory right to abortion, and overturning the Supreme Court's Citizens United decision that did away with limits on political spending by corporations and unions. 
The conference itself was peppered with potential presidential contenders, including Buttigieg, New Jersey Sen. Cory Booker, Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro, Arizona Sen. Ruben Gallego, and California Rep. Ro Khanna, one of the few progressive speakers.
No one announced an official run, but many gave previews of their theory of the case.
""I do think it's important that whoever we hire to be the chief executive of this country knows how to run something, knows how to get stuff done, knows how to not just have a vision but knows how to deliver on it,"" Shapiro told reporters.
Someone who was previously kicking the tires for a possible White House bid — Maryland Sen. Chris Van Hollen — told reporters he's no longer considering it, as he instead pursues the top leadership post in the Senate.

                Sen. Elissa Slotkin, D-Mich., speaks during the What's Next summit on Nov. 13 in Denver.
                
                    
                    Mark Makela/Getty Images/Getty Images North America
                    
                
hide caption

Throughout the conference, Democrats debated whether and how to work with the Trump administration in order to deliver meaningful change for voters.
""If slapping Trump's name on it and putting it in gold letters would get it through — hell yes, I would do that,"" said Michigan Sen. Elissa Slotkin.
Talarico agrees.
""The American people cannot wait two years for change, so we must work with President Trump,"" Talarico said. ""We should make the final two years of his presidency the most productive of any American presidency.""
But New Jersey Gov. Mikie Sherrill added a note of caution, describing the president as a bad faith actor.
""The art of the deal to Trump is he figures out what you want, he leverages the you know what out of you, and keeps taking, taking, taking, and never delivers,"" Sherrill said. ""My advice to Sen. Talarico is if Trump wants to work with us, we will lay out the agenda, and he can sign it.""
NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews.
                Anyone who makes such an offer or request is not an NPR employee and does not represent NPR.

Become an NPR sponsor"

Stories you might be interested in"
22,2026-11-14,"Growing up in Guayama, a town nicknamed ""witch city"" in Puerto Rico, Maritza Ciliberto heard the rumors that her family was cursed.
She didn't believe in curses, rejecting the idea that anyone could ever deserve to get sick, but she did believe there was something lurking in her relatives' brains — a ""sleeping, giant monster"" waiting to emerge.
First, it came for her grandmother, who struggled with daily chores. Then her mother, who began repeating the same questions over and over. Eventually, her mother, two of her aunts, and one uncle died from a rare and inherited form of early-onset Alzheimer's disease due to a genetic mutation.
Ciliberto knew she could be next.
Now 57, she's lived past the age when her relatives began experiencing cognitive decline. And for more than a decade, she's been trying an extraordinary treatment, hoping to keep her mind intact.
Every two weeks, a nurse comes to her home to administer an experimental Alzheimer's drug intravenously, a process that takes about three hours. It's a lifelong therapy routine with serious potential side effects, including the possibility of brain bleeding.
The technique, still under investigation, requires a big commitment from people who show no outward signs of decline. She's endured migraines, anxiety, and years of medical monitoring, all in the hope of preventing the illness that has afflicted her family for generations.
Ciliberto is part of a small cohort of people helping scientists explore a provocative possibility: What if Alzheimer's could be stopped in its tracks before symptoms set in?
Over 50 years and billions of dollars have been poured into Alzheimer's research, and still, there are no great treatments. Even the best drugs have only modest benefits, slowing the progression of the disease. Now researchers are testing whether those same treatments could prevent the dementia ""monster"" from ever waking up by clearing out the amyloid plaque that seems to propel Alzheimer's.
Ciliberto doesn't know yet whether the drugs are working. Neither do her doctors. Still, the implications are huge, and not just for people like her with a genetic predisposition. Several new and unconventional clinical trials are testing preventative treatments in people who have no symptoms yet but appear to have Alzheimer's related changes in their brains.
The new research raises an existential question: If a treatment could reduce — or wipe out — your risk of developing dementia, how much would you risk to get it?
For more than 120 years, doctors have tried to treat Alzheimer's disease after symptoms appear with barely any success.
The best doctors could do was prescribe drugs that helped temporarily manage symptoms, like antidepressants for agitation or neurotransmitter-boosting drugs for confusion. Then, a few years ago, doctors started prescribing a new generation of treatments designed to attack the disease itself by flushing out amyloid plaques that clog the brain.
The first, Aduhelm, was controversially approved by the FDA in 2021 at a speculative stage, and was later taken off the market after disappointing results. Two newer amyloid-clearing drugs have since been approved, using the same technique but with stronger clinical data.
Experts are still torn about the utility of these treatments. A recent independent analysis of the available evidence published in the gold-standard Cochrane Review generated a ton of heated discussion in the field when it found the dementia-slowing effects of these new anti-amyloid therapies to be ""trivial"" at best, while still increasing the risk of potentially deadly brain bleeds.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Still, what if the drugs weren't working well because they were being given at the wrong time? By the time people with Alzheimer's are showing the first signs of memory loss, forgetting where they put their keys, missing appointments, or getting confused while doing everyday chores, their brains have been quietly changing for decades. Scientists are investigating whether the real reason that amyloid-clearing drugs haven't worked very well yet is because they're being given to people too late.
Ciliberto and 39 others like her are among the first patients to help test this theory. And doctors say that after a decade of monitoring, their data looks promising.
""When you look at them, the earlier the person was when they started that treatment trial, the better off they did,"" Dr. Randall Bateman, the neurologist running the DIAN clinical trial that Ciliberto is part of at Washington University School of Medicine in St. Louis, said. ""That's an arrow. It's saying: Go earlier. Go earlier!'""
It's still too early to be sure, but by 2030, if the results still hold, ""it will be incontrovertible proof that we can delay the onset of dementia,"" he said.
Ciliberto has re-molded her life around taking this drug. First, she did it quietly while still a busy public school principal, a mom, and a student finishing her doctorate degree. Now retired, she fits the twice-monthly treatments around a less stressful, but ever-busy schedule.
Ciliberto's genetics make her an unusually clear candidate for preventive treatment. For most people, the calculation is less straightforward.
New blood tests can find out if amyloid is building up in your brain, but that alone doesn't mean you'll have dementia. Neither does the presence of genetic markers like APOE4, which is associated with a higher risk of Alzheimer's.
That hasn't stopped drugmakers from exploring repurposing their existing drugs to test if they can be used as preventive treatments. Both Eli Lilly and Eisai, the two drug companies with anti-amyloid therapies on the market, are testing their infusions in people who have no outward signs of dementia, but who have high levels of amyloid in their brains. Initial results are expected in 2027 for Eli Lilly, and 2028 for Eisai.
If it works, Alzheimer's — a disease that's long been considered a slow, heartbreaking and inevitable march toward death — could be relegated to the category of chronic, manageable age-related conditions, like high blood pressure, high cholesterol, diabetes, or arthritis. It's a huge gamble to make, but if the bet pays off, it would be transformative, arresting a disease that hits 1 in 9 Americans over 65.
""Nobody cares how much amyloid is really in your brain,"" neuropsychologist Laurie Ryan, chief of the clinical interventions and diagnostics branch at the National Institute on Aging, said. ""You care if you get dementia, right? That's really what we want to stop.""
Leading neurologist Richard Isaacson, who's been focused on treating and preventing Alzheimer's for decades, is skeptical. He said there's no way this treatment is going to solve Alzheimer's.
""In the right person, at the right dose, and used for the right duration of time, I believe that anti-amyloid drugs could be a really important additive treatment for people at risk for developing dementia,"" said Isaacson, who wasn't involved in any of these anti-amyloid trials.
As a doctor, he is both excited and extremely cautious about where these treatments are headed. He's seen, over the past five years, how well some patients do on these new drugs, but he's also seen patients with lots of amyloid plaques who don't ever develop signs of dementia, while in other cases, ""you could do everything right,"" and yet ""these drugs don't help them.""
""I go back to the old saying: Once you've seen one person with Alzheimer's, you've seen one person with Alzheimer's,"" he said.
Ciliberto is confident the strategy is working for her. She said she has regular consultations with an independent neurologist and neuropsychologist, tracking her brain data. She needs that reassurance, the flood of information suggesting that what she's doing, and the energy and stress she's taken on, is really worth it.
She knows lifestyle plays a role, too. Already fluent in three languages, she's back in school, earning a master's degree in theology and working toward a lifelong dream: becoming an ordained minister at her church.
""I just don't want the stress of the type of high-demanding job that I had before, because I also know that stress was not helping me,"" she said.
Like Ciliberto, Jake Heinrichs has remolded his life around the idea that the amyloid-clearing drugs he's been taking for more than a decade are working. They've convinced him to become something he never thought he would: a father.
Heinrichs' dad developed Alzheimer's when he was still a little boy, and had died by the time he was 14. His older brother, David, died from Alzheimer's several years ago, at age 49. He knew if he was going to get this disease, he didn't want to give another generation this pain.
Heinrichs is 52 now, already three years older than his brother was when he died of Alzheimer's, and he's showing no signs of cognitive decline.
""In Jake and other people's cases, they are discovering that Alzheimer's is going to be a chronic condition — potentially — rather than a fatal condition,"" his wife, Broadway producer Rachel Chavkin, said after dropping off their son at soccer practice. ""It will be about prevention, not about treatment.""
That conceptual shift, from helplessness to empowerment, has been the shift that Heinrichs needed to become a father.
""We decided that this is something that we can do with our future: to make a family and not live in terror of passing that part of my gene on to my kid,"" Heinrichs said. ""In my life, my kid is the physical embodiment of my hope.""
Every year, Heinrichs heads to Washington University in St. Louis to check his progress, with three days of brain scans, blood draws, a painful lumbar puncture to tap into cerebrospinal fluid, which can reveal the buildup of Alzheimer's-related proteins, including amyloid. He also sits for some interviews to test his memory.
""You did good,"" Chavkin said, eyeing him across the coffee table, remembering their most recent visit.
""I did all right,"" he said, smiling. ""But I was cheating.""
Heinrichs said he employed what's called a ""memory palace"" technique to remember some of the details on his mental exam. The visualization strategy can be a tool to aid memory and recall, but it requires building an imagined physical space to place objects or facts you need to remember inside your head. It's certainly not a technique someone with dementia would be very good at.
Already, wealthy clients are pressing their doctors for early access to these drugs, an elite trend Bateman said he is aware of, though he insists he's not participating in it. It's not only an ethical principle that keeps him from prescribing the drugs prophylactically, but it's a scientific one as well.
Bateman knows there is no way to pinpoint precisely which patients with high levels of amyloid will develop Alzheimer's. With familial Alzheimer's, disease progression is a given. For the rest of us, there's the possibility you'd take a medication with real, serious side effects, and get no upside.
""I'm not ready to do that,"" Isaacson said of using anti-amyloid drugs preventatively.
Early trial results have encouraged Cilberto, especially when she thinks of her kids, who are now starting their careers and settling down. ""The science, now more than ever before, it's projecting a very hopeful future,"" she said.
Twice a month, she hosts a little tea party. She brings out cookies and warm Irish tea and invites just one special guest into her living room. It's her nurse, who will deliver the IV infusion of anti-amyloid therapy she hopes will protect her mind. The whole process takes about three hours, including time for warming up the infusion, and plugging it into her arm. Often, there's a nap in the middle of the party.
Teatime helps Cilberto remain calm. She used to get migraines and had anxiety about the appointments. ""Now it's like nothing. It's just like just sitting at a spa,"" she said.
Back in Brooklyn, Heinrichs also opens the door to a nurse every two weeks to dispense his drug. It's a bit like welcoming in an old friend who happened to be in the neighborhood.
""He's my age, and we get along very well and we hang out,"" Heinrichs said. When his young son wonders what's going on, the responses are simple: doctors are studying dada's brain, and dada's getting some medicine. He thinks of his older brother David, who died of Alzheimer's in 2019, and was only four years older than him.
""The difference in the outcome of those four years has been everything,"" he said. ""It's given me a family. It's given me hope.""
Correction: An earlier version of this story said that Ciliberto takes anti-nausea medication before her anti-amyloid infusions. She no longer needs such medication, as her body has adapted to taking the drug.

Stories you might be interested in"
25,2026-11-14,"Nvidia could lose 30 percent of its value following cofounder Jensen Huang’s decision to step down, analysts warns.
Save
Share
In the wake of Jensen Huang’s resignation as CEO of the artificial intelligence giant Nvidia, the company has brought in a new interim chief, Colette Kress, who has been with the company since 2013.
Along with Kress, the AI bellwether appointed its first-ever board chair, Harvey Jones, a venture investor with three decades of tech industry experience.
The changes mark a major overhaul of Nvidia’s corporate structure and leadership, which Huang has dominated since cofounding the AI chipmaker in 1993. A powerful figure in Silicon Valley, he stepped down on Thursday following reports of his affairs with a suspected Chinese intelligence operative and two employees that have drawn scrutiny from United States lawmakers, the company’s board and shareholders.
Despite the shakeup, the question remains how much of Nvidia’s nearly $4 trillion value was pegged to its longtime leader, who is synonymous with the company’s exponential growth. Dan Ives, an analyst at Yorkville Ives, predicted that Nvidia’s value could decline 25 to 30 percent without Huang at the helm.
Historically, executives across corporate America have been beholden to shareholders, who hold executives accountable. In Silicon Valley, however, companies have often been built around a founder’s vision – as was the case for Nvidia.
Analysts have long maintained that the success of companies like Nvidia hinges on the success and vision of their founder. A report from Bain and Company found that founder-led companies perform 2.1 times better than companies that are not led by their founders between 2015 and 2025, and when it comes to tech, that jumps to 2.6 times.
“When it comes to succession, the data shows that, as great as these founders are, it’s incredibly rare for their successors to do as well as the original founder. So I wouldn’t necessarily call it a risk. It’s simply the evolutionary life cycle of building a company,” Michael Monaghan, portfolio manager of Founders 100 ETF, told Al Jazeera.
Silicon Valley has long embraced this model, with companies such as Meta – Facebook and Instagram’s parent company – and Tesla built around the vision and personalities of their founders.
“It’s a common challenge among tech giants. You can ask: What does SpaceX look like without Elon Musk? What does Meta look like without Mark Zuckerberg? I think the same principle applies here, particularly in an emergency situation like this,” Xiaomeng Lu, director of geo-technology at the Eurasia Group, told Al Jazeera.
Musk came under scrutiny from the board at the electric vehicle maker as his attention was divided among a growing portfolio of businesses, which was a distraction from the company.
Musk’s increasingly public political activities with the administration of US President Donald Trump became a source of controversy for Tesla, with the company facing protests at its showrooms amid damage to its brand image and declining vehicle sales. In the first quarter of 2025, Tesla deliveries fell 13 percent year over year to their lowest level in nearly three years.
Ultimately, Tesla’s board opted not to remove him. However, his other company made changes to its governance structure that made it essentially impossible for the board to fire him when SpaceX prepared to go public earlier this year.
Musk was expected to retain approximately 82 percent of SpaceX’s total voting power after the IPO, despite selling shares to outside investors, a move that gave him control of who sits on the board, a role that is supposed to oversee the CEO. The Texas-based aerospace giant wrote into its charter that removal requires approval from holders of its special class of shares, and because Musk owns the majority of those shares, he can veto his own firing.
For Nvidia, Huang’s leadership often paid off. Nvidia invested heavily in graphics processing and helping build out data centre infrastructure amid a rapid expansion in the AI sector.
Nvidia has continued to make those kinds of investments. Just this week, the company agreed to purchase $10bn worth of shares in a private placement ahead of Anthropic’s blockbuster IPO next week.
 

 
Nvidia’s management structure was heavily centred around Huang. He had dozens of executives reporting to him directly rather than operating within a more traditional hierarchical chain of command.
Under Nvidia’s bylaws, Huang could be removed by the 11-person board of directors “with or without cause”, as they hold a majority of votes. Unlike other tech giants, Huang does not have outsized control. Huang owns about 3.6 percent of the company and does not have majority voting power.
Nvidia’s structure is unique among major Silicon Valley companies.
Meta and Alphabet, for example, have special stock arrangements that give their founders more voting power than their actual ownership stakes would normally provide. As a result, founders like Zuckerberg can maintain control over who sits on the board, which can make it more difficult for shareholders to challenge leadership decisions.
This means that while Huang had enormous influence over Nvidia’s direction, he did not have the same formal voting control wielded by other major tech founders.
The company appointed Kress as its interim CEO. She previously served as Nvidia’s chief financial officer.
Historically, when a founder leaves, it hurts the direction of the company and can sway its competitive advantage in the market, and it has happened several times in corporate history.
In the 1980s, after a power struggle between Apple founder Steve Jobs and then CEO John Sculley, the company struggled for a decade. By 1997, the tech giant was on the brink of bankruptcy, with nearly $1bn in losses that year.
“When Apple’s founder Steve Jobs left the company for the first time, Apple experienced a major downturn, eventually prompting the board to invite him back,” Lu said.
When Jobs returned to the company, the Cupertino, California-based tech giant’s luck began to change and led to the launch of its most well-known products, including the iMac and the iPhone.
Starbucks faced a similar setback after founder Howard Schultz stepped down as CEO in 2000. He handed leadership to Orin Smith, the company’s then-president and chief operating officer.
The Seattle-based coffee shop chain continued to grow under Smith, but struggled after he retired in 2005 and was replaced by Jim Donald. Amid slowing sales and increased competition, Starbucks lost more than 80 percent of its value.
In January 2008, Schultz returned as CEO and launched a turnaround effort that included closing hundreds of underperforming locations.
However, founder-led companies are not necessarily destined for a downturn when their founders leave.
Jobs, who left the company in 1985, returned in 1997 and led its subsequent turnaround before handing the reins to former CEO Tim Cook in 2011. Under Cook, Apple continued to grow, briefly surpassing a $5 trillion market capitalisation in July 2026.
At Microsoft, Bill Gates stepped down as CEO in 2000, with Steve Ballmer succeeding him. After Ballmer retired in 2014, Satya Nadella took over.
Nadella shifted the Redmond, Washington-based tech giant’s focus towards cloud computing and enterprise software – a move that helped its market capitalisation grow from approximately $302bn in 2014 to $3.88 trillion in November 2026.
Nvidia faces another issue, and that is that much of the AI sector’s growth is pegged to its powerful chips.
OpenAI, which is eyeing an IPO, has up to $100bn worth of planned financial agreements with Nvidia. Nvidia has also agreed to invest $10bn in Dario Amodei-led Anthropic through a private placement tied to its planned US IPO, which could take place as early as next week.

27,2026-11-14,"If you’re new to the motorcycle scene, you’d probably only know Benelli as a Chinese-owned bike maker that plays in the affordable end of the market, producing some really frugal twin-cylinder motorcycles – whether that's adventure tourers or sportsbikes. Well, let me give a bit of a history lesson.
Benelli is one of Italy's oldest motorcycle manufacturers, with roots dating back to 1911 in Pesaro. Over the decades, Benelli earned its stripes on racetracks, collected championship titles, and developed some rather outrageous machinery. We're talking about everything from the legendary six-cylinder 750 Sei to triple-cylinder marvels like the Tornado Tre 900 and TNT 1130 – machines that had absolutely no interest in being sensible commuter motorcycles.
The brand eventually became part of China's Qianjiang Group in 2005, but its Italian engineering heritage is far richer than its current budget-friendly reputation suggests. So when the Tornado Naked Tre 903 Concept broke out at EICMA 2026, it obviously caught my eye.
Not just because it's Benelli dipping back into its three-cylinder heritage, but also because of a decidedly futuristic twist. The concept packs an all-new 900cc inline-three, a modular frame and, most intriguingly, a set of active aerodynamic winglets that can move independently depending on what the motorcycle is doing. Yes, we're talking about a naked motorcycle with wings that actually move. Because apparently, fixed winglets are just so last season.
Powering the Tre 903 is an entirely new inline-three – not just a tweaked older engine, but an entirely new mill – displacing around 900cc. I say around because Benelli has yet to disclose the official figures.
That new triple is an aggressively over-square unit, engineered to rev all the way up to a rather heady 14,000 rpm. That's the sort of number that should make fans of high-revving nakeds like the Triumph Street Triple and Yamaha MT-09 sit up and take notice. And it’s a light engine as well, tipping the scales at a remarkably low 123 lb (56 kg).
The exhaust gets some special treatment as well, with Benelli incorporating a 3D-printed component into the system. I have to say, judging by the pictures, its integration into the rear bodywork makes for a rather clean-looking design, although it does raise questions about practicality.
That’s because the arrangement appears to leave little room for a pillion, so if you’re planning to take your partner along, you might have to look elsewhere. The rest of the hardware is pretty standard: Marzocchi forks, fully adjustable Öhlins suspension at the rear, an inverted-design aluminum swingarm and slick tires.
As for the electronics, the usual premium-bike goodies are all present and accounted for. You get ride-by-wire throttle, a slipper clutch, a two-way quickshifter and a six-axis IMU to keep tabs on the motorcycle's movements and manage its rider aids. All perfectly welcome additions, of course, but let's be honest – none of that is what makes this particular Benelli worth talking about.
The real engineering rabbit hole begins with the chassis. Benelli has come up with a modular, three-piece frame architecture comprising interchangeable front, central, and rear sections. Rather than designing an entirely new chassis for every motorcycle, Benelli’s engineers could theoretically swap individual modules to alter the frame's geometry, rigidity, and overall character depending on the intended use case.
The front and rear sections are made from aluminum, while the central module uses composite material. It's an interesting bit of engineering that could eventually allow Benelli to develop an entire family of motorcycles around a common platform, potentially reducing development costs while giving designers considerably more freedom to experiment with different configurations.
And then there’s the party trick: active winglets. Unlike the fixed aerodynamic appendages we've become accustomed to seeing on increasingly wing-heavy performance motorcycles, the Tre 903 Concept's winglets can change their angles independently.
Their movements are governed by information from the six-axis IMU, allowing them to respond to acceleration, braking, and cornering inputs. When the additional aerodynamic assistance isn't required, the winglets can simply retract.
That's a much more sophisticated proposition than simply bolting a pair of carbon-fiber wings onto the front of a motorcycle and calling it aerodynamic development. Sorry, small displacement sportsbikes.
What makes the active aero setup particularly interesting is that the winglets aren't mechanically linked to one another. Each can operate independently, with the system taking into account the motorcycle's speed, riding conditions and rider inputs before deciding how much aerodynamic assistance is needed and when.
In theory, this should help improve stability and control during hard acceleration, braking and cornering, potentially making the bike both quicker and safer. Whether that translates into a meaningful advantage on public roads is another question entirely, but it's certainly a fascinating bit of engineering.
Styling-wise, the Tornado Naked Tre 903 Concept boasts exposed mechanical elements, muscular proportions, and sharply sculpted surfaces. But that compact front end and unusual lighting arrangement mean it looks unlike anything else in Benelli's current lineup.
Those moving winglets sit outboard of the front bodywork like a pair of mechanical appendages, giving the motorcycle an almost insect-like appearance. The tail is equally unconventional, with that integrated exhaust and minimal rear section creating a particularly clean silhouette.
Benelli has carried the minimalist approach into the cockpit, too, with the instrument display integrated directly into the top triple clamp rather than sitting in a conventional dashboard housing. The designers say this arrangement is intended to simplify the overall layout and reduce visual clutter.
It's certainly not the sort of design that'll appeal to everyone, and some of its more outrageous details will probably be toned down if it ever reaches production. Still, in an industry where naked motorcycles increasingly seem to follow the same muscular-headlight-and-angular-tank design template, it's refreshing to see Benelli take a few risks.
As for whether the Tornado Naked Tre 903 will eventually make it into production looking anything like this, Benelli hasn't made any promises just yet.
Source: Benelli

28,2026-11-14,"The 1980s were stacked with classic sitcoms. ""Cheers,"" ""Taxi,"" ""The Golden Girls"" and so many more had us pinned to our couches during primetime. Of course, there was also formulaic garbage like ""Who's the Boss,"" ""Full House"" and ""Growing Pains."" This is always the state of play in television. But the quality-to-trash ratio was pretty favorable throughout the entire decade.
Nevertheless, there were many terrific '80s sitcoms that got axed before they had a chance to flourish. In most cases, they were flouting formula, which meant viewers had to adjust to different comedic rhythms. Audiences proved malleable with variety shows like ""The Smothers Brothers Comedy Hour,"" ""Rowan & Martin's Laugh-In,"" and ""Saturday Night Live,"" but the sitcom structure was sacrosanct: set-up and punchline all the way to a tidy finale in 30 minutes. Every minor crisis got resolved with a closing laugh (unless it was ""a very special episode""). Deviating too far from the norm was typically fatal.
The '80s sitcom graveyard is littered with coulda-been classics, most notably ""Police Squad!,"" which died a quick death after six episodes, but later spawned the wildly successful ""Naked Gun"" franchise. Alas, there are others that were so unsuccessful and/or controversial that they're only streamable on YouTube via old VHS rips (if they're streamable at all). Here are five '80s sitcoms that undeservedly got a quick hook.
Martin Mull was a brilliant comedic performer whose television career got off to a promising start in the 1970s. He was a scream as the detestable Garth Gimble on Norman Lear's ""Mary Hartman, Mary Hartman,"" which earned him a spinoff as his character's brother Barth Gimble on the influential talk show spoof ""Fernwood 2 Night."" The show was something of a lark, but got a second season as ""America 2 Night."" After that, it seemed to be only a matter of time before he got another network show.
It took six years after the end of ""America 2 Night,"" but that opportunity finally arrived in 1984 with ""Domestic Life."" The show had a seemingly can't-miss pedigree with Steve Martin as a co-creator, but CBS lost confidence in it quickly when it got off to a rocky ratings start as a mid-season replacement. Mull starred as Martin Crane, a Seattle TV news commentator who — along with his wife, rebellious 15-year-old daughter, and business-whiz 10-year-old son — encounters a variety of stock sitcom complications. 
""Domestic Life"" wasn't a parody per se, but, for 10 whole episodes, it was a hilarious vehicle for Mull's droll sensibility. Robert Ridgely was a delight as pompous anchorman Cliff Hamilton, and the series upended expectations at every turn. One of the best episodes found Mull scrambling to come up with $4,000 due for a balloon payment on his house, and being forced to ask his investor son for a loan. Mull plays the scene with an uproarious mix of flattery and outright shame. ""Domestic Life"" should've run for many seasons, but, despite critical acclaim, the ratings were never there.
When I watched the premiere of ""Frank's Place"" on CBS in 1987, I was simultaneously grateful that such an idiosyncratic sitcom could exist on network television and saddened that it was almost certainly destined for an early cancellation. The show reunited ""WKRP in Cincinnati"" creator Hugh Wilson with Tim Reid, who played DJ Venus Flytrap on the hit series, and was blessed with a nifty premise. Reid stars as Frank Parrish, a Boston college professor who's inherited a New Orleans restaurant. Frank wants to sell the restaurant, but is forced to stay on when one of the eatery's waitresses puts a voodoo curse on him. How will this erudite New Englander adjust to life in the Big Easy? On one level, this is basic fish-out-of-water stuff, but it goes places racially and culturally that no series dared to explore during that era.
Wilson adopted a single camera approach, and eschewed a laugh track. The latter threw a lot of viewers at the time, but, judging from solid Nielsen ratings over summer reruns, people were getting used to this approach. That's probably because the show was dripping with New Orleans atmosphere; it was every bit as lively and enchanting and delicious (oh, the food porn) as the city itself. Why was it canceled? According to Walter Kronkite, the legendary CBS newsman (who was a huge fan of the show), then-CBS CEO Laurence Tisch took offense to an episode that was critical of junk bonds (a major source of Tisch's wealth). 
Why isn't this beloved series available via streaming or physical media? Much like ""WKRP in Cincinnati,"" the music licensing is crazy expensive, and soundalike zydeco would rob the show of its soul. So the blurry VHS rips on YouTube are, sadly, the best we're going to get.
Three years before they brought the mean-spirited ""Married... with Children"" to the upstart Fox network, Ron Leavitt and Michael G. Moye caused a caustic stir with the blithely nasty ""It's Your Move."" The show starred Jason Bateman as a conscienceless teenager who scams everyone in his orbit, but takes particular pleasure in torturing his neighbor (David Garrison), a struggling writer who commits the cardinal sin of dating Bateman's mother. The two quickly engage in a tit-for-tat battle intended to discredit each other, but, until the series veered away from this format, their petty battles always ended in a stalemate.
Bateman landed this leading role on the strength of his smarmy work as Ricky Schroeder's friend on the NBC sitcom ""Silver Spoons."" He's matched every step of the way by Garrison, who toes the line between decency and sheer malevolence. There wasn't a nastier sitcom on network television at the time, and that, in part, proved to be its undoing. NBC found itself inundated with letters from concerned parents, who were alarmed to see their children mimicking Bateman's corrupt/criminal behavior. Pair this with the show trying to compete in the same timeslot as ABC's popular nighttime soap ""Dynasty,"" and NBC opted to cut bait. 
In the end, it worked out for everyone: Bateman smoothly segued from slappable kid to flustered adult (shining in ""Arrested Development""), while Garrison turned into a confidante for Ed O'Neil's Al Bundy on ""Married... with Children."" But ""It's Your Move"" was a sharp (perhaps unintentional) satire of the greed-driven Reagan era. The characters on this show are either naive dopes or venal predators.
For five weeks in the spring of 1982, ABC burned off episodes of a sitcom that must've melted the brains of people who stumbled across it in a crowded time slot. Created in part by Michael Jacobs, a sitcom machine who would reach high highs with ""Dinosaurs"" and deep lows with ""Boy Meets World,"" ""No Soap, Radio"" aimed to turn an inside-baseball comedy non-sequitur (on par with ""The Aristocrats"") into a successful sitcom. Had it debuted on HBO, it might've become a sustainable critical sensation. As a network series, it was dead on arrival.
The very loose premise of the show revolved around Steve Guttenberg as a manager of a failing Atlantic City hotel. These segments present the series as a slamming-door farce a la ""Fawlty Towers,"" but it detours into absurd bits that, like its title, have nothing to do with whatever threadbare narrative it's trying to advance. People have compared the show to ""Monty Python's Flying Circus,"" but it's clearly inspired by ""Kentucky Fried Movie."" The intermittent news breaks are straight out of the chaotic David Zucker-Jim Abrahams-Jerry Zucker classic. I'd love to talk to the creators of this show, because I simply don't understand how it was greenlit in 1982. ""No Soap, Radio"" is a shotgun spray of silliness. As a late-night show, devoid of the Guttenberg anchor and with a better recurring cast, it could've been a sensation. Instead, it's a fun little show that couldn't.
When Clint Eastwood's ""Dirty Harry"" film series sunk to a vile level of self-parody with the putrid ""Sudden Impact,"" the conditions were perfect for a straight-up, ZAZ-type spoof of the star's judge-jury-executioner shenanigans. Comedy wunderkind Alan Spencer, who broke into the industry at the age of 15 as a joke writer, delivered the goofy goods in 1986 with the splendidly silly ""Sledge Hammer!"" The series starred David Rasche (Karl Muller from ""Succession"") as a trigger-happy San Francisco cop who's carrying on a near-relationship with his .44 Magnum. He sleeps with the gun (which rests on a satin pillow""), showers with it, and talks to it. Unlike Dirty Harry, Sledge Hammer is generally an affable guy, who, despite his extrajudicial bent, is liked by his partner Detective Dori Doreau (Anne-Marie Martin). He drives his supervisor Captain Trunk (Harrison Page) nuts, but typically gets the job done — albeit messily at times.
""Sledge Hammer"" received mostly positive reviews and initially posted solid ratings. This changed when ABC began moving it around the schedule. When the show's numbers plummeted, ABC placed ""Sledge Hammer!"" squarely on the chopping block. Knowing they were doomed, Spencer and his writers ended the series finale with Hammer botching the disarming of a nuclear warhead, thus leading to the destruction of San Francisco. Amusingly, this episode did surprisingly well in the ratings, so ABC greenlit a second season (set five years in the past). The network promptly did ""Sledge Hammer!"" dirty by pitting it against ""The Cosby Show."" This was the end, but unlike the other shows on this list, you can currently stream ""Sledge Hammer!"" on Prime Video (it also received a physical media release). It's still a riot 40 years later."

But what do the people behind the stalls think? Are they benefiting from the hikes or are their own profits being squeezed?
Traders at Manchester's Christmas markets told the BBC they were feeling the pressure of increasing bills for staff, ingredients and energy, with some saying they were making less money despite charging more.
One trader said it costs at least Â£4,000 a day to run each her four stalls, while another has cut prices to keep customers coming back.
Nils Borstel, 25, helps run Bratwurst King, a family business which has operated at the markets since 1999.
He said a sausage that previously cost Â£3.50 now sold for Â£8, with much of the increase taking place over the past five or six years.
""But we don't earn more money,"" he said.

Manchester Christmas market traders explain prices
Borstel, whose family operates four stalls across the city centre, said rising costs for meat, electricity, staffing and accommodation had forced the business to increase prices.
About 15 workers travel from Germany to help run the stalls, alongside staff recruited in the UK.
He said that despite busy crowds and dry weather during the opening weekend, people appeared more cautious about spending than in previous years.
For example, some customers were now buying one portion of food to share, rather than purchasing one each, he said. 
Borstel said he had seen a change in spending habits since 2019, when customers were more willing to buy food and drinks without worrying about the cost.
He acknowledged that prices were high and did not want to increase them, but said the they were necessary to cover expenses.
Poppy (left) and Neve questioned whether the portions they bought justified the prices
