""I think it was pricey for what it was,"" Neve said.
Poppy also felt she had not received much food for the money, although said it was tasty.
Both said they enjoyed going into the city and looking around the markets, while Neve sympathised with traders, saying she thought the cost of renting space could force businesses to raise prices.
Charlotte Whittle, director of CT Catering, has been trading at the markets for 10 years and now operates four stalls.
She said each stall cost at least Â£1,000 a day to run, bringing her total daily operating costs to at least Â£4,000.
Whittle, who employs a range of staff, said customers often underestimated the work and expense involved.
Before the markets opened, her business spent two weeks preparing its stalls, with another two weeks needed to dismantle them after Christmas.
CT Catering director Charlotte Whittle says her business employs 57 people across four Manchester market sites
She said the costs included staff wages, accommodation, deliveries, storage, packaging and card payment fees.
Parking three vans in Manchester city centre could cost Â£100 a day alone, she said, with the vehicles needed to replenish stock throughout busy trading periods.
""They think that you're just selling cookies,"" she said.
Whittle, who began her business selling homemade cakes at street food markets, said the cost of stock and packaging had increased significantly over the years.
Although she had increased some prices, she said she had chosen to absorb part of those costs rather than risk putting customers off.
""We've had to take a massive cut on profits,"" she said.
She also argued that the standard of street food had improved and customers were receiving food comparable in quality to that served in restaurants.
But not every trader has responded to rising costs by putting up prices.
Clark Murphy, whose family has traded at the markets for 22 years, runs Mr Crimble Crumble, which sells fruit crumbles from two stalls.
He said the business had kept most prices unchanged for three years and had reduced the cost of its largest portion by Â£2.
Clark Murphy (left), pictured with his nephew Davis, said his business had reduced the price of its largest crumble
Murphy said cooking oil and biodegradable packaging had become more expensive, but the business wanted to retain customers who returned each Christmas.
""We haven't increased our prices for three years now,"" he said.

His decorations cost Â£4 each or three for Â£10, while metal signs were priced at Â£9.

31,2026-11-14,"
  By Anthony Franco |
  at November 13, 2026 7:36pm CDT  
The Royals are looking for a right-handed hitting outfielder, general manager J.J. Picollo told reporters at the GM Meetings this week (link via ESPN’s Jesse Rogers). The GM added that an acquisition would ideally be capable of playing center field on at least a part-time basis.
That’s essentially the role Lane Thomas played for K.C. this year. They picked him up on a one-year, $5.25MM free agent contract last offseason. Thomas played reasonably well for a half-season, batting .232/.326/.393 across 97 games. He started 72 contests in the outfield, 52 of them up the middle. They traded him to Atlanta at the deadline for a pair of minor league pitchers and to dump what remained of Bailey Falter’s arbitration salary.
Thomas didn’t play particularly well for the Braves. He’s back on the market and might be available even more cheaply than he was a year ago if the Royals wanted to reengage. Tyrone Taylor, Myles Straw, Jake Meyers and Victor Robles are other possibilities as fourth/fifth outfield types. If the Royals wanted a potential everyday center fielder, Luis Robert Jr. is the top right-handed hitter on the free agent market.
Dane Myers and Jonny DeLuca are fourth outfielders who could be available on the trade front. The Nationals are fielding interest in former second overall pick Dylan Crews, who’s a change of scenery candidate. Washington will probably be looking for an affordable pitcher in return, though, and that’s not an area of organizational strength for Kansas City. The Royals might balk at giving up Noah Cameron given Crews’ lack of big league production to this point. Players like Luinder Avila (who’ll likely miss next season after August Tommy John surgery) or Daniel Lynch IV don’t seem enticing enough for Washington to part with a player who has Crews’ draft and prospect pedigree.
Kansas City arguably should be looking for two outfield acquisitions. Right fielder Jac Caglianone is their only clear regular. Left fielder Isaac Collins underwhelmed in his first season after being acquired from Milwaukee. Kyle Isbel is a strong defensive center fielder but had a career-worst .224/.268/.323 showing at the plate. He’s in his final year of arbitration control and a potential non-tender candidate on a projected $3.5MM salary (courtesy of MLBTR contributor Matt Swartz). Even if the Royals keep Isbel around, they’d ideally push him into a true fourth outfield role. Tyler Tolbert and John Rave are fifth outfield types.
There’s room on the roster for K.C. to both add a right-handed hitting center fielder like Taylor or Meyers and acquire a true everyday player in left field. 35,2026-11-14,"Tesla was founded in 2003, but it wasn't until 2008 that it launched its first vehicle, the all-electric Roadster sports car, powered by a battery pack containing commercially available lithium-ion cells. Fourteen years later, the automaker started delivering vehicles with its own 4680 cylindrical battery cells – named after its 46mm diameter and 80mm height — and while this marked an important step toward reducing its reliance on external suppliers, Tesla continues to use battery packs produced by a range of companies in various countries.
Tesla manufactures its 4680 battery cell at its Gigafactory Texas in Austin, using it in a number of Tesla vehicles that includes the Model Y SUV and its ambitious but struggling Cybertruck pickup. In September 2024, Tesla announced it had produced its 100 millionth 4680 cell across all of its factories. The automaker is also expanding production of its own lithium iron phosphate (LFP) cells at its Gigafactory Nevada, though these are used mainly for energy storage products. Its Kato Factory in Fremont, California, meanwhile, supports battery development and early-stage cell manufacturing.
Tesla's external suppliers include long-standing partner Panasonic, which produces 2170 cells in the U.S. and 18650 cells in Japan for Model S and Model X vehicles. China's CATL (Contemporary Amperex Technology Co. Limited) is another major supplier, especially of LFP cells for certain Model 3 and Model Y cars. South Korea's LG Energy Solution, meanwhile, supplies 2170 cells, manufactured at its facility in Nanjing, China, for certain Model 3 and Model Y versions, and also has an agreement with Tesla to manufacture LFP cells in Michigan for its Megapack energy storage systems from 2027. Even China's BYD, Tesla's biggest rival in the global electric-vehicle (EV) market, has supplied LFP battery cells for Model Y production at Tesla's German factory, although its current supply arrangements with Tesla are unclear.
Tesla is best known for its EVs, including the Model 3, Model Y and discontinued Model S and Model X, as well as its outspoken CEO, Elon Musk. You might be less familiar, however, with the 4680 battery cell, which, as we've learned, the automaker developed and manufactures itself. Indeed, this relatively little-known part of the business could play a major role in shaping Tesla's future, helping it to reduce production costs and giving the company more control over its battery supply.
Tesla's 4680 battery cell was developed to improve battery performance while also making vehicle assembly more efficient. The cell is much larger in size compared to Tesla's older 2170 cell, meaning that fewer individual cells are needed to make a battery pack, thereby simplifying pack assembly and reducing the number of required components. Another innovation linked to Tesla's 4680 cells is the structural battery pack, which incorporates the battery into the vehicle's structure rather than having it as a standalone component. The idea is that doing it this way reduces the number of parts and contributes to the vehicle's overall rigidity.
Tesla has also developed a dry-electrode manufacturing process for its 4680 cells, which could make battery production cheaper and less energy-intensive by removing a drying stage that's common in battery production. However, scaling up 4680 cell production has proved challenging, especially when trying to achieve consistent quality while keeping manufacturing costs competitive. Despite the difficulties, designing its batteries in-house should help Tesla lower production costs and give it greater control over its battery supply, as well as allowing it to better tailor battery packs to upcoming vehicle designs. As for buyers, further battery development by the automaker will hopefully lead to more affordable EVs."
36,2026-11-14,"We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.
Why spend upwards of $1,000 on a big-screen TV or home theater projector when a bite-sized lens costs significantly less and delivers just as large (if not larger) an image? We're glad you asked, because there are tons of budget-friendly portable projectors on the market right now. Unfortunately, some folks are buying these products with the expectation that they'll bring the same kind of picture quality and features to the table as hardware that's more than double the price. That's simply not the case, and sometimes, it really is as easy as the numbers.
In the case of projection technology, the main digits to fret over are the device's ANSI lumens rating. This is the official metric for measuring projector brightness, and most compact models struggle to output more than 300 ANSI lumens. For small bedrooms and viewing spaces with easy-to-control ambient lighting, that's probably just enough illumination; for a dedicated home theater with a sizable projector screen, 300 lumens probably won't cut it. Ideally, screens that are 100 inches or larger should be paired with a projector that reliably outputs at least 1,500 ANSI lumens.
A bigger screen needs a lot of lighting, and if you're building off of a strong source, you run the risk of visuals looking dim and color-starved: the antithesis of good home theater picture quality. And while ambient lighting from a lamp or ceiling fixture might be manageable, if your theater space has windows that drench the room in sunlight for most of the day, you should definitely bundle some blackout curtains with your projector purchase — even if you decide on a premium model.
Home theater-level picture quality shouldn't just look good because the room is dark and your projector is bright; factors like High Dynamic Range (HDR) are equally important. Cheap projectors tend to get the short end of the stick when it comes to HDR support; you'll be lucky if you can find one that supports HDR10 formats and isn't completely lackluster in most ways. Not to single out a brand, but this Freeski mini projector might be the perfect example:
At $50 (on sale for $40 at the time of writing), it's super-cheap, and the manufacturer claims it has ""HDR technology."" But there's no indication of which HDR format(s) the projector supports, and it only outputs up to 120 ANSI lumens — best steer clear of this one, friends! Cheap projectors tend to have fewer image correction features, too; if you need a unit with auto-keystone correction, zoom tools, and input settings, you may need to increase your budget.
We might be making it sound like you can't get good picture quality in a home theater from a portable projector. That's not true, but it is exceptionally difficult. We 100% recommend going with a portable projector if it's from a top brand and has all the right picture features; this should also be reflected in the final price. The $250 XGIMI Vibe One and the $300 MoGo 2 Pro are the kinds of dollar amounts you should familiarize yourself with.
The lion's share of portable projectors are made by companies the world has never heard of, with brand and product names that are often impossible to pronounce. There are hundreds of these projectors vying for your attention, and ""home theater"" is a phrase these manufacturers love throwing around. You might also see ""4K supported,"" which isn't the same thing as native 4K. It's really just a clever way of saying the projector will accept a 4K signal, but that the movie or show will need to be downscaled to 1080p (or the highest native resolution the projector supports).
At the very least, you'll always want to have an external speaker or soundbar connected to your projector, even if you own a high-end model. Manufacturers will sometimes include built-in speakers on cheap projectors, while talking up the fact that the product delivers ""Dolby Digital."" But the speakers may only total 10 watts altogether, and all ""Dolby Digital"" really means is ""up to a 5.1 signal but probably stereo."" You may also come across portable projectors that advertise apps like Netflix, HBO Max, Disney+, etc. If the projector does indeed have streaming apps, it'll need to be connected to Wi-Fi to access these platforms.
You'll also want to verify what network bands the projector supports. Most smart home devices are limited to 2.4GHz connectivity, and the same goes for compact projectors. If you're doing a movie night outdoors, it might actually make more sense to run a long Ethernet cable between the projector and your router if the projector has a LAN port."
37,2026-11-14,"Sharing Shina songs online, musicians in Kashmir, Ladakh and Gilgit-Baltistan are keeping their language alive and renewing ties across the divide.
Save
Share
This feature is part of a series examining how artificial borders reshape daily life, divide families and force loved ones to navigate the human cost of political geography.
In the remote Tulail Valley close to the Line of Control in Indian-administered Kashmir, 25-year-old singer Aasim Yaseen was walking through his village, Buglinder, when a college student from a neighbouring hamlet stopped him.
The young man, Tanveer, took out his phone and played Sangh Mukh Shamah, a song Yaseen had recently released in Shina, their shared language.
The encounter motivated Yaseen.
“People here don’t consider music work,” he says.
“Sometimes I think about going into another profession, but then someone plays my song on their phone, and I keep going.”
Across Gurez in Indian-administered Jammu and Kashmir, Drass in Indian-administered Ladakh, and Pakistan-administered Gilgit-Baltistan, young musicians like Yaseen are sharing Shina songs on social media. They are helping keep their language alive and renewing ties within a community that has been divided for generations.
The songs cross the Line of Control, which divides Indian- and Pakistani-administered parts of Kashmir. But the musicians face financial pressures, unreliable internet connections and political restrictions on their movement.

For Yaseen, that separation is part of his family history.
His grandfather has told him stories about Yaseen’s great-great uncle, who lived in Gilgit-Baltistan before Partition and stayed there as the region fell under Pakistani control. Yaseen says movement became extremely difficult after the Line of Control was established, and security concerns eventually led the relatives to lose contact.
“We were one family,” Yaseen says. “But we were divided into two countries.”
Gurez, the homeland of Indigenous Shina-speaking people, lies on historic routes linking the Kashmir Valley with Central Asia, including the Burzil Pass route to Astore in Pakistan-administered Gilgit-Baltistan.
Gurez has no local crossing where families can visit relatives in Gilgit. Buses once connected Srinagar with Muzaffarabad and Poonch with Rawalakot, but neither route passed through Gurez.
Residents in Gurez and Drass say security restrictions make them wary of calling or messaging relatives across the Line of Control. They worry that making contact could attract suspicion. Sharing Shina songs online feels safer: People on the other side can listen without either person needing to make direct contact.
Artists began sharing songs on Facebook and YouTube in the late 2010s. By 2020, residents were using online Shina music to reconnect with a community scattered across the mountains.
A 35-year-old teacher from Purana Tulail village, who requested anonymity due to security concerns, says songs have become an emotionally powerful way to communicate.
“Songs became a medium,” he says. “Through music, we could convey a message without having to send it. When a singer from this side sings, our community across the border listens to it.”

In Buglinder, Yaseen’s family life is all about farming.
Outside the family’s wooden home, a zomo — a hybrid of yak and cattle — stands under a tree while chickens roam the yard. Inside, his mother prepares namkeen chai, Kashmir’s pink salted tea, on a clay stove. The family drinks it with local roti and desi ghee.
Yaseen crosses a bridge with the animal towards a neighbouring village on the hillside. Below him, the Kishanganga River flows through the valley before flowing into Pakistan-administered Kashmir. Above him, bunkers sit atop hills and fences run across the mountains. Cameras surveil the ridgelines, and security checkpoints line the roads. In winter, snow can close the roads for months, making it difficult to reach schools, markets and medical care.
After the April 2025 attack near Pahalgam, which killed 26 people, most of them tourists, India and Pakistan exchanged strikes. In Gurez, residents say they sheltered in bunkers or were evacuated to temporary camps. Many had to leave their homes and livestock behind.
Yaseen’s 71-year-old father, Abdul Rahim Sheikh, works the family farm and runs a small shop selling groceries and daily essentials. He herds cattle in a nearby mountain range lined with barbed wire, while Yaseen helps fence a potato field.
Later, Yaseen’s sister calls him back to the house. Guests from Mumbai have arrived. The family welcomes them with kehwa, a spiced Kashmiri tea, and introduces them to Yaseen in the kitchen, where he sings.
His audience is just a handful of guests, but his recordings help extend his reach much further.

“Politically we are divided, but musically our hearts beat together,” says 38-year-old Salman Paras, a prominent contemporary Shina folk singer.
Paras was born in Sheikhpora Kilshay, a village in the Minimarg-Gurez area of Astore district, Pakistan-administered Gilgit-Baltistan. He grew up hearing stories of shared ethnic ties and separation from Gurez, where some families from Kilshay remained on the Indian-administered side after the region was divided.
He says his grandfather’s brother stayed behind in Gurez while the rest of his family migrated to Gilgit. Eventually, they lost touch.
Decades later, a Shina musician on the Kashmir side of the Line of Control came across Paras’s songs on YouTube and contacted him to say that relatives of his had been traced to Kangan, a town in central Kashmir where many Dard families migrated from Tulail.
Paras has not disclosed the musician’s identity for security reasons, and the families have yet to reconnect. But someone had recognised his family ties through his music.
His fusion of Shina folk and modern pop has brought the language to listeners beyond Gilgit-Baltistan. Paras’s song Karay Karay, recorded with Pakistani singer Ali Zafar, was accompanied by a video filmed during the cherry blossom season in Skardu and in the Katpana Desert, a high-altitude desert near the city of Skardu in Gilgit-Baltistan. It brought Shina to audiences beyond the mountainous region and has been viewed millions of times on YouTube. He’s also had recent performances in Dubai and Muscat.
“For a long time, many people didn’t take me seriously as a singer,” Paras says. “But when Ali Zafar introduced me, I sang proudly as Shina from Gilgit-Baltistan. People took notice.”
On November 3, Paras went live on Instagram, singing in Shina and several of its regional varieties. The session drew listeners from Gurez, Drass, Kargil and Gilgit-Baltistan.
During that live interaction, Imtiyaz Samoon, a Shina speaker from Gurez on the Indian-administered side, told Paras how deeply his music is loved in the valley. He said local singers often perform Paras’s songs at community events, and that his music has become part of gatherings throughout Gurez.
Paras responded, saying he wanted to collaborate with musicians on the Indian-administered side and, if the necessary permissions and visa could be arranged, perform in Gurez and Ladakh.
“If you look at the official map of Gilgit-Baltistan today, Gurez and Ladakh are missing,” he says, referring to Shina-speaking areas on the Indian-administered side. “But in my mind, I’ve drawn a cultural map that includes Chitral, Kohistan, Gurez and Ladakh. They are my people.”
Iftikhar Uddin Iftikhar, 29, a singer based in Gilgit, has watched a similar audience grow around his music.
He began learning music in 2015 under Shina folk singer Muhammad Amin Shah and released his first Shina album in 2018. He now collaborates with local performers, composes music and records in studios in Gilgit.
“Now I have friends across the border: Shina speakers in Kashmir and Ladakh,” he says. “They listen to my music. I never thought my songs would reach them.”
Iftikhar considers Paras an inspiration. He wants to build his career and forge a path for younger performers.
“Shina music is our identity,” he says. “I am not just promoting songs – I am keeping our language alive.”

Yaseen’s songs are finding listeners, but he still struggles to afford studio time.
He normally lives in Srinagar, more than 160km (100 miles) from home, where he studies music at Cluster University and performs at local events. When Tanveer approached him in Buglinder, he was visiting his family to help with the annual harvest.
His interest in singing began at school. Standing on a hill above a neighbouring village, he points towards his childhood school and remembers Rasheed Samoon, a teacher who gave him 10 rupees to sing his first poem, Aavo Mil Kar Kam Karain (Let’s work together).
Now, when he can afford it, Yaseen rents a studio in Srinagar. Producing a song, including studio time and production costs, runs him between 30,000 and 35,000 Indian rupees ($318–$372), he says.
Musicians share microphones, speakers, instruments and editing skills to reduce costs. For his song Malalak, released in April, for example, Yaseen shared the cost of video production and editing software with fellow musician Zia Ul Alam.
Younis Majeed, 27, from Izmarg village, is part of the same emerging generation of musicians in Gurez. He blends Shina folk melodies with contemporary beats. His song Tamah Hoon (I Have a Desire) has reached more than 21,000 viewers online.
“The internet lets us get to know each other again,” he says. “It reconnects us emotionally and culturally, both across and within borders.”
But the infrastructure supporting that connection is fragile. Some villages close to the Line of Control have little or no mobile coverage. Artists may have a smartphone but no quiet place to record, or they may be able to upload a song but not earn from it.
Further east, in Drass in Indian-administered Ladakh, 24-year-old singer Zain Drassi faces similar problems.
In early September, he performed with a local band at the Drass and Suru festivals. For him, the performances were a chance to bring a shared musical tradition back into public view.
“It was a small recovery of our cultural space after it had been limited for so long by war and political borders,” he says.
Drass was a key theatre of the 1999 Kargil War, and residents have lived with displacement and restrictions on movement. Today, Drassi says, local musicians also struggle to find the resources needed to make professional recordings.
“The internet has brought us closer to our people in Gurez and Gilgit, but practically we have different challenges,” he says.
Musicians can record vocals locally, he explains, but must travel elsewhere for composition, arrangements and professional production, seeking services in Srinagar, Bhaderwah or neighbouring Himachal Pradesh. Traditional instruments and musicians skilled in playing them are also difficult to find.
The younger performers are finding new ways to record and distribute Shina songs. Much of the repertoire they draw on, however, survived through singers and writers who had no digital audience.

In Kangan, in central Kashmir, 53-year-old singer Yousuf Naseem has spent three decades preserving Shina through music.
Sitting on his veranda, he plays a six-stringed rabab. His songs carry memories of migration, loss and the home he left behind.
Born in Dangithal, near the Line of Control in Gurez Valley, Naseem remembers long winters, family storytelling and songs passed along by older relatives.
“The elders in our house were our library,” he says. “Whatever they hummed, we sang.”
During the 1999 Kargil conflict, his family was among those forced to leave Gurez. They resettled in Kangan.
“We lost everything,” he says. “But the songs travelled with us.”
Naseem began taking Shina music to new listeners in Srinagar. In Bohri Kadal, a historic market in the old city where many Dard families work as wazas, or chefs, he gave a shopkeeper a copy of his first cassette, Dardistan, and asked him to play it every morning.
His voice began travelling through the market.
“People would ask what language it was,” Naseem says. “It was Shina. For many, it was the first time they had heard it.”
While Naseem carried Shina music beyond Gurez, Fareed Ahmed Kaloo worked to preserve it within the valley.
At the Shina Cultural Centre near Dawar, young musicians gather as Kaloo, 67, adjusts his harmonium and begins to sing. He wears a traditional shoka and white pakol cap.
 
“Gilgit, Kargil, Gurez, Drass – our pride, our own,
This is our Golden Garden: Dardistan, our home.”
Kaloo leads the Habba Khatoon Dramatic Club, a cultural group founded in 1976 to preserve Shina folklore in Gurez.
“After Partition, our culture faded,” he says. “Kashmiri music dominated, and our Dardic roots went silent.”
For years, he travelled between villages on foot, carrying a harmonium and a tumbak naari, a clay percussion instrument. He performed at weddings, fairs and local gatherings, accepting food, a place to sleep or a small honorarium as payment.
“We didn’t do it for money,” he says. “We did it because someone had to, and no one else would.”
Some of the club’s earliest members have died. Others left amid cross-border shelling and never returned. Kaloo stayed, teaching folk songs to younger generations.
He now listens to new tracks on his phone and admires how musicians in Gilgit have developed their sound while retaining elements of traditional Shina music.
“They’ve gone far ahead of us,” he says with a smile. “As they should. Our songs must evolve, but never lose their roots.”

The connection between these generations also runs through the work of Masood Samoon, a 78-year-old Shina writer and linguist from Gurez.
For more than two decades, Samoon has written poetry that musicians have turned into songs. He wrote Yaseen’s songs Malalak and Sangh Mukh Shamah.
“Yousuf Naseem sang my songs,” Samoon says. “He took my words to Kashmir, to people who had never heard Shina before.”
“Now Aasim Yaseen sings them,” he adds. “My words travel farther now than I could.”
In Gurez and Drass, Shina has relied heavily on oral transmission, with limited support from formal education. But new primary-school textbooks introduced by the Jammu and Kashmir school board this year are intended to give the language a stronger place in classrooms.
A Srinagar-based linguist who specialises in Shina welcomes the books but says their impact will depend on how the language is taught and whether children continue to use it in everyday life. He requested anonymity for fear of repercussions.
“Written resources are valuable, particularly for a language spoken by a relatively small population, but efforts to preserve Shina must also support its continued use as a spoken language,” he says.
Samoon’s poems are part of that spoken life. They are performed by older singers, recorded by younger artists and heard by listeners who may speak different varieties of Shina.
He says people from one area may not understand every word sung in another dialect. Yet the songs continue to find audiences.
“Music is a boundless thing,” Samoon says. “Shina is the language of my heart. When musicians sing my words, I travel across mountains and borders – even when I cannot physically travel there.”

As evening settles over Srinagar, Yaseen sits with fellow musicians at Al-Bahr Studio, working on another Shina track. The lyrics draw on folk tradition. The beat is modern.
The language is the same one heard in Naseem’s cassettes, Kaloo’s village performances and Samoon’s poetry. But the route it takes has changed.
Yaseen hopes one day to share a stage with musicians in Gilgit. For now, he records in Srinagar, performs when he can and returns to Tulail with new songs.
“The border will remain,” he says. “But our songs will still travel.”

38,2026-11-14,"EchoMotion turns a stock smartwatch into a full-body tracker, reading ultrasonic echoes off your arms, legs and torso to place 12 joints from your head to your ankles. No extra hardware rides along today.
Cornellâs SciFi Lab built the system around a commercial watchâs speaker and microphone. Cheng Zhang directs the lab. Its tests ran on a Galaxy Watch 6.
Samsung Galaxy Watch 7 40mm BluetoothEchoMotion ran on this watch with no retrainingPrice: $219.00 $249.99 (save $30.99, 12% off)Where to buy: Buy on Amazon
The watch speaker sends out ultrasonic sound, and its microphone listens for the echoes that bounce back from the wearerâs body as it moves through space. A lightweight model turns those echoes into joint positions. Development started on a 3D-printed watch replica carrying a speaker and microphone pair on each side, with two speakers wrapping the wearer in a sphere of inaudible sound that nobody could hear. Retail watches carry a single speaker and microphone pair, though the model still reads the full body from that one contact point on the wrist. Its microphones picked up the echoes returning off the body, and the model read those returning patterns to place each joint in three-dimensional space around the wearer.
Your watch today tracks only itself. Its accelerometer and gyroscope know where the device sits and how it moves, and nothing in that hardware knows where your knees or elbows have gone. Whole-body tracking has traditionally meant adding a smartphone, earbuds or a VR headset to the watch, and camera systems solve the same problem by filming the wearer and everyone nearby.
Training started with 10 volunteers wearing the prototype on the nondominant hand through 18 exercises in the lab. The model learned echoes, not video.
The team then moved the same model onto an unmodified Galaxy Watch 6 and handed it to 15 people the system had never encountered during its training runs. For each of those new wearers, the average error per joint came in under four inches, measured against the true position of that joint in the room. A one-time calibration of 12 minutes, spent moving while the watch learned the wearerâs body, cut that error by more than a third to about 2.4 inches. A deep learning model translated each echo pattern into movement, and the scoring compared its predicted joint positions against the camera-captured poses from training.
The same model also ran on a Samsung Galaxy Watch 7 and a Google Pixel Watch 3 with no retraining, so the software travels across current watches without starting over. Three watches ran one model. Sensing on the prototype drew about 12 milliwatts, roughly 40 times less power than a wearable camera, and a 30-minute session on the Galaxy Watch 6 used 5% to 6% of the battery. âThis is a very important jump for body tracking,â said lead author Saif Mahmud. The National Science Foundation backed the research.
The team also ran 10 new participants through the same 18 exercises in a small room, a narrow hallway and a spot beside a noisy road, outside the labâs controlled setup. Furniture and ambient noise left the readings alone, and the acoustic data stayed small, cheap to process and free of any video of the wearer or bystanders. Echoes bouncing off other people in that narrow hallway nudged performance down slightly, the one condition in the testing where the surroundings pushed back against the sonar readings. Traffic beside the road session gave the microphones a competing wall of sound to sort through, and the readings held up through all 18 exercises anyway.
EchoMotion remains a research system, with no app, no download and no release date attached to it, so the watch on your wrist gains none of this today. Nothing here ships yet. The team presented the paper in Shanghai on October 13, and it runs in an ACM journal covering interactive, mobile, wearable and ubiquitous technologies.
The groupâs current work points the system at fitness, building a personal fitness agent that counts reps and corrects form during a workout, down to a Bulgarian split squat. Form correction drives the roadmap. Mahmud also expects the model to improve as data arrives from thousands of users moving through crowded places like the subway, where echoes get harder to read and the body sits among other moving bodies.
The group is also exploring a pairing with smartglasses, where first-person vision from the glasses tracks the objects the wearerâs hands pick up while the watch tracks the body itself. Mahmudâs longer vision is a personal agent describing a whole day, what its wearer did and what that person ate, with EchoMotion supplying the body tracking underneath it, joint by joint and hour by hour. That agent remains a vision.
âWe have made significant efforts in making EchoMotion work on commercial devices, so that the adoption can be very easy,â Mahmud said. Any current watch with a speaker and a microphone already clears the hardware bar, and the Galaxy Watch 7 in the box above ran the same model in testing at a current price of $219.00. Buy that watch for its own features today, and treat EchoMotion as a reason to watch the next software cycle rather than a feature waiting in an app store for your current watch. Mahmud put the longer bet in his own words: âThis project enables the body tracking part of that vision.â The software decides the timeline.

39,2026-11-14,"Blog / IPv4 economics
12 November 2026 Â· Guide Â· By Mohammad AlSaraf
Our cheapest plan costs $1.00 a month. That's less than the rent on a single IPv4 address at many providers. So how does it work? The short answer: you don't get an IPv4 address of your own. You share one, and here are the numbers behind that.
There are only about 4.3 billion IPv4 addresses, and the free pools ran out years ago. Today they are bought, sold and rented like property:
These figures come mostly from brokers and vary by source and block size, so treat them as a range, not an exact price. The point stands either way: if every $1 server came with its own IPv4 address at retail prices, the address alone would cost several times the whole plan.
Each of our hosts has one public IPv4 address. Every server on that host gets its own block of ports on it: 5 on the free trial, 10 on Nano, 20 on Box and Box CPU, 30 on Box Plus. The first port goes to SSH, the rest go straight to the same port numbers inside your server. On 9 November 2026 our three addresses carried 56, 31 and 14 customer servers.
So the cost of one address is split dozens of ways. Even at Contabo's retail â¬3.50, that's well under 10 cents a month per server on our busiest host. That is most of the trick behind a $1 plan.
Ports 80 and 443 can't be split into blocks, because every website needs them. So we share those by domain name: you link your domain in the client area, and our proxy sends each visitor to the right server without decrypting anything (we load-tested it).
IPv6 has so many addresses that nobody needs to share. Every natbox server, including the free trial, gets its own routed /80: 248 addresses, every port open, port 22 and 443 included, no proxy in between (how it works). As more of the internet speaks IPv6, the shared IPv4 address matters less and less.
If you need a full IPv4 address with every port, a NAT VPS isn't the right tool, and that's fine. If you want a real Linux server for a bot, a small website, a VPN, a monitoring tool or for learning, sharing an IPv4 address is what lets it cost less than a coffee. See the plans.
Get new tutorials in Telegram. Follow @natboxio: every new post, plus short server tips.
More from the blog: Keeping natbox clean: our first abuse and fraud report, Why some servers booted without SSH, and how we fixed it, We made disk writes on natbox over 100 times faster. Here's the trade-off. RSS feed.
Small, honest Linux servers on modern Incus containers. From $1 a month."

41,2026-11-14,"Kay Roscoe was found unresponsive at her home in October
A woman has described how she could not speak or stand before being rushed to hospital after taking an unlicensed weight-loss jab.
Kay Roscoe, 38, from Birkenhead, said she had used a substance sold as retatrutide, or ""reta"", for two months before falling seriously ill.
The experimental drug is not approved for general use and is available only through clinical trials. Products claiming to contain it are widely sold online, but it is not known whether Kay took genuine retatrutide.
She is now warning others about the risks. ""I am the luckiest person to be alive. Honestly I can't say it enough, get off the pens,"" she said.
On the day of her collapse, Kay had been due to go out for a roast dinner with a friend.
When she failed to answer her phone, her friend went to check on her at home and found her unable to speak or stand.
""I was frothing at the mouth, not responsive at all,"" she said, recounting what her friend later told her. ""My eyes were moving around, but I couldn't speak or move or do anything.""
Kay, who has no memory of the episode, said her friends rang an ambulance and told paramedics she had been taking ""reta"" before she was rushed to hospital.
She said it took more than an hour for her to regain consciousness.
Kay took this selfie the night before she became unwell and said she felt ""absolutely fine""
Hospital tests confirmed Kay had suffered severe hypoglycaemia, a condition where blood sugar falls to dangerously low levels.
Medical records attributed the episode to an unlicensed weight-loss injection.
Kay said doctors warned her she could have died if her friend had found her just half an hour later.
In the weeks since, Kay said she has continued to suffer health problems, including extreme fatigue.
She said further tests had shown she had signs of liver damage and high cholesterol, neither of which she had before the episode. .
""I don't drink very much, I don't smoke,"" she said. ""I've never had a problem with anything like this. I just feel run down and drained. My body feels all over the place.""
Kay said she lost a 'drastic' amount of weight while taking the jab, but was also hardly eating
Kay had started injecting herself with the substance about two months earlier.
A former regular gym-goer, she said she was attracted to the prospect of quicker weight loss after seeing posts on social media.
""Going to the gym takes longer. I saw my friends on it and saw them losing weight. And I thought, everyone is taking it and they've all been fine, so I'll try it. I didn't really read into the licensing,"" she said.
Kay bought the jab from a friend and said she believed it was legitimate after scanning a QR code on the pen, which took her to what appeared to be a pharmaceutical website.
Initially, she was pleased with the results, saying she lost a ""drastic amount"" of weight within weeks. But the injections had also significantly reduced her appetite.
""I was made up with the change, but I was never hungry,"" she said.
In the days leading up to her hospital admission, Kay said she had eaten very little.
Retatrutide is being developed by pharmaceutical company Eli Lilly as a potential treatment for obesity.
Unlike existing weight-loss drugs, such as Wegovy and Mounjaro, which target one or two hormone receptors, retatrutide acts on three, earning it the nickname ""triple G"".
The drug is still undergoing clinical trials, with Eli Lilly planning to apply for approval in the US early next year.
A previous BBC investigation found vials purporting to be ""reta"" were being sold in gyms, beauty salons, hairdressers, on social media and even by flower sellers.
Eli Lilly said retatrutide had not been approved by any regulator and could not legally be sold for human use.
A spokesperson said: ""Counterfeit and black market medicines are unverified, unregulated and potentially dangerous - in some cases, deadly.""
The company said it was working with the UK's medicines regulator, the MHRA, police and social media platforms to remove illegal products.
""Stronger, coordinated law enforcement must be sustained to protect UK patients from unsafe fake medicines,"" the spokesperson added.
Dr Nas Khattak is a research doctor who has worked on clinical trials of weight-loss drugs
Dr Nas Khattak, a research doctor based in Chester who has worked on clinical trials of weight-loss drugs, warned of the dangers of injections obtained through unregulated sources.
""You cannot verify the dose, purity, sterility, or even the contents,"" he said.
""If something goes wrong there is no proper medical oversight.""
Dr Khattak said he had seen substances advertised through messaging apps, bodybuilding forums and private sellers, with users openly sharing dosing advice and supplier recommendations.
He said he had also encountered serious complications during his NHS hospital work, including a young woman who required intensive care after taking a weight-loss injection obtained from a beautician. He said it was never established exactly what substance she had taken.
""It really does feel like a roll of the dice,"" he said.
The UK's medicines regulator, the MHRA, has seized products claiming to contain retatrutide and says it is working to disrupt their illegal importation and distribution.
Dr Khattak said common side effects of weight-loss injections included mild nausea, feeling full more quickly, constipation and tiredness.
He said headaches, dizziness, a dry mouth and very dark urine could be signs of dehydration and should prompt medical advice.
However, he warned people to seek urgent medical attention if they experienced more serious symptoms, including:
Severe abdominal pain
Persistent vomiting or diarrhoea
Pain beneath the right ribs
Yellowing of the eyes
Significant changes in mood
Kay said she now plans to return to the gym and eat healthily
Since sharing her story on Facebook, Kay said she had received messages from about 100 people describing their own experiences with weight-loss injections.
""You never think anything's going to happen to you. But we all do things because we want to lose weight,"" she said. ""You see a new thing on the market and want to try it. I just think it's very dangerous.""
Kay also revealed the impact her ordeal has had on her family.
""They've gone mad at me and warned me not to take it. They said, 'There's nothing of you', but you don't see yourself like that, do you?""
She now plans to return to the gym and eat healthily.
""Please, everyone, stop taking a needle to be skinny. Imagine not waking up, like I nearly didn't. And then my poor family losing someone over taking something you didn't even know what it was.""

42,2026-11-14,"You’re using the new Bored Panda. Send feedback
We all know how precious cars can be for some people. From having posters stuck on the wall as teens to saving up and buying their dream car, it’s no less than a love story. And when people finally own the vehicle, they like to make some changes so that it stands out as something that belongs to them.
Whether it’s hanging lucky charms inside or completely changing the look from the outside, some folks love to give it their own identity. Of course, sometimes things can get out of hand, and they might end up with something they regret. However, a few owners don’t even realize they’ve ruined the vehicle, as we can see in this list.   
Believe it or not, car customization has a massive market. According to a study by the Specialty Equipment Market Association (SEMA), an estimated 7.9 million enthusiasts aged 16-24 accessorize their vehicles, spending roughly $7.2 billion on upgrades and accessories and representing a sizable share of the $43 billion industry marketplace.
These accessorizers say they get several key personal benefits from their purchases, including a better-looking vehicle (39%), a sense of pride (37%), enhanced vehicle performance (34%), and greater automotive knowledge (33%).
The study also claims that these enthusiasts are creating their own car culture. “It’s about taking pictures of your car and putting them on Instagram,” mentioned Kyle Cheng, the report’s lead SEMA research manager.
“They see their cars less as projects and more as a means to connect with friends. If they do take on projects, they involve their peers, and most feel that they can handle only light to moderate upgrades,” Cheng added. 
Meanwhile, Gavin Knapp, SEMA director of market research, explained, “This group wants to do something cool and get the picture up on social media. Even with engine mods, they want to do those things they can take pictures of and look cool.”
Well, car customization might be an “Instagrammable” activity today, but it is nothing new. In fact, Performance Auto & Sound Magazine (PASMAG), an automotive industry expert, notes that the roots of car modification date back to the early 20th century. 
It turns out that back in the 1920s and 30s, American enthusiasts began stripping down their cars to make them lighter and faster. It adds that they often scavenged parts from junkyards, giving birth to a do-it-yourself tinkerer’s ethos that still permeates car culture today.
Never miss a story that brings joy to the world.Add us on Google
It also states that the post-World War II era marked a significant turning point in the extent of available car modifications. Soldiers returning from the war effort brought home newfound mechanical skills and a penchant for innovation. 
The 1950s and 1960s then saw the rise of custom car culture. However, in the 60s and 70s, the effect snowballed. It’s worth noting that from the 1920s to today, every generation has left its mark on car customization culture.
Some exceptional customizations look like art. And it’s important to do what you love, whether it is something as simple as painting or turning your car into a masterpiece. 
Research emphasizes that taking time for activities that make you happy can improve mental health. Spending quality time on activities you enjoy also improves your performance in your professional life. It improves your creative problem-solving abilities, helps you build better relationships with your coworkers, and makes you more empathetic.

We can thank ABC-Mart for the latest release. The Japanese shoe brand essentially remixed the classic Authentic model with dressy brogue details and winged toes. Unlike the previous Vans Wingtip Eras, which debuted in black-and-white ""tuxedos,"" ABC-Mart's Authentic Wingtip Brogue shoes come in luxurious brown and black leather options.
It all comes at the perfect time, as dressy sneakers continue to hold strong on the market. Vans has also released several nice formal-style shoes, including other brogue skate sneakers and even session-approved loafers.
It's about time the wingtip Vans made a comeback. Thank you, ABC-Mart.
Speaking of which, the Vans Authentic Wingtip Brogue shoes are scheduled to drop in-store for￥11,000, which is around $68 at current exchange rates.

It will handle AI tasks and data transfer across One UI apps and services.
Reading time: 2 minutes
Abhijeet Mishra / SamMobile
Galaxy S26
Agentic AI will be one of the most important pillars of One UI 9.5 for high-end Galaxy phones and tablets. Alongside a more powerful Bixby app capable of getting daily tasks done, Samsung has developed a new system-level AI agent that takes Galaxy AI to a much deeper level.

We have discovered that Samsung designed the Personal Agent Core feature to deeply integrate AI assistants across its phones. Unlike traditional voice assistants, it can manage multi-step workflows and offer proactive follow-ups through natural language conversations.

Based on our findings, it offers a unified daily overview by gathering appointments, emails, package deliveries, reservations, and payment notices into one central hub. It appears to be a natural extension of the existing Now Brief feature.

Instead of just viewing your schedule, the assistant can check your calendar, message your contacts to arrange a meetup, track their replies, and automatically add confirmed plans to the Calendar app.

Asif Iqbal Shaik / SamMobile
                                            
Personal Agent Core connectors for stock One UI and Google apps

Asif Iqbal Shaik / SamMobile
                                            
Personal Agent Core connectors for Bixby Work, Custom Widget, and Daily Brief.

It can also take actions across Google and One UI apps and extract important details. Working across contacts, emails, messages, and recorded voice calls, it can identify meeting commitments and action items from past conversations.

You can also use conversational language to adjust device settings, control media playback, manage notes, send emails and messages, and set alarms and reminders. Additionally, the AI system can turn recorded calls, lectures, and meetings into clean transcripts and actionable summaries.

Samsung's upcoming AI system can automatically follow up and help with recurring tasks as well. It runs recurring tasks in the background and alerts you when an expected email or message arrives, saving you from constantly checking your inbox.

Over time, it learns your habits, interests, preferences, upcoming plans, and use cases to offer more relevant assistance.

It will likely need the Galaxy AI Premium plan for all its features to work. However, it remains unclear whether it will arrive in the beta release for the Galaxy S26 series later this month or wait for the stable version launching alongside the Galaxy S27 series early next year.

47,2026-11-14,"Apple @ Work is exclusively brought to you by Mosyle, the only Apple Unified Platform. Mosyle is the only solution that integrates in a single professional grade platform all the solutions necessary to seamlessly and automatically deploy, manage, and protect Apple devices at work. Over 45,000 organizations trust Mosyle to make millions of Apple devices work ready with no effort and at an affordable cost. Request your EXTENDED TRIAL today and understand why Mosyle is everything you need to work with Apple.
I’ve spent the past few weeks thinking about how Apple built up the best deal in enterprise AI. Two things happened that make me think Apple knew that all along (or maybe they didn’t and got lucky). Meta released its most capable open-weight model yet and optimized it to run on a Mac Studio. I also spent time with Osaurus, an app that runs a usable local AI model on something as small as a 16 GB Apple silicon laptop without needing a network connection. They even have two models that will run on 8 GB.
The Xserve has been dead since 2011, but its spiritual successor sits on a desk rather than in a rack.
About Apple @ Work: Bradley Chambers has been an Apple IT admin since 2009. Through his experience deploying and managing firewalls, switches, a mobile device management system, enterprise grade WiFi, 1000s of Macs, and 1000s of iPads, Bradley will highlight ways in which Apple IT managers deploy Apple devices, build networks to support them, train users, share stories from the trenches of IT management, and ways Apple could improve its products for IT departments.
For the last few years, the narrative has been that Apple fell behind in AI because it didn’t have a frontier model. Siri AI got delayed, and the Apple Intelligence rollout was not great. In hindsight, I think Apple was right not to run that race, as the frontier-model business has turned into somewhat of a capital bonfire. Every frontier lab is spending billions on GPUs to be the best for a few months at a time, and the moment a model is good enough, someone else releases an open-weight version that runs on hardware you already own.
Open-weight models prove it: Muse Spark Open has 816 billion parameters, 42 billion active parameters, and a million-token context window, and Meta is giving it away. Apple didn’t build that LLM. It built the hardware that runs it. With that being said, Siri is still far behind.
Look at what Apple has shipped while everyone was waiting on them to try to compete with Anthropic or OpenAI. With Apple silicon’s unified memory, the GPU can use up to 512 GB of memory on a single Mac Studio, instead of being limited to whatever fits on a graphics card.
MLX is Apple’s machine learning framework, and it’s built specifically for the unified memory architecture of Apple silicon. If you’ve used NumPy, the API will feel familiar right away. On top of that, MLX includes higher-level packages for neural networks and optimizers, along with function transformations for automatic differentiation and graph optimization, so you can build more complex models without giving up efficiency. It also has bindings in Swift, C++, and C, which means it runs on any Apple platform.
Apple’s own Machine Learning Research post shows four M5 Ultra Mac Studios running Muse Spark Open at over 36 tokens per second, which is faster than you can read, while peaking around 300 watts per machine.
Meta’s own words on the setup:
Apple’s unified memory architecture lets the M5 Ultra’s integrated GPU use up to 512 GB per Mac Studio, so two machines can hold an entire model. And the chip’s 1.2 TB/s of memory bandwidth is fast enough to read the small slice each token needs. The result is two Mac Studios behave like one supercomputer with a terabyte of unified memory.
We tested a range of agentic workflows running entirely on-device, using Muse Code powered by Muse Spark Open, including shipping real iOS changes to Ice Cubes (an open-source Mastodon client) — bug fixes, new features, full build — and driving DaVinci Resolve end to end, with the model reviewing footage frame by frame to find the strongest moments and crop them vertically.
That’s the Xserve pitch, rebuilt for 2026. Apple killed the Xserve in 2011 because nobody wanted a Mac in a data center. The Mac mini came along and provided some new use cases over the years. Now, a couple of Mac Studios can host a model that would otherwise require a rack of Nvidia hardware and a power budget to match, and it does so quietly enough to sit under a desk. Apple wasn’t trying to compete with OpenAI, Anthropic, or Nvidia. It was building the infrastructure for everything except the frontier model, and it looks like that’s where the market is going. A company like MacStadium has to be loving this future as well.
The Mac Studio story is the headline, but what matters most to most IT teams is at the other end of the lineup. I had Terence Pae, co-founder and CEO of Osaurus, on the Apple @ Work podcast last week. Osaurus is a free, open-source AI tool for the Mac. It helps manage local models that work offline, but it can also call a frontier model when needed. A really cool feature, when it does this, is that Osaurus runs a small local model first to find names, phone numbers, and account numbers in whatever you pasted, and swaps them for placeholders before anything goes to Claude or OpenAI.
Osaurus has no servers and no customer database. If the company went out of business tomorrow, the app would keep running until macOS broke it. Individuals use it for free. Workspaces, the paid tier, is for teams who want to share agents across their own Macs. There’s a Windows beta as well, which I think helps Apple’s case more than it hurts it. When the same app runs on both platforms, the Mac’s unified memory is the difference.
In talking with him, Terence mentioned he wouldn’t be surprised if a local model matches what today’s frontier models can do by this time next year. I believe him, and that’s the point. The frontier will keep moving, but good enough keeps moving too, and good enough is what most employees need most of the day. Apple spent the AI race building memory, bandwidth, and a framework instead of trying to match the frontier, and the narrative was that they were behind.
Now the most capable open-weight model in the US is optimized for the Mac.

Mosyle is the only solution that integrates in a single professional grade platform all the solutions necessary to seamlessly and automatically deploy, manage, and protect Apple devices at work. Over 45,000 organizations trust Mosyle to make millions of Apple devices work ready with no effort and at an affordable cost. Request your EXTENDED TRIAL today and understand why Mosyle is everything you need to work with Apple.

Bradley has worked at K-12 independent schools for much of the last 20 years, serving as the head of the information technology department and leading classroom technology integration. He’s well-versed in enterprise Wi-Fi, macOS and iOS system management, school technology, and SaaS tools.

48,2026-11-14,"The Olympus 17mm f/2.8 pancake is old, slower than most modern primes, and easy to find used for very little money. On Micro Four Thirds, where much of the appeal is keeping the camera small, that raises a fair question about whether a lens this modest still deserves a place on a current body.
Robin Wong answers by pairing the Olympus M.Zuiko Digital 17mm f/2.8 with an OM System PEN and walking through a wet market in Kuala Lumpur. The lens launched in 2009 with the E-P1, one of the first two Micro Four Thirds lenses Olympus ever made. Wong is upfront that it doesn't lead in sharpness or autofocus speed. What he values is the size, along with the way the silver pancake suits a small rangefinder-style body. The f/2.8 aperture also helps in the darker stalls of the market, where a little extra light makes a real difference. You get an honest look at how a lens with no standout spec performs when you're working fast in a crowded space with mixed light.
Autofocus turns out better than you might expect from 2009 glass. On the PEN, using continuous AF, Wong finds the lens quick and dependable, a noticeable step up from his memory of using single AF on older bodies like the E-PL7 and the original E-M5. On image quality, he calls the results decently sharp. The corners hold up well enough, and he doesn't see serious trouble from chromatic aberration, distortion, or flare. A newer camera body can change how an old lens behaves, and that's useful to remember the next time a cheap used prime shows up in a listing. The street frames he brings back from the market show what that combination can do.
On Micro Four Thirds, the 2x crop turns 17mm into roughly a 34mm field of view, and f/2.8 gives about the same depth of field you'd get at f/5.6 on full frame. For street work, that sits right where a lot of people want to be. Enough of the scene stays sharp to keep context around your subject, and you can still get some separation when you move in close. The other mounts have been heading back toward this kind of lens, too. Canon released the Canon RF 28mm f/2.8 STM in 2023, and Nikon put out the Nikon Z 26mm f/2.8 that same year. Fujifilm has offered the Fujifilm XF 27mm f/2.8 R WR for years. Each of those makers accepted a modest aperture to keep the lens close to the body. If your camera stays home because it's too bulky to carry, a pancake can be what gets it into your jacket pocket. The habit Wong shows in the market is easy to copy: pick a fixed focal length, stop chasing the shallowest depth of field, and let the size of the setup decide how often you shoot.
Wong also lays out the other 35mm-equivalent choices on the system, and there aren't many. The Panasonic Leica DG Summilux 15mm f/1.7 ASPH, a lens he uses and likes, lands closer to a 28mm equivalent, and he treats it as a different tool, since the two focal lengths ask you to work a scene in different ways. The Olympus M.Zuiko Digital 17mm f/1.8 is newer, sharper, brighter, and quicker to focus, but he dislikes its flat rendering and prefers the look of the older f/2.8. The Yongnuo YN 17mm f/1.7, which he has reviewed, is among the best 17mm lenses on the system in his view, though it can't match the pancake for size. The OM System M.Zuiko Digital ED 17mm f/1.2 PRO brings weather-sealing and a much brighter aperture, but it's also larger and heavier, and it costs a lot more, so he doesn't think it suits a camera as small as the PEN. Wong wants Panasonic and OM Digital Solutions to update their pancake lineups with weather-sealing, better coatings, and refined optics, since the current pancakes are showing their age. In his view, lenses this small are what Micro Four Thirds was built around.
Six stretches of the video follow Wong through the market from his point of view, so you can watch the pancake at work between his notes on it.
Alex Cooke is a Cleveland-based photographer and meteorologist. He teaches music and enjoys time with horses and his rescue dogs."

中文新闻
BERITA BAHASA INDONESIA
TOK PISIN
analysis
Topic:Housing Policy
Australia's housing crisis has entered new territory. (ABC News: John Gunn)
Australia's housing crisis has entered a new phase: a little less conversation, a little more action.
The first seeds were planted three years ago, with a landmark national agreement to build more homes. The target, 1.2 million in five years, is likely doomed. But what mattered was that a target was set at all. After years of lip service, leaders had promised to act on the root cause.
Then there was last year's economic roundtable. After much fanfare, the closed-door event had seemed like a fizzer. But for Treasurer Jim Chalmers, it helped build the case that Labor's old policy to wind back negative gearing could be reinvented, and framed as a leg up for homebuyers over investors.
A policy to boost supply and a policy to slash demand. Throw in the highest interest rates in 18 years, and all of a sudden a housing market that seemed as if it would never turn is turning very fast indeed. This week saw the steepest drop-off in new home loans ever recorded.
Unit values outperform houses in most capital cities as rising interest rates shrink borrowing power and reshape demand.
Commentators had long wondered whether, for all the hand-wringing about the housing crisis, Australians would actually want a solution when it came, since, one way or another, cheaper homes for the young can only mean less valuable homes for those who already own them.
But no more need to wonder: we're finding out. A tumbling market may not have been federal Labor's ideal pathway to affordable housing, but after months of dithering, the government took a tentative step this week towards embracing the chaos, framing it as a win for homebuyers.
In the coming weeks and months, Labor governments in Australia's two largest states will put their own higher-density plans to voters. At federal and state levels, Liberals are betting voters will dislike the prescribed cure worse than the sickness.
Topic:Housing Construction Industry
Topic:Housing Construction Industry
Topic:World Politics
Topic:Property Prices
Topic:Indigenous Culture
Labor did not expect its changes to the capital gains tax and negative gearing to tank house prices. Treasury analysis predicted prices would still grow, but by 2 per cent less. Chalmers has repeatedly said he is ""not targeting a particular price"".
Nor did it want prices to tank. In 2024, Housing Minister Clare O'Neil said it was her ""strong view"" that falling prices would be bad.
""Our policies are not going to reduce house prices. We want house prices to grow sustainably,"" she said then.
Clare O'Neil has been caught between championing affordability and protecting people's investments. (ABC News: Luke Stephenson)
But now they have fallen. The median national house price is 5.6 per cent below its March peak, and 3.9 per cent for apartments. The drop-off has been sharper in Melbourne and Sydney, and some analysts predict double-digit declines.
Other indicators point in the same direction. The auction clearance rate has plunged below 50 per cent, compared to its long-run average of 60 per cent. And new figures this week showed a record 15 per cent dip in new loans between June and September.
The chill was evident across the board, but it was clearly worse for investors (31 per cent) than for homebuyers (5 per cent). First home buyers (8 per cent) fared worse than established homebuyers, but still far better than investors.
High rates have clearly played a role. But they do not explain why investors are falling away by ""so much more than in previous interest rate increasing cycles"", as Reserve Bank governor Michele Bullock observed last week.
""[That] suggests something else is going on here, and [that] is the tax changes,"" she said. In other words, the tax changes are doing what they were designed to do: discourage investors.
Until this week, the government had been skittish about embracing that. Even after opinion polls suggested a majority of Australians believed that prices needed to fall, ministers were nervous about embracing the nosedive.
But on Wednesday, O'Neil ditched the script and stopped equivocating.
Research shows nurses, teachers and social workers can only afford a small percentage of rental listings across Australia.
""We've had a housing market geared towards investors for a generation, and where did it get us? Plummeting ownership, out-of-control house price increases, and a generation losing hope,"" she said.
""These numbers show the balance is shifting back … It is absolutely the case that volumes are down, as you would expect in a period where prices are softening. But these figures also clearly show a strong shift away from investors and towards Australians buying a home to live in.""
It is a risky gambit. First home buyers may be getting a leg up — CBA's Matt Comyn said yesterday their share of new loan applications doubled last month. But a fast-falling market is a risky one to enter, especially with high rates, and it can also chill housing supply.
Liberal housing spokesperson Andrew Bragg seized on the comments, calling them ""strange"".
""People would look at what Clare O'Neil says and think, 'Well, what planet is she living on? Why is she saying fewer loans is good?' Fewer loans is bad. We need to see more lending.""
A chorus of real estate moguls and personal finance celebrities agree. A prominent example is podcaster Mark Bouris, who predicted in The Australian this week ""the longest downturn in our history"" and warned: ""We're going to have a serious structural hit to people's wealth.""
The comments are revealing, hinting at what motivates many critics. Not affordability, but wealth.
That cannot be said of Bragg, one of a handful of politicians who have publicly embraced the idea that lower house prices could be welcome if they happen via a supply boom, not a demand chill.
But for many home owners, wealth is front of mind, and this is just as true on the supply side as it is on the demand side, as state governments are finding out.
In Victoria, the Labor government has pursued the most aggressively pro-supply policy in the country, designating several Melbourne suburban hubs as so-called activity centres, where high-density towers will be allowed in narrow strips and medium-density in wider surrounds.
Typical of the cynical political style of former premier Daniel Andrews and his successor Jacinta Allan, many zones are in places Labor will never win, including in Brighton and state Liberal leader Jess Wilson's Kew.
Jess Wilson said a Coalition government would shift the focus of high-rise development to Melbourne's inner city. (ABC News)
Locals have been whipped into a frenzy by dystopian artists' impressions of amorphous black skyscrapers invading their leafy shopping strips, a feature of media coverage.
Kew and Brighton will hardly shape the election, which pundits predict Labor will lose for other reasons, but Wilson has vowed to scrap the plan and give local councils a veto if elected.
A similar dynamic is emerging in Sydney, where the Minns Labor government will face voters early in the new year and where designated density zones in suburbs such as Woollahra and Crows Nest are also opposed by the opposition leader, Kellie Sloane.
Sloane, echoing Wilson and many Teal MPs, says she supports more housing but frames her complaint as one of consultation, and is also promising a council veto.
The argument has a reasonable-sounding guise — ""We just want locals to have a say"" — but to pro-supply campaigners including Peter Tulip, it is an all-too-familiar smokescreen for building fewer homes, since councils make decisions for existing residents, not potential new ones.
""[The] households who move in would be better off. As would renters and homebuyers who benefit from improved affordability. They greatly outnumber the opponents, but they don't get to vote for the local politicians who are biased against new housing,"" he said last week.
If Tulip is right, the political numbers game should favour Labor governments at federal and state levels if their policies really do make housing more affordable: they would lose Kew and Crows Nest, but win the country.
In different economic and political circumstances, that reckoning might have come gradually. Instead, we are hurtling towards it.

Three days after its stated launch, there's still has no price info

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Gigabyte has announced the AI TOP AERO, a roughly one-liter desktop built on Nvidia's RTX Spark platform that pairs a 20-core Arm-based Grace CPU with a Blackwell architecture RTX GPU, up to 128GB of unified LPDDR5X memory and up to a 4TB PCIe Gen5 SSD.
The company is pitching the machine as a Personal AI Studio for local AI development, content creation, and gaming on Windows 11.
However the announcement still lacks a price, a list of configurations, or anywhere to place an order, even as its closing line tells buyers to ""refer to local GIGABYTE announcements for regional product configurations, pricing and availability""; As of November 13, Gigabyte's own AI TOP PC listing showed only the AI TOP 100 B850 and the AI TOP ATOM.
Gigabyte now sells two kinds of Nvidia's Spark platform-based hardware. The AI TOP ATOM is a DGX Spark system built on Nvidia's GB10 chip. It runs the Linux-based DGX OS and, according to Gigabyte's October 2026 announcement of a 64GB version, can be clustered up to four units deep over built-in ConnectX-7 networking.
The AERO is an RTX Spark machine built on the N1X Superchip. It runs Windows 11 and lists USB Type-C, 10GbE, and Wi-Fi 7, with no mention of clustering hardware.
Under the lid, however, the two are close relatives: Nvidia's specification table lists the desktop N1X with 6,144 CUDA cores, 20 CPU cores, and a 140W TDP, and The Register describes RTX Spark systems as built on the same GB10 silicon as DGX Spark. Gigabyte's release, however, states none of those figures, nor the AERO's dimensions, weight, or port count.
The argument for the AERO is that Windows makes this hardware useful to more people. Jensen Huang, then Nvidia's chief executive, set that tone at Computex in May 2026, declaring, ""This is the new PC. The personal AI computer,"" in a release naming Acer and Gigabyte as vendors that would follow the first six.
Sign up to the TechRadar Pro newsletter to get all the top news, opinion, features and guidance your business needs to succeed!
Gigabyte's version of the pitch leans on LM Studio, Ollama, ComfyUI and Nvidia's Nemotron models, plus Nvidia PAIR, a free, open-source router introduced in beta in September that spreads independent inference requests across the PCs on a local network.
Finding a unit available online is just as hard as finding the price tag for an AI workhorse that already finds most of its competition sold out. Gigabyte's silence on cost comes at a time when memory sets the price. Nvidia has raised its own 128GB DGX Spark to $6,950, almost three-quarters more than it cost at launch, and The Register judged at the time that the pricing ""doesn't bode well"" for RTX Spark notebooks and mini PCs.
Gigabyte's 64GB AI TOP ATOM, the nearest reference point in its own range, starts at $4,999 with a 2TB SSD in tow. The first RTX Spark desktops to reach checkout are no cheaper. MSI's 1.3-liter EdgeMesa N AI+ went on sale in the US this week at $4,699 with 64GB and 1TB, and $7,999 with 128GB and 4TB, the latter out of stock when Notebookcheck checked on November 11.
That is a $3,300 step for the extra memory and storage for a platform that is already seemingly prohibitively expensive for end users. Microsoft lists its 128GB Surface RTX Spark Dev Box at $5,999 with shipping due this month, and the 128GB Surface Laptop Ultra has had lead times stretch to late December within days of its launch.
Buyers who value capacity over CUDA have another route. GMKtec's EVO-X5 Pro, built on AMD's Gorgon Halo platform, launched from $6,399 with 192GB of memory, up to 160GB of which can be assigned to graphics. None of the price gouging is likely to ease soon, with one forecast predicting the memory crisis will last at least another three years.
Until Gigabyte's regional offices publish prices and stock, the AI TOP AERO is a spec sheet with a date on it. The hardware is credible, largely because it already ships under other names. The open questions are how well a one-liter chassis cools 140W, how much of that 128GB a Windows workload can really use, and whether the top configuration can be bought at all before the year is out.
