52,2026-11-14,"It costs less than lunch to buy a smart plug, which is why you don’t think twice before making the purchase. But new research from Bitdefender and NETGEAR puts some of these everyday devices among the most commonly linked to security flaws.
I'm not trying to create panic, but before buying your next home gadget, there's a question you must ask—and a check you should run on the devices you already own.
Your phone or PC updates can wait, but not these devices.
 For context, Bitdefender and NETGEAR's security analysis drew on 10.4 million protected home routers. This research ranked the devices by how often they matched known vulnerabilities. Top of the list were smart TVs, with 270.3 million recorded matches with known vulnerabilities, while smart plugs came in second with 266.7 million observations.
Note that these observations are matches between a device and a known vulnerability. This doesn’t imply a count of unique products or successful attacks. A single device may rack up several observations. Also, since Bitdefender and NETGEAR sell router protection, they have a stake in keeping you concerned. So I wouldn't call this a definitive verdict, but the numbers are worth taking seriously.
The simple mismatch between a device's useful life and its security support period seems like the major factor in this problem. Some of these everyday gadgets may last between five and 10 years even though the security support for them lasts only two to four years.
When I ran the math, at best, a device may have up to an additional year without fixes; at worst, up to eight years. I came to this conclusion by factoring general ranges, since I couldn't identify any plug-specific lifespan.
Here are the top three devices and percentage share in Bitdefender and NETGEAR's report:
Device
Share
Observations
Smart TVs
22.7%
270.3 million
Smart plugs
22.4%
266.7 million
Storage (NAS)
22.0%
261.6 million
 This report doesn’t rank devices by price. But NETGEAR's summary points a finger at low-cost devices when it refers to plugs as cheap and rarely updated. It says once makers stop selling them, they often stop getting fixes. Because NETGEAR stands to benefit from selling router protection, I reviewed manufacturers' policies and firmware histories to verify claims.
You can tell how long fixes will run from a manufacturer’s security support period. This is often a fixed date or time frame after the product is discontinued. It’s different from a warranty, which legally binds the manufacturer to repair, replace, or refund a product if it breaks.
I went through the product listings, firmware histories, and security-support policies of four U.S. market smart plugs at different prices. The goal was to go beyond simple evidence that a manufacturer releases firmware updates and find a published minimum security support period.
Plug
Price per plug
Published security support
Meross MSS110HK
$8.75
At least two years after discontinuation
TP-Link Tapo P125M
$9.99
Security-related firmware updates documented; no minimum duration found
Ring Smart Plug
$24.99
At least until December 31, 2030
Shelly Plug US Gen4
$24.99
Gen4 firmware updates promised; no minimum duration stated
Neither TP-Link nor Shelly establishes a guaranteed support lifespan. Of the two priced below $15, one published its minimum support duration. The same goes for the devices priced at $24.99. I couldn't conclude which manufacturer would include clear commitments just from the prices.
My takeaway was not to focus on price alone, but on whether the manufacturer tells you when security updates will stop.
That said, smart plugs are generally cheap devices, and that's why I'm giving them more attention. TVs are a pricier counterpart that topped the report. Their bigger price tag doesn't guarantee patched devices.
The report specifically calls out TV apps with bundled residential proxy software. After you accept the terms, you may carry someone else's traffic on your connection, and the sites that receive it see your home address.
The case for a plug being a security loophole may not be immediately evident, since it doesn't hold your photos or passwords. But it becomes clearer when you understand that it shares a network with devices that do. If an attacker gains access, they can hide and move across the network, and that's a risk worth taking seriously.
However, what I found more worrying was that 99.97% of the recorded flaws were publicly known. If a vulnerability is known, chances are that a fix may exist, but that fix will never get to your smart plug if the manufacturer has already stopped updating it.
Port scans account for 90.1% of observed attack activity; they indicate probing for responsive services rather than a successful breach.
This is an important perspective, because when I call a plug the biggest liability, I mean how easy it is to be overlooked, despite being on the same network as more valuable devices. It's one small device that isn't getting as much attention as its security warrants.
The rule should be simple for your next purchase: assume there's no support if a maker doesn't explicitly mention a support period. For the devices you currently own, replace them if you can't find a support period published by the manufacturer. Where the support is still active, turn on automatic updates.
So before you close this page, go around your devices and find out whether any of them are worth decommissioning. But also remember that you don't have to stop using a TV because it's past its support period. You can take it off your Wi-Fi and plug in a streaming stick that still gets updates.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

53,2026-11-14,"
Vivo has added “Max” to the name, but the X500 Pro Max is essentially the successor to the X300 Pro rather than an entirely new addition to the lineup. Following its September launch in China, the phone has now made its way to global markets. The family resemblance is clear, from the oversized circular camera island to the emphasis on Zeiss imaging, but updated camera sensors and a larger battery promise improvements where they matter.
Battery life was one of my frustrations with the X300 Pro, and software updates never quite brought it up to my expectations. That made me particularly interested in whether this generation could deliver better endurance alongside the photography experience that keeps drawing me back to Vivo. The extensive camera controls and optional telephoto extenders certainly give photography enthusiasts plenty to explore. But with prices starting at €1,499 (roughly $1,600), these improvements also need to make a convincing case for spending more.
Designer: Vivo

The Vivo X500 Pro Max carries forward the design language of the X300 Pro, with a bold, round camera island taking center stage on a back panel that resembles frosted glass. The large circular module gives the phone a distinctive, camera-focused identity and an unmistakably flagship look. Vivo offers four color choices, including white, orange, gray, and black.
I received the gray variant, which features shimmering speckles that catch the light as you tilt the phone. The rear panel is subtly divided into two sections, with the entire upper section sitting slightly higher than the lower portion. A grid pattern adds visual interest around the camera island, contrasting with the solid lower section without changing the texture shared by both areas. Together, the subtle shimmer, patterned upper section, and prominent camera housing give the X500 Pro Max a premium look without making the design feel overly busy.












Despite its large footprint, the Vivo X500 Pro Max is surprisingly comfortable to handle. The white, gray, and orange models measure 6.43 × 3.02 × 0.32 inches (163.32 × 76.82 × 8.12 mm) and weigh 8.08 ounces (229 grams). The black version is slightly thinner at 0.31 inches (7.95 mm) and lighter at 7.94 ounces (225 grams). Gentle curves along the frame and balanced weight distribution make the phone comfortable to grip during extended use. Even the prominent camera island doesn’t interfere with my natural grip.
The back panel has a pleasantly matte texture that resists fingerprints and smudges, keeping it looking clean even after hours of use. Vivo also retains the customizable Shortcut button on the left side of the phone, giving it more flexibility this time around. You can assign separate actions to a double press and a long press, making it useful for more than a single shortcut. Customization goes beyond choosing from a list of preset functions, giving you more control over how those shortcuts work.

The Vivo X500 Pro Max is powered by the MediaTek Dimensity 9600 Pro, built on a 2nm process. Storage options include 256GB, 512GB, and 1TB, with every configuration limited to 12GB of RAM. It runs OriginOS 7, and everyday performance is consistently smooth. Multitasking, gaming, and demanding apps are handled with impressive ease, with little to complain about even when pushing the phone hard.
The expansive 6.85-inch 2K AMOLED display delivers sharp visuals, with an adaptive refresh rate ranging from 1Hz to 144Hz. The full 144Hz is available only in select apps and games, so you won’t see that maximum refresh rate throughout the interface. The display has a stated peak brightness of 4,400 nits, and outdoor visibility is excellent, even in harsh sunlight. The stereo speakers are less impressive, offering serviceable sound for everyday listening without a particularly immersive experience.

As with last year’s Pro model, the X500 Pro Max places its main emphasis on the camera system co-engineered with Zeiss. Sensor sizes, megapixel counts, and focal lengths remain largely familiar. The changes this year focus on updated sensors, faster focusing, and improved HDR processing rather than a dramatic overhaul of the camera configuration.
The 50MP main camera uses a new Sony LYTIA L910 sensor in a 1/1.28-inch format, paired with LOFIC technology and an f/1.57 aperture. The 200MP telephoto camera gets the same 1/1.4-inch Samsung ISOCELL HP0 sensor found in the X300 Ultra. Completing the trio is a 50MP ultra-wide camera with a 1/2.75-inch Samsung ISOCELL JN5 sensor, an f/2.0 aperture, and autofocus.
Main Camera, 1x
Main Camera, 1x
Main Camera, 1.5x
Vivo continues to offer an extensive selection of manual controls and filters, which it calls color styles. You can fine-tune parameters including exposure, sharpness, highlights, shadows, grain, and vignette, among others. There’s plenty of room to shape the image to your taste rather than simply accepting the default processing. My favorite Humanistic mode has also been renamed Moment.
In use, the X500 Pro Max produces more vivid images than I’m accustomed to seeing from Vivo, particularly with the Vivid color style. If that isn’t your preference, switching color styles or adjusting individual parameters makes it easy to dial things back. Regardless of your preferred look, the phone takes excellent photos. The main camera delivers wide dynamic range and retains detail across varied lighting conditions. The telephoto lens produces crisp images with great detail and accurate white balance, making it a reliable option for portraits or distant subjects. I especially enjoyed taking black and white portrait with the telephoto lens.

For those choosing the top-tier package, the photography kit adds versatility. It is essentially the same kit offered with the X300 Ultra, including 200mm and 400mm telephoto extenders that attach over the 200MP telephoto camera using a dedicated case and adapter. The extra reach opens up more possibilities for photographing wildlife, sports, and distant landscape details. Sadly, the selfie monitor included in the Creator Kit for the Chinese market is absent from the global package.
Video has often taken a back seat to photography on smartphones. This year, though, brands are giving video capabilities more attention in their flagship phones. The X500 Pro Max continues that trend, particularly with its high-frame-rate recording options.
Telephoto Camera, 3.5x
Telephoto Camera, 3.5x, Textured Color Style
Telephoto Camera, 3.5x, Amber Color Style
All cameras support 4K recording and Dolby Vision. The main camera can record 4K at up to 240 fps, while the telephoto supports 4K at up to 120 fps, giving you high-resolution slow-motion options at different focal lengths. The ultra-wide and front cameras both support 4K at up to 60 fps. For those who prioritize resolution, the main and telephoto cameras can also record 8K at 30 fps.
Video quality is very good overall, with sharp detail and effective stabilization. Beyond image quality, automatic subject tracking with auto framing is a particularly useful addition. You can choose full-body, half-body, or close-up framing depending on the shot you want. In my experience, it works impressively well, keeping the subject framed as they move from side to side or up and down.
Battery life is another meaningful improvement over the X300 Pro. My experience with that phone at launch was disappointing, and although software updates helped, its endurance never quite met my expectations. I’m happy to report that the X500 Pro Max’s larger 7,800mAh battery comfortably lasts a full day of super heavy use and two days of moderate use. Support for 90W wired and 40W wireless charging rounds out a much more reassuring battery experience.
Ultrawide Camera, 0.6x, B&W
Telephoto Camera, Portrait Mode, B&W
The Vivo X500 Pro Max is built for long-term durability, featuring both IP68 and IP69 ratings for robust protection against dust, water, and high-pressure jets. Its Vivo Armor Glass adds an extra layer of scratch and drop resistance, helping the device withstand daily wear and unexpected accidents. These measures ensure that the phone is equipped to handle the demands of everyday life and occasional mishaps without quickly showing signs of damage.
Vivo also promises five years of OS updates, which, while not the longest in the industry, still offers a decent level of long-term software support. This commitment provides peace of mind for most users looking to keep their devices secure and up to date. While these durability and software measures put the X500 Pro Max on par with other premium flagships, there remains room for growth in packaging sustainability and repairability.


The Vivo X500 Pro Max starts at €1,499 (approximately $1,600 USD), placing it in the ultra-premium flagship category. For those who want the top-tier 1TB model bundled with the photography kit, the price climbs to €2,199 (around $2,350 USD). For context, last year’s X300 Pro with 16GB RAM and 512GB storage was priced at €1,399, while the X300 Ultra with 1TB storage reached €2,000 in Europe without the photography kit. This year, Vivo follows a broader industry trend of higher pricing that is largely driven by increased memory costs and ongoing component shortages.
At these price points, the X500 Pro Max competes directly with high-end devices such as the Samsung Galaxy S24 Ultra and iPhone 15 Pro Max. The strongest value is found if you are a photography enthusiast who appreciates Zeiss color science and advanced creative controls. If you do not need the photography kit, the X300 Ultra still stands as a compelling option since it offers even more robust camera hardware along with greater RAM and storage. Ultimately, while the X500 Pro Max impresses with its hardware and imaging capabilities, this year’s higher pricing is more a reflection of market pressures than unique upgrades from Vivo.

The Vivo X500 Pro Max addresses my biggest frustration with the X300 Pro, delivering the battery endurance I wanted without losing the photography experience that keeps drawing me back to Vivo. The telephoto camera remains a highlight, particularly for portraits, while the extensive controls leave plenty of room to develop your own look. Video feels equally well considered, with sharp footage, effective stabilization, and genuinely useful auto framing. Add comfortable handling and consistently smooth performance, and this feels like a meaningful refinement of a familiar formula rather than a reinvention.
The harder part is the price, especially if you want the 1TB model bundled with the photography kit. The telephoto extenders are appealing, but the missing selfie monitor is disappointing. The X300 Ultra also deserves consideration if camera hardware matters more to you than the bundled accessories. For me, the X500 Pro Max’s appeal comes down to how much I enjoy creating with it, now backed by battery life I can trust. It is an expensive choice, but if photography and video are central to how you use your phone, there is plenty here to appreciate.

54,2026-11-14,"Steelmaker promises back pay for workers whose layoffs its US owner blamed on tariffs, weak demand and imports.
Save
Share
Canadian steelmaker Stelco says it will recall all 175 workers laid off at its plant in Hamilton and pay them retroactively, following a weeks-long standoff that pitted workers and the Canadian government against its owner in the United States.
The announcement on Friday came shortly after Canada’s Industry Minister Melanie Joly said she would seek a court order to force Cleveland-Cliffs to sell Stelco unless she heard from management by the end of the day.
The company said in a statement on Friday that it had reached agreements with enough customers to restart its finishing operations and that workers would be recalled on December 1. They would receive back pay for the standard wages they would have earned while laid off.
The president of United Steelworkers Local 1005, Ron Wells, welcomed the announcement and credited pressure from workers, the union and the government.
At a news conference, he called the layoffs “illegal” and “immoral” which should never have happened.
Alongside him was Joly, who said the outcome sent a message to investors that Canada was “a country of the rule of law and that the law applies to everyone, notwithstanding the size of your wallet.”
“This fight was absolutely worth it,” she said.
Cleveland-Cliffs, which bought Stelco in 2024, announced in late September that up to 500 workers would be laid off at Stelco’s plants in Hamilton and Nanticoke, Ontario, citing weak demand and difficult market conditions for cold-rolled and coated steel products used to make everything from cars to appliances.
It blamed US President Donald Trump’s tariffs on Canadian steel, which made it more expensive in the US, as well as competition from cheaper foreign steel, which it argued was eating into domestic demand for Canadian steel.
Ottawa said the layoffs violated binding commitments the company made when it bought Stelco, including keeping the same number of Canadian unionised employees through 2029.
The government and the union also argued that Cleveland-Cliffs’ CEO Lourenco Goncalves violated a commitment to advocate for policies that would benefit the steel industries in both Canada and the US when he publicly supported Trump’s tariffs on steel, including Canada’s.
Goncalves’ open support for the tariffs raised questions, given that he also blamed them for Stelco’s financial troubles.
In its statement, Cleveland-Cliffs said its customers had committed to supporting its operations in Hamilton “despite the lack of response from the federal government to address the shortfalls in their tariff response program.”
It didn’t provide details on the agreements it had reached with customers or explain what had changed to make restarting operations viable.
Cleveland-Cliffs did not respond to a request for comment.

56,2026-11-14,"With a promotion and production budget estimated at around $120 million, the anti-capitalist feature film How to Rob a Bank got mugged by the free market at this weekend’s box office.
Ha.
Ha.
Although it debuted on over 2,800 screens, it still opened to a humiliating $4 million.
Meanwhile, Jim Caviezel’s Ghost Soldier, which opened on fewer screens (2,560) and was either ignored or outright hated by America’s fascist and corrupt critical establishment, nearly doubled How to Rob a Bank with a $7.1 million opening.
Topping the weekend box office, and this makes me very happy, is The Mighty Johnny Depp’s Ebenezer, a spin on the widely beloved tale of Ebenezer Scrooge and a Christmas Carol. Depp landed at number one with a $20 million-plus opening and is expected to hold on straight through to the holidays.
Depp is not only one of our most interesting and talented actors, but he was also unjustly sidelined for years due to the fascist #MeToo movement’s assault on men. Depp’s ex-wife, Amber Heard, accused him of abuse. Without due process, Depp’s career was summarily annihilated by Hollywood’s sexual McCarthyites.  Depp then took Heard to court and won his vindication. She was proven to be a liar and exposed as a disgusting piece of work who (allegedly) defecated in Depp’s bed.
Depp is now on the way to a long overdue comeback, and the movies are better for it.
Ebenezer’s production and promotion budget probably hovers around $90 million. By Monday, its global gross should get it about halfway there.
Of How to Rob a Bank, movie critic Christian Toto reveals at the New York Post, “Bank repeatedly lectures the audience on the evils of modern-day capitalism, demanding we rise up and violently fight back. Rob a bank. Shoot an off-duty cop trying to restore law and order,” he writes. “Through it all, our ‘heroes’ are treated like flawless Robin Hoodlums, beyond moral reproach.”
What a disservice modern-day Hollywood is doing to young people clueless enough to fall for all of this anti-American, anti-capitalist propaganda.
Give up, it’s hopeless, cried Hollywood. Why bother trying at all?
Sure, the message that turns violent bank robbers into folk heroes is bad enough. But many more people will be negatively affected by a message that portrays the American way of life as oppressive and futile.
No system is perfect, but free market capitalism is as perfect as you will ever find on this earth, and the relentless message from pop culture (and our government-run school system) is one of despair and hopelessness, as opposed to one that speaks the truth, which is this: It might be harder for some, but if you work hard, stay away from drugs and alcohol, and don’t have children before you’re married, life is good in America. Keep trying. Keep fighting. Don’t ever give up.
To choose a message of hopelessness instead of urging people to aspire and keep fighting really is a form of cultural abuse.

Jensen Huang resigned as Nvidia’s CEO, president and board member within days of apologizing to employees for being unfaithful to his wife of about 40 years. His departure followed reports of multiple affairs and a board investigation into whether he’d been targeted by foreign intelligence.
One reported relationship involved Alice Huang, a Chinese businesswoman who’s unrelated to him and an executive at Megaspeed, a major Nvidia customer. U.S. officials alleged she may have ties to Chinese intelligence, raising concerns about national security and chip exports.
Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of healthcare who reported to Huang, was also reportedly involved with him. Those allegations included claims that the pair had been filmed at a hotel during a company event. A separate report described an alleged affair with another, unnamed Nvidia employee.
In his memo to employees, Huang acknowledged his infidelity and apologized. That admission didn’t establish every allegation surrounding the reported relationships.
Nvidia shares fell sharply during the turmoil, with reports putting the loss in market value in the hundreds of billions of dollars. CFO Colette Kress was named interim CEO.

58,2026-11-14,"Arm Holdings CEO Rene Haas, who previously worked at Nvidia, is being lined up as a potential successor to Jensen Huang."
59,2026-11-14,"Globally, one in nine people lives with diabetes, as blockbuster injectable drugs begin making way for tablets instead.
Save
Share
Medication that has been a lifeline for patients with uncontrollable diabetes is now slowly shifting from injections to pills, a move that could fundamentally alter treatment for the disease – and who can access it.
This shift could also affect the treatment of obesity, a long misunderstood condition once associated with overeating and laziness, rather than the metabolic disorder it is now recognised as.
GLP-1 (glucagon-like peptide-1) receptor agonists therapy isn’t new. The first FDA-approved drug, Exenatide, hit the market in 2005 and was followed by Liraglutide in 2009, which was seen as a revolutionary alternative to insulin.
But the market has until recently been dominated by weekly injections like Ozempic and Wegovy (semaglutide), and Mounjaro (tirzepatide). These drugs have also proved successful for weight loss.
Now, once-daily oral GLP pills are emerging as an alternative, especially for the diabetes-stricken Global South.
Oral once-daily pills of the same wonder drug could make access more affordable, and less reliant on the cold chain that GLP-1 injections and insulin depend on – and that are harder to secure in emerging economies.
One in nine people in the world between the ages of 20-79 lives with diabetes, and one in eight people globally is obese.
The GLP injections come with a high price tag and are often not covered by insurance companies, which treat them as an elective drug.
In the United States, Eli Lily’s Mounjaro costs about $500 for a month’s worth of doses; in the UK, about 199 pounds ($264); and in Saudi Arabia, 1,261 riyals ($336). In India, the brand is locally manufactured and available at 14,766 rupees ($157). In Pakistan, the imported Mounjaro is a whopping 220,000 rupees ($723), while the locally manufactured Tirzepatide from Getz Pharma costs 17,000 rupees ($61).
While pills are usually taken once a day, injections are once per week.
Pakistan has the highest diabetes prevalence in the world – 31.4 percent of its population suffer from the disease – followed by the Marshall Islands (25.7 percent) and Kuwait (25.6 percent).
Many other Middle Eastern countries rank among nations with the highest diabetes prevalence.
For many countries in the Global South, the relative affordability of oral GLP drugs, where the locally manufactured oral semaglutide is about $18 for a 30-day supply, could make them more accessible for diabetes and obesity patients, remove the need for refrigeration and make rural distribution easier.
Dr Zakir Alavi is an endocrinologist in Karachi, Pakistan and has been treating patients with diabetes and obesity for nearly 30 years.
“Pakistan is a country of contrast. We have, on one hand, childhood malnutrition, underweight, and stunted growth. On the other hand, we have childhood and adult obesity,” said Alavi. “And surprisingly, there is at least some element in the adult and childhood obesity which can be linked to a malnourished mother, and an underweight, premature baby.”
Malnutrition during pregnancy can lead to low birth weight, and babies born small who gain weight rapidly in infancy face a higher risk of childhood obesity, according to doctors.
Alavi said GLP drugs – whether the injectables or pills – have revolutionised the management of type 2 diabetes and obesity. They not only help type 2 diabetes patients manage their weight, but can also help non-diabetics tackle obesity, improving cardiac and renal outcomes, liver disease, obstructive sleep apnea and osteoarthritis.
“They can be used for management of all these things when weight reduction is important, or sugar control is important, or both,” he said.
Rayaz Malik is a professor of medicine at Weill Cornell Medica College Qatar, and a senior consultant at Hamad Medical Corporation in Doha. Malik has been at the front line of helping patients with diabetes and obesity, and he echoed Alavi’s views.
“We looked at how much weight you need to lose to cure diabetes, the magic number seemed to come in at around 15kg, at least,” he said, speaking of research on the subject.
With the wider availability of GLP medication in oral pill form, a cure for diabetes is closer than ever for many more people.
Jens Juul Holst is one of the central scientists behind the GLP revolution that has given millions around the world a way to live with diabetes and, for those struggling with obesity, a chance to reclaim their bodies.
A professor, physician and physiologist, Holst lives in Copenhagen and began his journey with gut hormones in the 1970s.
“When we discovered this, what we found was an insulin-releasing peptide from the gut,” Holst told Al Jazeera. “We were interested in what regulates insulin secretion, and that was as a physiological mechanism because we had some people who were developing too low blood glucose levels, [which is called] hypoglycemia, and we were looking for a peptide that could create insulin release stimulation and then be responsible for the low glucose levels.”
He says reactive hypoglycemia, which patients experience after certain operations like bariatric surgery, explains how the GLP molecule works.
Eventually, his team discovered that the molecule plays a role not only in insulin release and secretion, but also helps regulate appetite and food intake.
“The discovery of that was actually perhaps even more exciting than the discovery of its insulin-releasing activities,” Holst said, the excitement in his voice rising. “And then, the big day really was when we were able to infuse people with diabetes with this new peptide and see what happened.”
They found that an intravenous infusion of GLP-1 “would normalise blood glucose levels completely in people with long-standing, problematic type 2 diabetes and this happened in the course of four hours,” he said. “From then on, we knew that we were really onto something big.”
Holst and his team’s discovery led to what is now known as semaglutide, which paved the way for another drug, tirzepatide, and a third drug called retatrutide that is currently undergoing trials with a potential release in 2027.
Eli Lilly and Novo Nordisk, two of the primary manufacturers, expect the GLP market to jump from $80bn per year to $135bn by 2030.
Obesity alone was responsible for 3.7 million deaths worldwide in 2024.
In December 2025, the World Health Organization issued guidelines recommending GLP-1 medicines as a long-term treatment for obesity, which affects more than 1 billion people globally.
“As we realised that it was a very good anti-diabetic therapy, it was obvious that millions could benefit from this,” Holst said.
“Because now you have a population which is 40 percent of the globe who would be interested in regulating body weight and the amount of interest would be huge,” he said, referring to the WHO’s 2016 estimate that 39 percent of adults worldwide are overweight. In 2024, WHO revised this estimate to 45 percent.
Injectable GLP medicines, particularly tirzepatide and semaglutide, remain the most effective treatments because they provide the strongest evidence for long-term weight loss and cardiovascular benefits.
The human gut releases a hormone called GLP-1 when we eat. As blood sugar rises, GLP-1 instructs your pancreas to release insulin. GIP is a gut hormone also released when we eat. Like GLP-1, it tells the pancreas to release insulin as blood sugar rises.
GLP-1 also slows how quickly food leaves your stomach, helping you feel full. GLP-1 medicines, therefore, mimic the hormone’s effects to lower blood sugar and reduce appetite.
Imagine a six-way traffic light when the lights aren’t working, leading to chaos over who can pass the intersection and who can’t.
Semaglutide, a GLP-1 drug, works on one receptor. This is like having a single-lane underpass at this intersection, meaning there is a guaranteed flow of traffic, albeit limited.
Tirzepatide is a GLP and a GIP, meaning it works on two receptors. That’s like having a signal-free overhead bridge, traffic lights at the intersection and an underpass making traffic much smoother. In effect, the organs and cells are rerouted to work the way they are supposed to.
Oral GLP-1 medicines promise the convenience of a pill without sacrificing much of the weight-loss effect, but they still face one major hurdle: the stomach.
Unlike injections, which deliver the drug directly into the body, oral semaglutide must survive digestion before a small fraction is absorbed.
That is why it must be taken on an empty stomach and at a higher dose.
Newer oral GLP-1 drugs such as orforglipron, however, are designed to overcome those limits and can be taken without fasting.
While oral drugs appear capable of delivering similar results for many patients, there are still few head-to-head trials comparing pills directly with injections, experts say.
The biggest winners may be people in countries already carrying the world’s heaviest diabetes burden.
If future versions become available as tablets and prices fall after patent expiry, the impact in South Asia and the Middle East could be enormous.
Malik said once-a-week GLP oral medication isn’t that far from being a reality.
Meanwhile, he added, efforts are also under way to develop formulations of the current drugs that are much longer-acting – once-a-month doses might work.
What makes the GLP-1 drugs work is their ability to mimic what the body naturally does, Malik said.
“The clever way for treatment is to do what your human body is doing,” he said. “And it’s transforming people’s lives.”

Snecx Tan designed the Vision FG around his Superlock, and CIVIVI built it as the affordable way into that mechanism, with a 3.54-inch reverse tanto blade in Nitro-V steel and a full-size black G10 handle over stainless liners. Twenty-five percent off is a real cut.

The Elementum II runs smaller at $65 with a 2.96-inch blade, while the Vision FG spends its size on grip and lock access instead. The Superlockâs bar rides along the spine of the handle, worked from the top of the knife instead of from a liner buried inside the scales. Because the bar sits outside the bladeâs swing path, a left-handed carrier gets the same controls as a right-handed one, and the tip-up clip swaps sides to match. It closes without a fingertip crossing the edge. The bar is the first thing a thumb finds on the draw.
A caged ceramic ball bearing runs the pivot, so the blade rolls open with a push on the thumb studs rather than a flick of the wrist. Both thumb studs are stainless, like the screws, the clip and the backspacer, and the whole hardware set carries the same black finish as the blade. Nothing on this knife is decorative.
No flipper tab interrupts the spine. The studs handle every opening.
The reverse tanto keeps most of its 3.54 inches straight for draw cuts through cardboard, strapping and shrink wrap, then finishes in a thickened tip that takes the piercing work a fine drop point usually refuses.
CIVIVI grinds the Nitro-V flat and rates it at 59 to 61 HRC, a working hardness that favors toughness over bragging rights or brochure numbers. Itâs a work profile, not a slicer.
A straight edge is the easiest shape to keep sharp on a stone, since the whole edge meets it at one angle from heel to tip.
Black runs across the blade, the blackened stainless liners and the backspacer, so the whole knife carries one dark finish from every angle. Open, the knife measures 7.95 inches overall, with a blade 0.12 inches thick behind a handle just under half an inch across. The proportions stay plain.
A flat grind and 0.12 inches of blade stock put this knife on the slicing side of the folder market rather than the pry-bar side. The long straight edge presents its full length to flat material in a single pass, from the heel right up to where the tip takes over.
At 4.35 ounces with a 4.45-inch closed length, the Vision FG fills a pocket the way a work knife does, and the broad G10 handle takes up real room beside a phone or a wallet. The trade buys a full four-finger grip on textured G10 and a lock that works the same in either hand, with four round cutouts through the scales keeping the big handle from feeling like a brick. You feel it in the pocket daily.

62,2026-11-14,"Brandon Sanderson is one of the most successful fantasy authors alive, and we're about to see a lot more of him. Apple TV is adapting his Stormlight Archive and Mistborn books to the screen, which should funnel a lot of people back to the source material.
Hopefully they have time on their hands. While Sanderson has written plenty of shorter books, he can write a doorstopping fantasy series as well as anybody. He's written five Stormlight Archive books so far, with another five planned, and the shortest one is still almost 400,000 words long.
They're worth the read, but there are plenty of other great fantasy book series out there that don't require quite as much of a commitment.
Published in the 1990s, the Farseer Trilogy centers around Fitz, the illegitimate son of a prince who is brought to live with the royal family and gets trained as an assassin. His royal blood gives him access to a magic technique called the Skill, whereby he can perform feats of telepathy. He can also practice the Wit, a kind of magic that lets him bond with animals.
There's a lot of social stigma attached to the Wit. It's outlawed, and its practitioners banished to the edges of society. Much of the Farseer trilogy deals with Fitz's sense of isolation and otherness. There are royal plots and murders and action scenes, but it's more internal than a lot of other famous fantasy series; you won't find many world-ending battles here.
The first book in the Farseer trilogy, The Assassin's Apprentice, runs around 160,000 words, and the next two books are longer. It's not a tiny series, but it's a far easier read than some of the larger fantasy epics out there. That said, if you really enjoy this trilogy, Robin did write 12 more books in the series.
Published between 2017 and 2021, The Green Bone Saga takes place mainly on the island of Kekon, which is the world's main source of jade. In this world, jade can be used to greatly augment strength, speed, and senses, and is highly sought after. Rival clans fight to control the market. We mainly follow the members of the No Peak Clan, led by the Kaul family. The books take place over multiple generations, so we see the characters try to adjust to a changing world that might just phase out the clan system entirely.
The Green Bone Saga is ambitious, sweeping, and full of imaginative details. The first book in the series, Jade City, is around 177,000 words long, and the two subsequent books slightly longer. The series is about as long as the Farseer trilogy, and every bit as good.
The Winds of Winter may never get here. But that doesn't mean you have to go without.
Published between 1984 and 1986, The Fionavar Tapestry pulls a little bit from The Chronicles of Narnia and a litlte bit from The Lord of the Rings. Five students from the University of Toronto are drawn into the magical world of Fionavar, first of all possible worlds. There, they get pulled into a battle with a dark god named Rakoth Maugrim, who threatens not just Fionavar but the worlds beyond.
The stakes in this story are real, which is one of the reasons the trilogy is so popular; not everyone makes it out of this trilogy alive. The writing by Guy Gavriel has a lyrical quality to it, perhaps something he gained while helping edit The Silmarillion, J.R.R. Tolkien's lost Lord of the Ring book. As for length, the trilogy is shorter than either The Farseer Trilogy or The Green Bone Saga.
Published between 2014 and 2019, The LIcanius Trilogy is among the denser series on our list, although it still fits comfortably within the bounds of a trilogy. It takes place years after a war that ended the reign of the Augurs, godlike beings who can manipulate time. Since then, the use of magic has been heavily suppressed. The story kicks off when a student named Davian has the powers of an Augur, and is forced to flee.
That's just to start. We also meet Caeden, an amnesiac who may be the reincarnation of an ancient, immortal entity, as well as other characters. The Licanius Trilogy involves a lot of time travel and asks heavy questions about the nature of free will. It's a puzzle box of a series, where part of the fun is figuring out how the pieces all fit together. The ending wraps things up in a very satisfying way that leaves few loose ends.
We have something shorter on tap next.
The Earthsea books by Ursula K. Le Guin break sidestep a lot of clichés of the genre, mainly the expectation that an epic fantasy story has to be about war. The first book in the series, A Wizard of Earthsea, follows a young man named Ged as he leaves his lonely island to study magic. It's a small-scale, almost cozy character study, and although things can get more intense, most of the other books follow suit.
Moreover, save for the short story collection Tales from Earthsea, none of the five main books in the series exceed 70,000 words in length, meaning they're quick reads.
Many modern, sprawling fantasy book series take inspiration from The Lord of the Rings, J.R.R. Tolkien's classic story about the dark lord Sauron and the brave people of Middle-earth who resist him. But few had Tolkien's gift for brevity. At around 150,000 words per book, these books can't be called short, but they have nothing on The Stormlight Archive or George R.R. Martin's A Song of Ice and Fire series.
And yet, The Lord of the Rings is the single most influential fantasy series of the past hundred years, so maybe length is overrated when it comes to epic fantasy.
You can finish most of these series in a reasonable time, but if you want a fantasy series that will dominate your life for weeks, those exist too.

UPDATED with FAA lifting the ground stop: The FAA’s nationwide ground stoppage has been lifted, but, coming on the eve of one of the entertainment industry’s busiest weekends, the residual effects could affect numerous entertainment industry events as organizers scramble to determine if attendees will be able to make it to the events.

	One of the most high-profile events, the Academy of Motion Picture Arts and Sciences’ 17th Governors Awards, a must-attend for every A-lister in the industry with more than 225 invited guests for this year’s show, is scheduled for Sunday at the Ray Dolby Ballroom at Ovation in Hollywood. It features honorees Glenn Close, Floyd Norman, and Ridley Scott. Killer Films producers Christine Vachon and Pamela Koffler will receive the Irving G. Thalberg Memorial Award. With everyone in town, several events are planned around the Governors Awards, and attendees are scheduled for roundtables, Q&As and other FYC events.

	










	Deadline reached out to the Academy for comment but had yet to hear back.

66,2026-11-14,"Corruption was a top issue for midterm voters this year. How is the White House responding? Like this:
Trump: ""THE DEMOCRATS ARE CORRUPT AND EVIL, and I am the exact opposite! Fight hard, Republicans, or you won’t have a Country left!""
— Aaron Rupar (@atrupar.com) 2026-11-12T12:59:21.534Z
Voters are angry about congressional stock trading and politicians of both parties who listen to billionaire donors rather than ordinary citizens. They're focused on the corrupt activities of President Trump and his allies: the billions made on cryptocurrency, the Jared Kushner diplomacy that's intertwined with dealmaking, and all the rest.
This is not a good time for Republicans to argue that 100% of the political corruption in America is Democratic, and that we shouldn't scrutinize Republicans for corruption at all. Exit polling shows that midterm voters didn't like either party, but they disliked Republicans a lot more. Democrats' favorability/unfavorability among polled voters was 47%/52%; the GOP's numbers were 40%/60%. And voters who said they don't have a favorable opinion of either party -- the so-called double haters, who made up 15% of the electorate -- favored Democrats by a 61%-33% margin.
But that's the approach that makes sense to Trump. His early mentor was Roy Cohn, whose always advised him never to apologize, never to admit error, and always go on offense. And like many other old white boomers, Trump had his brain rewired by Rupert Murdoch's Fox News, which turned its audience into a voluntary sort of North Korea where the news says that the Party is always morally flawless and its enemies are pure evil.
Trump's new chief of staff, Stephen Miller, isn't a boomer, but he's on Fox a lot and he's a child of the right-wing media: he got his start in politics calling in to right-wing talk radio programs, and he was mentored by the far-right attack dog David Horowitz. So it's no surprise that he pursues a ""Democrats are evil, Republicans are blameless"" approach to corruption. The Washington Sun reports:
Stephen Miller was on a quest.
The president’s fierce loyalist, at the time his deputy chief of staff, pressed administration officials for evidence of fraud related to immigrants, Minnesota and the state’s Somali population nine times in a span of about 10 minutes during a December 2025 call, according to a recording obtained by The Washington Sun.
Miller cut one official off as they were describing a fraud problem in Florida, a hot spot for bad actors who siphon off government benefits, according to the government’s own data. He appeared uninterested in the official’s description of fraud when it didn’t prominently feature immigrants.
At one point, Miller was told about an active criminal investigation into a national fraud case out of the Eastern District of Virginia.
“I hear you, but that’s a long way off from Minneapolis,” Miller responded.

Corrupt Somali fraudsters!!!! didn't even work as a GOP selling point this year in Minnesota, where Peggy Flanagan, who is the state's lieutenant governor and thus can be linked to the state's fraud scandal, won her Senate race by 10 points. (Kamala Harris won the state by only 4 points in 2024.)
Trump and Miller seem to imagine that they still have a barricade made up of white heartland voters who uncritically believe the ""Democrats evil, Republicans blameless"" narrative. There clearly are millions of people who continue to believe that, otherwise the blue tsunami would have been even larger. But I think reality is catching up with Republicans -- Trump is doing everything wrong, and even (some) heartland whites get it now. Exit polls said the white vote was split 49%-49% this year. (Pew says that whites voted for Trump over Kamala Harris two years ago by a 55%-43% margin.)
Also, I think Trump and Miller haven't caught on to the fact that the media diet of their target voters is changing. A few years ago, when TV and cable were most Americans' main source of news, Fox dominated the white heartland. It was on in every diner, every bar, every auto repair shop waiting room.
But now people get their news from social media and TikTok, along with streamers and podcasters. The Atlantic's Adam Serwer thinks Elon Musk created a sense of revulsion by turning Twitter into a far-right cesspool:
The Twitter takeover went wrong for conservatives in two ways. The first is that they posted more and more toxic content in an effort to drive engagement. The second is that they had no idea how unpopular that toxic content had become; both influencers and politicians mistook engagement for approval....
If you were spending hours on X, you might have believed that most Americans were delighted by cruel memes celebrating deportations, or that affordability issues were a “hoax,” as President Trump kept insisting, or that a “red wave” was inevitable.
... racist mediocrities ... spent the last few days of the campaign posting memes idolizing the fictional character Anakin Skywalker, one of the main antagonists of Star Wars. One MAGA poster said Skywalker, who becomes a mass murderer after rightly being denied a promotion, was emblematic of “the white male experience.” If you say so!

I don't think this failed to work for Republicans only because it alienated non-Republican X users. I think it failed to work because right-wing content just doesn't dominate the new media environment the way Fox dominates cable news and right-wing Rush Limbaugh clones have long dominated talk radio. Though it seemed for a while that the right dominated new media ...

... many of the largest red dots here belong to bro podcasters who have begun to shun Trump and the right, as have Tucker Carlson and some other ex-Trumpers. And there's a non-Republican narrative breaking through even in the heartland: that the war and tariffs and inflation are bad; that data centers, AI, and Flock cameras are being shoved down our throats; that Trump is corrupt and out of touch; that ICE agents shouldn't be killing people on the streets of America.
But Trump and Miller still think we're in the heyday of right-wing media, when white heartlanders could be persuaded to care about only what Fox and talk radio told them to care about. You know what else Trump is ranting about today?

68,2026-11-14,"Most BI dashboards excel at showing what already happened inside your business, such as revenue by region, conversion by channel, or churn by cohort. What they rarely show is what happened outside while those numbers moved.
For BI teams using alternative data to explain internal metrics, land public web records raw, normalize them into historical snapshots, match external items to internal SKUs, and join on shared dates and regions. Buying feeds reduces maintenance but limits source and refresh control; collecting data yourself provides that control while adding infrastructure responsibilities.
Alternative data fills that gap. Suppose a competitor cut prices on your best sellers, or a rival posted dozens of job ads in a market you thought was quiet, or reviews in your category started mentioning the same defect. None of that lives in your CRM, though plenty of it sits on public websites.
Here is how to bring public web data into the dashboards your team already uses. We will cover picking the right signals, choosing between buying web data as a service or collecting it yourself, modeling the records, and keeping the pipeline trustworthy once it goes live.
Internal data is precise, yet self-referential. Say sales fell by an illustrative 8% in your northern region last month. The dashboard tells you which products and which stores drove the drop, though it usually can’t tell you why external conditions shifted.
Sometimes the reason is internal, like a stock problem or a key account manager leaving. Often the cause sits outside your walls. A competitor ran a promotion, a new player arrived, or search results for your main category shifted. Analysts end up chasing those explanations by hand, opening browser tabs and pasting screenshots into slide decks.
Bringing external signals into the same business intelligence model fixes that workflow. When competitor prices sit on the same time axis as your own sales, the Monday meeting changes. People stop guessing and start comparing.
In investment circles, “alternative data” tends to mean anything beyond filings and market feeds, from satellite images to app downloads. Investment managers spent approximately $2.8 billion on alternative data in 2025 according to Neudata’s market analysis, and in a business intelligence setting the term is looser, covering any external data that helps explain your internal numbers.
Public web data is the most practical slice for most companies. It stays accessible, changes often, and reflects what customers actually see. Common examples include:
None of these feeds need perfection to be useful in day-to-day reporting. They require consistency in collection method and scope. A shift in the trend line should reflect a shift in the market, never a quirk in how the data was collected.
“WDI is used today to extract targeted web content that may indicate product or pricing fluctuations, consumer sentiment about a product or service, and more.. Data on websites is in the public domain and for years has been accessed manually, allowing organizations and individuals to make data-based decisions of all kinds.”
Collecting everything that looks interesting and hoping a use case turns up later is a common trap. It rarely works out. You end up with a lake of competitor pages that nobody queries after the first month.
A better starting point is one recurring decision that people currently make on gut feel. Pricing reviews, regional marketing budgets, hiring plans, and assortment planning are all strong candidates for a first pipeline. Ask the people who own that decision what they want to know first.
Write the question down in plain terms. For example, asking “Are we more expensive than our three main competitors on a sample of 200 core products, by region, each week?” gives you a concrete scope you can design a dataset around. “Keep an eye on the competition” is not.
That single sentence defines the sources, the entities, the geography, and the refresh rate. It also shows you roughly what the dashboard tile should look like before you collect a single page.
Once you know what you need, two broad paths get you there. Buy a feed from a provider who already collects it, or build the scrapers yourself to manage the collection schedule and target sources directly.
Buying moves faster and hands off the day-to-day maintenance. Websites change their layouts regularly, and with a purchased feed someone else fixes the breakage. ProxyEmpire’s explainer on web data as a service covers how these feeds are delivered and what to ask a provider before signing. Delivery by API, scheduled files, or a direct share into your warehouse all fit neatly into a modern BI stack.
Control is the trade-off. You get the vendor’s sources, fields, and refresh schedule, while your competitors can buy the exact same feed. If you need a niche set of retailers, a specific city, or hourly checks, a standard feed might fall short.
Collecting the data yourself gives you that control, along with the job of running the infrastructure. Many teams settle on a hybrid model. They buy the broad, standard data and collect the few sources specific to their market. That split usually mirrors where their competitive edge lies.
Whichever route you take, ask the same baseline questions. Which sources are covered, and which are missing? How often does each field refresh? Can you test a historical sample against pages you check yourself? What does the license allow you to do with the records?
Web data tends to arrive messier than the internal tables your BI team is used to. Prices come as text strings with currency symbols, product names vary between sites, and fields appear or vanish without warning. Applying standard data warehousing discipline fixes the mess.
Land the records raw first. Keep the original payload alongside the collection timestamp and the target location. That location field matters more than people expect, as we will see shortly.
Build a clean layer on top next. Standardize currencies and units, strip formatting, and map everything into a consistent schema. Store observations as snapshots over time instead of overwriting yesterday’s value, because historical trends hold the real value.
Entity matching is usually the hardest step. Suppose your internal catalog lists an item as “Trail Runner 2, Blue, Size 9” while a competitor’s site lists “TR2 Men’s Running Shoe.” Without a reliable mapping between their items and yours, the price comparison falls apart. Barcodes and manufacturer part numbers help when published, while a short manual review queue handles the rest.
Budget real engineering time for matching. A smaller set of well-matched products beats a massive catalog where a chunk of the comparisons are quietly wrong.
To see how a single observation moves from collection to the dashboard, trace one product record across three warehouse layers. First, the scraper or feed lands a raw JSON payload containing the unparsed string ""$119.99"", the competitor product title, the proxy city code, and the UTC timestamp. Second, a transformation job strips the currency symbol, casts the price to a numeric decimal, maps the external URL to your internal SKU via a lookup table, and writes a row into a daily snapshot table keyed by (internal_sku, region_code, snapshot_date). The following SQL query aggregates those normalized competitor snapshots and joins them directly to internal daily sales at the product-region-day grain:
Much of public web data depends on where your request originates. Retail sites show different prices, stock levels, and delivery options by region. Search results differ from city to city. Some sites display a different catalog or currency depending on the visitor’s country.
For a BI team, geography is a core variable. Say your regional sales dashboard compares performance against prices collected from one data center in one country. The comparison can be wrong for every other region without anyone noticing.
Teams collecting their own data usually route requests through proxies with IP addresses in the target markets. Residential proxies with country and city targeting are a common setup for consumer-facing sites when you want to capture the page version a local shopper sees. Whatever setup you use, record the location of every observation directly in the dataset.
When comparing proxy providers for this work, look at targeting depth, success rates, and support quality. Check whether you pay only for successful requests, too. That pricing model keeps costs predictable when certain pages prove hard to reach.
Keep request rates sensible as well. Collecting public pages at a steady, moderate pace respects the target sites and yields cleaner data.
Silent failure is a major risk with web data, often showing up more frequently than a total pipeline crash. When a site changes its layout, a field can start coming back empty while the dashboard keeps drawing a flat line that looks normal at first glance.
Five automated checks help catch these typical pipeline issues early:
Put that freshness timestamp directly on the dashboard. A small “data as of” label next to each external metric prevents plenty of awkward questions and builds user trust by showing when a figure is current or lagging.
External data works best when placed right beside the internal metric it helps explain. A competitor price index next to your conversion rate gives analysts immediate context during reviews. Tucking that same index away on a separate tab makes it easy for stakeholders to overlook.
A few practical design habits help:
Common BI platforms such as Power BI, Tableau, or Looker Studio can display these joined tables using standard chart types once the warehouse layer is modeled. The real work lives in the data model, never the visuals.
Public web data requires clear operational boundaries. According to a 2025 Lowenstein Sandler survey on alternative data, data ownership and privacy (42%) and the time and costs required to vet vendors on the quality, integrity, and reliability of AI systems (40%) rank among the top concerns for organizations. As a general operational practice, limit collection to pages that anyone can view without logging in, avoid bypassing paywalls or access controls, and keep request rates reasonable.
Watch out for anything that identifies individuals. Privacy frameworks such as the GDPR can apply to personal data even when it is publicly visible on the web, so BI teams generally stick to non-personal records like products, prices, companies, and commercial listings.
Certain jurisdictions also have rules regarding databases and creative content. Collecting factual points like a price or stock status can carry different legal considerations than copying an entire catalog of descriptions and images. If you buy data, review the vendor license terms carefully, particularly if you plan to feed the records into machine learning models, and consult legal counsel for your specific sources and markets.
To test this workflow without a massive commitment, keep the initial scope tight. Pick one decision, a few competitors, and a limited product set, then run the pipeline for a couple of months as a trial before expanding.
A practical pilot project looks like this:
Watch how the team uses it from there. When the pricing team starts asking for more competitors or additional regions, you have your validation. If nobody opens the view after an initial trial month, the initial question was probably off target, which is a useful signal to catch early.
In business intelligence, alternative data refers to external market signals collected outside your internal CRM, ERP, and financial databases that help explain why internal metrics changed. Practical BI examples include public web data such as competitor product pricing, stock availability, customer review ratings, regional job postings, and search rankings.
Bring public web data into BI dashboards by first landing raw web payloads with collection timestamps and geographic proxy tags in your data warehouse. Next, normalize currencies and units into a clean snapshot table, match external items to internal SKUs, run automated null and coverage checks, and join the external table to internal metrics on a shared date and regional key.
Internal records will always form the core of your BI stack, while the context behind their movements often sits on public websites your customers browse every day. As Federal Reserve research on alternative data notes, acquiring third-party data and organizing external records alongside internal systems requires dedicated vendor vetting and data modeling effort.
Bringing public web data into your dashboards doesn’t require a moonshot. It takes a sharp question, a reliable collection method, a disciplined data model, and honest labels showing how fresh each figure is. Nail those fundamentals, and your dashboards move past describing history to start explaining it.

69,2026-11-14,"(Photo by Natalie Behring/Getty Images)
A federal power agency is weighing whether to join a California-operated electricity market, but most Westerners polled say their utilities should stay independent.
A draft decision on which day-ahead electricity market the Bonneville Power Administration will join is expected in December, Bonneville senior spokesperson Kevin Wingert said in a statement to the Daily Caller News Foundation. The agency had proposed in March to join a rival to the California market, Markets+.
Seventy-two percent of adults surveyed in five Western media markets said their local utility should stay “independently governed.” Just 10 percent would rather see it coordinate with a “California-run system,” the council’s slides show. The remaining 19 percent said they were not sure.
Causeway Solutions ran the poll for the Western Energy Markets Integrity Council. The advocacy group opposes putting Bonneville’s federal hydropower in what it called a “California-centric trading platform.”
“Pushing these federal assets into the hands of California will have profound adverse consequences for affordability and reliability of power in a region that can ill afford it,” the council said in an Oct. 22 statement to the DCNF.
“The council is an independent not-for-profit organization that receives contributions from power providers and consumers, and individuals. It coordinates with other public interest groups,” Scott Segal, a Bracewell LLP partner speaking for the council, said in a statement. His statement did not name any of those contributors.
“It’s good for California. It’s just bad for everybody else that joins with them,” H. Sterling Burnett, director of the Arthur B. Robinson Center on Climate and Environmental Policy at the Heartland Institute, told the DCNF in a Tuesday interview.
When the DCNF asked who would get power if the whole West ran short, Burnett said, “Well, the big dog eats first.”
He compared it to fights over the Colorado River, saying California gets the most water when there are shortages.
“They have the representatives in the House. They have the biggest population. They get it first,” Burnett said of California.
“California’s disastrous energy policies should serve as a warning to every Western state and no state should allow Sacramento to export its failures across state lines,” Oliver McPherson-Smith, vice chair for energy and environment policy at the America First Policy Institute, said in a statement to the DCNF.
“Customers in other Western states have good reason to resist putting their electricity bills and reliability under the influence of California politicians they didn’t elect and cannot hold accountable,” Jason Isaac, founder and CEO of the American Energy Institute, said in a statement. “Accountability should stay with the people who pay the bills.”
“The Department of Energy is committed to ensuring that American families and businesses, including those across the Pacific Northwest, have access to affordable, reliable, and secure electricity,” a department spokesperson said in a statement to the DCNF.
“Markets+ provides independent governance today,” the Southwest Power Pool, which would run Markets+, said in a statement. “More than one market in the West promotes choice and competition, which enables better outcomes for all participants and states.”
The California Independent System Operator did not respond to the DCNF’s request for comment. (RELATED: Data Center Boom Sparks Battle Over Power Costs, New Regulations)
Independence came out ahead in each market. Support ran from 64 percent in the Billings, Montana, area to 81 percent in the Tri-Cities of Washington.
Those two results carry margins of error of 4.7 points in Billings and 4.9 points in the Tri-Cities, per the slides.
Among the 1,733 people who picked a side, 88 percent chose independence and 12 percent favored coordinating with California.
Eighty-four percent of those surveyed were at least somewhat worried about their electric bills.
Causeway surveyed 2,095 adults, not registered voters, from Sept. 21 to 25 in the Boise, Idaho; Tri-Cities, Washington; Medford, Oregon; Gilbert and Mesa, Arizona; and Billings, Montana, media markets, the council’s polling page says. The text-to-web survey has an overall margin of error of 2.1 percentage points.
Bonneville’s March proposal set a target start date of Oct. 1, 2028, for Markets+. That came after a May 2025 Bonneville policy that put the agency on a path toward Markets+.
That plan is now under review. Bonneville Administrator and CEO Travis Kavulla wrote in a Sept. 3 letter that he had committed to reviewing that course “with fresh eyes.”
He would propose a change in Bonneville’s next draft decision if the agency sees “concrete actions” on governance of the California operator’s market, he wrote. Independent board leadership topped his list.
California revised its laws in 2025 so a separate regional body could govern that market with a board “appointed through an independent process rather than by state actors,” Kavulla noted.
That body, the Regional Organization for Western Energy, is set to interview candidates for its first CEO in December, RTO Insider reported Nov. 11.
“A more unified grid in the West with greater scale, market depth, and more contiguous corridors may alleviate operational and economic risks that would persist in a more fragmented market footprint,” Wingert, Bonneville’s senior spokesperson said. (RELATED: ‘Whole ’Nother Rent’: Soaring Electricity Prices In Kamala Harris’ Home State Are Crushing Residents)
Size is the draw. Leaving out Bonneville’s balancing area, the California operator’s market averaged 39 gigawatts of net generation, against 19 for Markets+. Kavulla cited federal Energy Information Administration data.
The Southwest Power Pool’s website lists an address in Little Rock, Arkansas.
Lawmakers from the Southwest Power Pool’s home state want Bonneville to stay the course. Republican Arkansas Sens. Tom Cotton and John Boozman wrote to Kavulla on Sept. 25 backing Bonneville’s “timely participation” in Markets+.
“The importance of independent governance cannot be overstated,” they wrote. The senators added that the California operator “is and remains statutorily beholden to the interests of the State of California.”
Western Montana’s electric co-op managers said in an Oct. 1 letter to Kavulla that their co-ops “strongly favor SPP.”
Advanced Energy United, which represents clean energy businesses, backed Bonneville’s review in September. The group’s senior director, Brian Turner, said the agency “recognizes the important strides that have been made around governance,” Utility Dive reported.
Bonneville has a workshop on its choice scheduled for Nov. 19, Wingert said.
Kavulla asked the regional body to adopt a framework that lets market participants “initiate and evaluate major market proposals,” with the end of November as his target.

As Brewers manager Pat Murphy walked to the stage at Tropicana Field after winning the World Series, he turned to his head of baseball operations to deliver a message. 
""Hey, I have some thoughts for spring training in ‘27,’"" Matt Arnold recalled Murphy telling him. ""I’m like, ‘Not right now. Now’s not the time.’""
Amid the revelry, Arnold wanted to take a moment to absorb the present before considering the future. But that time has come now, especially with one of the Brewers’ top sluggers already set to wear a new uniform in 2027. 
Jake Bauers signed a multi-year deal with the Red Sox. (Photo by Stacy Revere/Getty Images) 
This week’s GM meetings laid the groundwork for what could be a flurry of activity before the collective bargaining agreement ends on the night of Dec. 1. As Major League executives departed Las Vegas on Thursday, the Braves struck a three-year deal for longtime Dodgers reliever Evan Phillips, who returns to the team that drafted him in 2015. Less than 24 hours later, the Red Sox made the biggest move in free agency to this point, signing NLCS MVP Jake Bauers.
Boston found the power bat in Bauers, who declined his qualifying offer in Milwaukee and agreed to a four-year, $58 million deal with the Red Sox. Perhaps that catalyzes the market. As of now, nearly every free agent in our Top 50 is still available. 
Here are 10 of the top players on the market, along with potential landing spots for each. 
Why He's Ranked Here: Skubal is in a clear tier by himself in this year’s otherwise light free-agent class.
His agent, Scott Boras, will look to make him the highest-paid pitcher in the sport’s history, which would require a team surpassing the 12-year, $325 million contract the Dodgers gave to Yoshinobu Yamamoto. 
It’s no surprise, then, why the New York Mets are considered the favorites to land him. While there will only be a handful of teams willing to shop at this level of the market, it does sound like one of them might pounce before a potential lockout. 
Why He's Ranked Here: King opted out after the first year of his three-year, $75 million contract with the Padres to become a free agent. Clearly, the former reliever would like another multi-year offer after producing a 3.15 ERA over three seasons as a full-time starter in San Diego. His strikeout rate dipped, and his walk rate climbed last year while throwing a career-high 185 innings, but he no longer has a qualifying offer attached to him the way he did last winter. That should make him an especially appealing target on the open market and could yield him a bigger deal than he received last winter. 
Still, a return to San Diego remains a possibility. His impact on the Padres in October was immeasurable. King allowed one run in 14 postseason innings and was fearless, picking up a save in Game 3 of the NLDS with two scoreless innings of relief just two days after starting Game 2. 
Why He's Ranked Here: Arozarena has produced at least 20 homers and 20 steals in six straight seasons, an especially noteworthy feat considering his last 2 ½ years have been spent in Seattle. As Boras put it: ""He’s a model of consistency, and he’s done it in a market where right-handed hitters go to die."" 
Indeed, Arozarena was one of the only bright spots in the Mariners’ disappointing lineup, producing an .865 OPS that represented his highest mark over the course of a full season in his eight-year career. In a market with few right-handed offensive threats, it’ll be interesting to see whether teams value Arozarena or Seiya Suzuki as the better option as both outfield sluggers enter their age-32 seasons. Each player has a qualifying offer attached. 
Why He's Ranked Here: Overshadowed by MVP Pete Crow-Armstrong in the Cubs’ outfield, Suzuki just put together the most valuable season of his career (5.1 bWAR). It was also his fourth straight season with at least 20 home runs and an OPS over .800. Since joining the Cubs in 2022, Suzuki ranks 12th in MLB in both OPS and wRC+ among all qualified right-handed hitters. 
For teams seeking a right-handed slugger who won’t hurt them defensively in a corner, he might be the most alluring outfielder on the market. Suzuki’s five outs above average and 14 defensive runs saved last season both represented career highs. Can he replicate that level of defense as he gets deeper into his 30s? 
Why He's Ranked Here: Sato’s aggressiveness at the plate and questions defensively bring risk, but we’re also talking about a 27-year-old who might be the best bat on the market. He launched 40 home runs in 2025 and another 39 in 2026 for NPB’s Hanshin Tigers. His 79 homers over the last two seasons were 15 more than the next-closest NPB player, and he also won the Triple Crown last season. 
There will be plenty of teams willing to live with an elevated strikeout rate if the lefty slugger can maintain that power in his move to MLB. Sato may ultimately have to bump from third base to first base or, more likely, a corner outfield spot. But don’t be surprised if he ends up the highest-paid position player in this year’s class. 
Why He's Ranked Here: Lowe’s contract year in Pittsburgh couldn’t have gone much better. He led all MLB second basemen with 35 home runs, though the power was nothing new. He has hit at least 30 homers three times in his nine-year career, has hit at least 20 homers each of the last four seasons and has been an above-league-average hitter every season. The bigger changes were his defense and durability. 
Known as a bat-first infielder, Lowe is coming off the best defensive season of his career. He went from minus-14 defensive runs saved and outs above average with the Rays in 2025 to plus-13 and plus-15 in those categories, respectively, in his lone season with the Pirates in 2026. He also played in a career-high 150 games. Teams will have to weigh how much they believe in his improved health and defense as he enters his age-32 season. Either way, second basemen with this kind of power don’t become available often. 
Why He's Ranked Here: Arraez has hit fewer than 10 home runs in seven of his first eight seasons, but his bat-to-ball skills have made him an above league-average hitter every year of his career and a four-time All-Star. Last year, he hit .310 — even after a disappointing finish to the season following a trade from San Francisco to Philadelphia — and came a point away from winning the NL batting crown. 
More surprisingly, though, he also produced the best defensive season of his career after shifting from first base back to second, where the 29-year-old graded out as a top-five defensive player at his position by outs above average. As teams weigh the sustainability of that dramatic defensive improvement, he at least provided reason to believe he could be more than just an elite contact hitter. 
Why He's Ranked Here: For a while, Peralta’s walk year looked like a disaster. He had a 4.99 ERA in 22 starts with the Mets before finding refuge in Tampa Bay, where he made some tweaks, found his fastball again and gained his confidence back. Peralta had a 3.25 ERA in 10 starts for the Rays, including a 0.79 ERA over his final six starts of the regular season, before producing a 2.95 ERA over five playoff appearances. 
Overall, Peralta’s 21.4% strikeout rate last year was the lowest of his career, but his underlying numbers were better than his 4.42 ERA demonstrated, and his late-season uptick combined with a history of production could make the 30-year-old the most desirable starter on the market behind Skubal. 
Why He's Ranked Here: Over the last four seasons, Jeffers’ .782 OPS and 119 wRC+ (19% better than league average) rank eighth among all primary catchers. Last year, his .814 OPS was the second-highest mark of his career and ranked fourth among all catchers with at least 300 plate appearances. 
You have to decrease the minimum because he only played in 92 games after fracturing his hamate bone. Staying on the field has been an issue for the 29-year-old, who has never played more than 122 games in a single season, and he’s an average defender at best. Still, his bat makes him the clear top catcher on the market and should generate plenty of interest. The Twins gave him the qualifying offer for a reason. 
Prediction: Minnesota Twins — They decided not to trade him at the deadline; best not to let him walk now, even if he declines the qualifying offer, considering the lack of depth behind him. 
Why He's Ranked Here: The veteran ground-ball extraordinaire looked like an All-Star through his first nine starts last year, producing a 2.39 ERA for the Mets before fracturing his fibula. He was traded to the Cubs at the deadline and looked terrific in August (1.55 ERA in five starts) before falling apart in September (8.24 ERA) with as many walks (15) as strikeouts (15) over his final four starts of the season. 
While Holmes was limited to 101.1 innings last season, the former All-Star closer has demonstrated his worthiness as a starter, producing a 3.44 ERA over the last two seasons after converting from the bullpen. His ability to pitch in either role should be enticing to any playoff contender. With the highest ground-ball rate of any starter in MLB last season (60.7%), Holmes is best suited somewhere with a good defensive infield behind him. 
Prediction: Chicago Cubs — It's fair to wonder if his late-season struggles in Chicago — and the fact that the Cubs didn’t pitch him in the wild-card series — might impact his willingness to return. Still, the Cubs’ elite defense and multiple needs in the rotation make Chicago a logical fit.
