MEGA, the organization behind Ethereum layer-2 network MegaETH, has reduced its workforce by 20% following months of restructuring as it expands into artificial intelligence products. 
On Friday, MegaETH co-founder Shuyao Kong said the organization had reorganized its teams, resulting in the departures of staff who worked on its blockchain, wallet, ecosystem support and communications. Kong did not disclose the number of employees affected or when the layoffs occurred. She said MEGA’s founders, early employees and core engineering team remain committed to the organization.
“We’ve brought together new capabilities and reorganized our teams, a process that has resulted in a 20% reduction in our staff,” Kong said. “These are people who helped build MEGA from its earliest days.”
The workforce reduction comes as MEGA shifts its focus toward developing products on the MegaETH network, including artificial intelligence services. The organization has committed $30 million to the initiative, although Kong did not directly link the layoffs to its new strategy.
In a whitepaper, MegaETH co-founders Yilong Li and Lei Yang outlined a proposed inference market aimed at giving users access to competing AI service providers. Their approach seeks to address concerns about whether providers use the advertised models and how they handle customer data.
Kong said MEGA plans to launch a public inference market in December.

Interactives
Interactive reads from the businessline desk
Indian equities seem to have rediscovered their mojo. Foreign institutional investors (FIIs) have returned with their cheque books, helping the Nifty 50 index reclaim the 25,000 mark on November 6 after 168 consecutive trading sessions below that level. Suddenly, Dalal Street has plenty of reasons to celebrate.
But the market party still has several empty chairs. Although the Nifty 50 has returned to its late-February levels, more than half its constituents are yet to make the same journey. The benchmark may have arrived, but many of its passengers seem to have missed the train.
A bl.portfolio analysis of 737 stocks across the Nifty 100, Nifty Midcap 150, Nifty Smallcap 250 and Nifty Microcap 250, comparing adjusted closing prices on February 27 and November 12, reveals an uneven recovery.
This universe accounts for nearly 90 per cent of the market capitalisation of NSE-listed companies. While 55.8 per cent of the stocks have moved above their late-February levels, the remaining 44.2 per cent are still underwater. The differences across market-cap segments, sectors and individual stocks are even more striking. Read on.
The Nifty 50 closed at 25,299 on November 12, just 0.5 per cent above its February 27 level of 25,179. But only 44 per cent of its constituents had recovered their February closing prices.
The average return of the Nifty 50 stocks was a positive 1.9 per cent, while the median return was a negative 2.7 per cent. In other words, the typical Nifty 50 constituent was still in the red, notwithstanding the benchmark’s comeback.
A simple average of returns shows how stocks performed collectively, while median return reflects the typical stock’s experience. A much higher average than median suggests that a few big winners are lifting the overall performance, masking weaker returns for most stocks.
The Nifty 100 tells a similar story. Despite gaining 1.4 per cent, fewer than half its analysed constituents were above their February levels. Its median stock return was a negative 2.4 per cent.
Some heavyweights have certainly enjoyed the celebrations. Eternal gained nearly 48 per cent, while BSE and Tech Mahindra rose 44 per cent and 31 per cent, respectively. Adani Ports and Adani Enterprises gained around 27 per cent each.
But there were plenty of reluctant participants. Tata Motors Passenger Vehicles declined 22 per cent, ONGC nearly 20 per cent, HDFC Life 16 per cent and Hindustan Unilever nearly 16 per cent.
The Nifty Midcap 150 fared somewhat better at the index level, gaining 4.2 per cent. Yet, 54 per cent of its constituents remained below their February levels. Its median stock return was also marginally negative.
Interestingly, the smaller companies have been hosting the livelier parties.
The Nifty Smallcap 250 gained 13.3 per cent between February 27 and November 12, while the Nifty Microcap 250 surged 21.5 per cent. More importantly, these gains were accompanied by stronger participation among individual stocks.
Nearly 57 per cent of the analysed small-cap constituents had recovered their February levels. Among micro-caps, the proportion was higher at almost 64 per cent.
Their median stock returns were positive too, at 3.9 per cent (small-cap) and 9.6 per cent (micro-cap). This contrasts with the negative medians seen among Nifty 50 and mid-cap constituents.
However, the smaller-stock party has its own share of winners and wallflowers.
Among mid-caps, Laurus Labs more than doubled, while One 97 Communications, Radico Khaitan and Kalyan Jewellers gained more than 70 per cent each. Coforge also rose nearly 72 per cent. At the opposite end, PI Industries fell 44 per cent, Swiggy 36 per cent and Rail Vikas Nigam 35 per cent.
Small-caps displayed even wider differences. Cupid and Sterlite Technologies surged 373 per cent and 347 per cent, respectively, while HFCL gained 207 per cent. Meanwhile, KEC International lost nearly 42 per cent, Avanti Feeds 41 per cent and KPIT Technologies nearly 28 per cent.
Micro-caps were no less dramatic. Sigma Advanced System surged 448 per cent, while Shilpa Medicare and Sky Gold & Diamonds gained around 194 per cent and 191 per cent, respectively. In contrast, Aditya Birla Fashion, Sharda Cropchem and Latent View Analytics suffered declines of around 37-41 per cent.
The contrasts become sharper when stocks are grouped by sector rather than market capitalisation.
Healthcare, pharmaceuticals, chemicals, and aerospace and defence have emerged as some of the strongest pockets. About 85 per cent of healthcare stocks and nearly 80 per cent of pharmaceutical stocks have recovered their February levels. Their median returns were 15.6 per cent and 24.5 per cent, respectively.
Aerospace and defence stocks also displayed strong participation, with nearly 74 per cent trading above February levels and a median gain of almost 32 per cent. Chemicals and electrical capital goods registered recovery rates of approximately 76 per cent and 73 per cent.
This suggests that their strong averages are supported by gains across a substantial proportion of constituent stocks.
Other sectors have not been nearly as fortunate.
Only 20 per cent of cement stocks recovered their February prices. Their median return was a negative 13.8 per cent. Insurance fared little better, with a recovery rate of 30 per cent and a median decline of 9.1 per cent.
Infrastructure developers and power generation companies also struggled, with fewer than four in 10n stocks recovering their February levels.
FMCG offers a particularly interesting example. Its average constituent return was a positive 12.3 per cent, suggesting a fairly healthy performance. But nearly 59 per cent of FMCG stocks remained underwater, and the median return was a negative 4.9 per cent. The explanation lies partly in exceptionally-strong individual performers, such as Cupid, whose huge gain lifts the sector average. A few stars can make the entire class look brilliant, even when most students have struggled.
IT software presents another mixed picture. Its average stock return was a positive 8.3 per cent, but fewer than half its constituents recovered. The median return was marginally negative. Moreover, mid-cap IT stocks performed considerably better than many of their large-cap and micro-cap counterparts.
The sector divergence also reflects differences in business prospects. Pharmaceutical stocks, particularly contract development and manufacturing companies, have benefitted from improving earnings expectations. In contrast, traditional FMCG companies have faced pressure from rising input costs and concerns over profitability.
Cement makers have faced a similar problem, with higher fuel and freight costs eating into profitability even as volumes improved. Competition and capacity additions have also restricted pricing power. Power companies, meanwhile, have faced coal-supply disruptions despite robust electricity demand. These differences help explain why investors have rewarded some businesses while remaining cautious about others.
The broad numbers highlight another important distinction between market returns and individual stock performance.
Across the 737-stock universe, the average return was 14.4 per cent, but the median was only 4.2 per cent. That wide gap suggests that exceptional winners have pulled the average substantially above the experience of the typical stock.
The distribution of returns makes this clearer. Nearly 32 per cent of the stocks gained at least 20 per cent, while about 27 per cent lost at least 10 per cent. Almost 11 per cent suffered declines of 20 per cent or more.
Thus, two investors entering the market at the same time could have very different stories to tell, depending on the stocks they owned.
For investors, the lesson is not that the rally is unreliable or that smaller stocks will necessarily continue outperforming. Rather, headline index levels offer only a partial picture of market health. How much this rally broadens across the market is the important factor to monitor.

73,2026-11-14,"How much of the US economy is like a meme stock?
Empty? Worthless? Fake? Motley Fool:
Nvidia stock has wiped out $1.7 trillion in value since October…
Nvidia’s business hasn’t changed. Same chips. Same customers. Same profits. How could it be worth $1.7 trillion less? And could it shed another $1.7 trillion?
Without the hype...and the frenzy...how much is Nvidia really worth? And how much is it just pumped up...inflated by the AI capex boom and phony credit money?
We’ve pointed out before that, according to the famous Buffett metric, comparing stock prices to GDP output, there’s about $40 trillion in US listed stocks that never dirties its hand. It toils not. Neither does it spin. Like memes, these stocks can be traded for goods and services. But like memes, their values can also wilt like a lily at a dead man’s wake.
The math is simple enough. Up until around 2013 there was almost always at least $1 of output behind each $1 of stock value. Not until mid-ways through the Obama years did stock values go decisively and permanently above 100% of GDP. And now they’re about 231% of GDP. Which is to say, stock prices now float high above their real worth. About $40 trillion above, by our back-of-the-envelope calculation.

Let’s begin by reminding ourselves that stocks are evidence of ownership in what are supposed to be profit-making companies. Those profits are the difference between the value of goods and services produced and the cost of producing them.
If the costs are greater than the value of the output, the company is losing money...destroying wealth. Logically, the shareholders should participate in the losses as well as the gains. Sometimes they do.
So, here we have Opendoor Technologies. It is the brainchild of some fellows who thought they could make the housing market more fluid by putting it online. They buy houses online...send out crews to fix them up...and then resell them. In 2021, for example, they bought 37,000 houses. A year later, they sold 7,500 of them at an average loss of $28,000 on each one.
We’ve fixed up a number of houses. If someone had asked us about the Opendoor business plan, we would have said that it would never work. You can buy a house carefully, fix it up frugally, and sell it sensibly. And maybe you’ll make money. But you can’t do it at scale. Because the good deals go to the local guys, who know their trade, have the local knowledge, and often do much of the work themselves. And those guys, who are successful locally, will scale up as much as they can.
“Opendoor will lose money,” we would have predicted. But we never would have guessed that it would lose so much. It lost $339 million in 2019, $253 million in 2020, $662 million in 2021...and on and on, year after year, up to a loss of $1.3 billion last year.
Total losses are up to $5.1 billion.
How much is a company like that really worth? Back in 2021, it was valued at $18 billion, $28 a share. But in terms of what it was really worth — a serial wealth-destroyer on a grand scale — its value was always negative. And now the share price has dropped down to near $1; more than $17 billion of fake value has been wiped out. Wall Street Psychology:
The irony of flipping houses in a 7% mortgage environment while claiming to be an AI company shouldn’t be lost on anyone.
Opendoor is a gaudy example. But how much of Nvidia is unsupported by real output? Apple? Microsoft? These are real companies with sales and profits. But all through the economy, sales and profits — and losses — have been flattered by inputs of ‘money’ that isn’t real money.
As Tom has explained, it is actually debt. Debt is not the same as money. You can pile up all the money you want. You can even lend it out and receive interest on it. But pile up debt and you’ll be the one paying interest. Then, more debt is needed just to pay the interest on the old debt. And whee! You are on your way to hyperinflation.
Individual companies or memes can boom and bust...creating false value and then destroying it...all good fun. But when an entire economy does it, the fun stops. People believe they have real wealth. They expect to live on it...to retire on it...to use it to pay mortgages and tuition...and to fund the feds’ retirement and medical programs.
A few years ago, the feds inflated the economy for pleasure — it gave them a boost, like a glass of wine before dinner. Now, they inflate it to avoid the pain of a correction hangover.
Time to close the door.

Oil is falling for now, but the war’s not close to over.

Canova’s sculptures look like priceless works of art. They also reveal an older financial trick…"

Every Black Friday I tell myself I’m shopping for other people, and every Black Friday a knife somehow ends up in my own pocket. This year I’m making it easier on all of us. WE Knife Co., the company behind WE Knife, CIVIVI, and SENCUT, is running its holiday sale with up to 30% off across all three brands (plus free gifts with purchases). I’ve picked seven knives from that lineup, ranging from a $40 award winner to a $185 titanium flagship. Whether you’re gifting or “gifting,” there’s something here for every pocket and every budget.
SENCUT holds down the entry level, CIVIVI covers the $60 to $100 sweet spot most of us actually carry, and WE sits up top with titanium, MagnaCut steel, and machining that borders on showing off. A brand-new CIVIVI Elite tier now splits the difference between those last two. The Elementum shows up twice on this list, once as a $65 CIVIVI and once as a $185 WE, which makes for a fun lesson in what extra money actually buys you. Three of these designs have BLADE Show awards to their name, so the list comes pre-vetted. Grab a coffee and maybe your wallet, because we’re going from fanciest to friendliest.


The WE Elementum takes CIVIVI’s best-selling silhouette and rebuilds it out of materials that would make a gear nerd weep a little. The blade is CPM MagnaCut, which makes this XA series the first WE knife ever to use the steel. MagnaCut was designed by metallurgist Larrin Thomas to solve an old trade-off, using niobium and vanadium carbides for edge-holding while keeping chromium free to fight rust. In plain English, it stays sharp, shrugs off sweat and humidity, and handles abuse without chipping. Here it’s cut into a 2.96-inch drop point with a flat grind and a black stonewashed finish, which hides scratches better than I hide my Amazon orders.
Bronze and black 6AL4V titanium forms the handle, delivering a lightweight, strong, and highly corrosion-resistant construction. WE machines the scales with a 3D topographic pattern inspired by geological layers, so the knife looks like a trail map while the ridges give your fingers some bite. That same titanium doubles as the frame lock, which clicks into place behind the blade after the flipper sends it out on caged ceramic bearings. The whole thing weighs 2.39 ounces (67.8g), about as much as three AA batteries, and closes to a pocket-friendly 4.02 inches (102.1mm). A reversible titanium clip means lefties get invited to the party too.
Click Here to Buy Now: $184.96 $217.60 (15% off). Hurry, deal ends December 22nd!

WE built its name on flippers and frame locks, so it was a fun plot twist when its Notchline hunting knife won Factory Imported Fixed Blade of the Year at BLADE Show 2026. The 3.45-inch (87.7mm) straight back blade is sized for field dressing, where control matters more than reach. It’s stonewashed CPM S35VN, a powder steel that holds an edge through a long day of skinning and still sharpens without a fight. At 0.17 inches (4.2mm) thick, the stout spine can work around joints, while the flat grind keeps cuts clean. Spine jimping gives your thumb a place to park when you choke up for detail work like caping.
Milled rose carbon fiber covers the full tang, machined with swirling, petal-like patterns that give your fingers real traction. The handle runs 4.05 inches (102.9mm) long and 0.71 inches (18mm) thick, chunky enough to fill your palm properly when your hands are cold or slick in the field. Everything weighs 3.98 ounces (112.8g), almost exactly a stick of butter, which feels appropriate for something this good at cutting. It rides in a black Kydex sheath with a T-Clip designed by knifemaker Bob Terzuola, so it mounts on a belt, a pack strap, or anything with webbing. A paracord lanyard tied through the tang makes it easier to draw from the sheath, even with gloves on.
Click Here to Buy Now: $157.50 $210.00 (25% off). Hurry, deal ends December 22nd!


The CIVIVI Elementum shares its 2.96-inch drop point profile with the WE version earlier on this list, but it spends its budget on fun instead of titanium. Under the polished yellow Ultem scale, CIVIVI machined a little labyrinth with a ceramic ball rolling around inside it. Tilt the knife and you can steer the ball through the maze, exactly like those handheld puzzle toys from birthday party goodie bags. Ultem is a translucent, naturally amber plastic used in aircraft interiors and sterilizable medical gear, so pocket life won’t faze it. It also lets the stainless steel liners show through, giving the whole knife a honey-colored glow.
The blade is German Nitro-V, a stainless steel with added nitrogen for better corrosion resistance and toughness, hardened to 59 to 61 HRC. A satin finish and a machined rock-grind pattern give it the look of a hand-hammered blade, minus anyone actually swinging a hammer. A hollow grind makes quick work of cardboard, tape, and apple slices. The flipper runs on caged ceramic bearings and settles into a stainless liner lock, while spine jimping helps on tougher cuts. At 2.98 ounces (84.5g) and 4.03 inches (102.4mm) closed, it actually weighs a little more than its titanium sibling, which is the Ultem and steel liners talking.
Click Here to Buy Now: $65.25 $87.00 (25% off). Hurry, deal ends December 22nd!


Zac Whitmore, the EDC reviewer known online as Zac in the Wild, designed the original CIVIVI Yonder, and it won Best Buy of the Year at BLADE Show 2024. This Clip Point version keeps that compact, comfortable frame and swaps in a 2.88-inch (73.1mm) clip point blade, the shape you’ll find on plenty of classic pocketknives. The clipped spine drops down to a fine, sharp tip that’s great for detail work like opening blister packs or picking out splinters. It’s ground from Swedish 14C28N, a stainless steel that takes a very keen edge and stands up well to rust. At just 0.09 inches (2.2mm) thick with a flat grind, it slices like a good paring knife.
The handle pairs Guibourtia, a reddish African hardwood with dark streaks running through it, with stonewashed brass bolsters up front. Brass picks up a patina the more you handle it, so after a few months your Yonder will look like nobody else’s. The crossbar lock runs through both sides of the handle, so lefties and righties release it with the same thumb pull. Dual thumb studs on a caged ceramic bearing pivot get the blade open fast, and a reversible deep-carry clip keeps it low in the pocket. At about 3.3 ounces (93g) and 3.74 inches (95.1mm) closed, it’s small enough to pull out at Thanksgiving dinner without anyone flinching.
Click Here to Buy Now: $70.50 $94.00 (25% off). Hurry, deal ends December 22nd!


Ostap Hel designed the CIVIVI Purr to look like a cat when it’s closed, with ears, eyes, and a tail tucked into the outline of the handle. The pivot and hardware sit where the eyes should be, and an engraved kitty face finishes the joke. This black and white G10 version reads like a tuxedo cat, which might be the most dignified a pocketknife has ever looked. Ostap has a soft spot for fidget-friendly mechanics, so the Purr gets two flipper tabs, a front flipper and a traditional rear one. Both ride on caged ceramic bearings, which means you’ll be opening and closing it through every meeting you sit through.
Instead of a lock, the Purr uses a double detent slip joint, where spring tension holds the blade in both the closed and open positions. It snaps open with a crisp click and stays put for everyday cutting, though firm pressure can fold it, so mind your fingers. That non-locking design, plus a 1.83-inch (46.5mm) blade, makes it friendly to stricter carry laws, including the UK’s rules for blades under 3 inches. The reverse tanto blade is stonewashed Nitro-V, with a straight edge and a stout tip for packages and string. At 1.48 ounces (42g) and 2.89 inches (73.5mm) closed, it weighs less than a golf ball and comes with a lanyard for keychain carry.
Click Here to Buy Now: $44.03 $58.70 (25% off). Hurry, deal ends December 22nd!

The Brova is the first knife in CIVIVI Elite, a new line that sits between regular CIVIVI and WE in both price and materials. It also debuts a lock CIVIVI had never used before, called the Top Liner Lock. A regular liner lock asks you to push a spring bar sideways from inside the handle, which puts your thumb right where the blade wants to close. Here the locking leaf sits along the spine, and you release it by pressing a textured button on top of the handle. Your fingers stay on the outside, the blade swings shut, and the whole thing feels as easy as clicking a pen.
Gray aluminum scales with chamfered edges and a sculpted, faux-inlay design give the handle a look that’s a step up from typical CIVIVI G10. Blue thumb studs and hardware add a pop of color, and the dual studs flick the blade open on caged ceramic bearings. The blade is a 3.41-inch (86.5mm) clip point in black stonewashed S35VN. At 8.08 inches (205.2mm) open and 4.67 inches (118.7mm) closed, it’s the largest folder on this list. It weighs 3.78 ounces (107g), a little more than a deck of cards, and a reversible deep-carry clip keeps it riding low.
Click Here to Buy Now: $108.75 $145.00 (25% off). Hurry, deal ends December 22nd!


SENCUT is the youngest brand in the WE Knife Co. family, and it debuted in 2022 with the Sachse. That same year, the Sachse won Best Buy of the Year at BLADE Show, which is a pretty strong first impression for a brand-new name. Sachse is German for “Saxon,” and the knife has a sturdy, practical build to match. It opens three ways: a flipper tab, dual thumb studs, or pressing the button and letting the blade swing out on its caged ceramic bearing. SENCUT tuned the spring under the button so it resists accidental opening in your pocket while still releasing with a light press.
The 3.47-inch (88.2mm) drop point is satin-finished 9Cr18MoV, a high-carbon stainless that takes a fine edge and comes back quickly on a sharpener. With a flat grind and 0.12-inch (3mm) stock, it handles mail, meal prep, and the zip ties on new holiday gadgets. Contoured ivory G10 scales give it a solid grip, and the light color is easy to spot if you drop it in leaves. It weighs 3.90 ounces (110.6g) and closes to 4.34 inches (110.2mm), with a reversible tip-up clip for either hand. For about $40, it rides on the same style of caged ceramic bearing pivot as the $185 WE Elementum, and that shared DNA runs through this entire family.

SamMobile has affiliate and sponsored partnerships, we may earn a commission.
It integrates games to One UI stock apps.
Reading time: 2 minutes
Abhijeet Mishra / SamMobile
Samsung Galaxy S26 FE
Samsung is preparing several new features for One UI 9.5, some of which have leaked over the past few weeks. Now, we have come across a new agentic AI feature that lets you keep up with your favorite titles and in-game friends without actually opening a game.

PlayOn Agent is a system-level AI companion developed by Samsung to bridge the gap between mobile games and stock One UI apps. Rather than keeping interactions, achievements, events, rewards, and friend lists locked inside individual game clients, the new feature pulls them directly into your daily apps.

For example, you won't need to launch a game just to check messages from friends and teammates. PlayOn Agent brings in-game chat directly into Samsung Messages, letting you reply to messages and coordinate raid plans even when the game is closed.

It also syncs and stores your gaming friends in Samsung Contacts, so you can reach out to them without needing their personal phone numbers. The Contacts app will have a dedicated PlayOn section.

Asif Iqbal Shaik / SamMobile
                                            
Introduction screen for the PlayOn feature

Asif Iqbal Shaik / SamMobile
                                            
PlayOn brings in-game messages to the Messages app on Galaxy phones

PlayOn Agent integrates with Samsung's Now Brief as well, bringing live game data into easy-to-read cards. It surfaces in-game events, limited-time login rewards, daily quests, and personalized updates right on your lock screen and home screen widgets.

Asif Iqbal Shaik / SamMobile
                                            
Introduction of PlayOn's Calendar, Messages, and Now Brief integration
                                                                                
Asif Iqbal Shaik / SamMobile
                                            
In-game events and rewards will show up in Now Brief via PlayOn
                                                                                
Finally, if you juggle multiple gaming accounts, PlayOn Agent lets you link several titles and profiles under one hub and choose which in-game character is active for each game.

You can check PlayOn's introduction in our video below.

A high-end gaming PC without the assembly hassle.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Building a gaming PC is expensive, especially with ongoing price inflation for memory, storage, and graphics cards. Putting together a balanced system with a high-end gaming CPU and a capable graphics card can quickly push budgets beyond what many enthusiasts are willing to spend. If you'd rather skip the hassle of sourcing individual components, we've found a compelling deal on a CyberPowerPC prebuilt gaming desktop, currently available at Best Buy and eBay for $1,749.99. That's a substantial $550 discount off its original price of $2,299.99.
The prebuilt system is powered by AMD’s Ryzen 7 9800X3D, which is regarded as the gold standard for gaming PCs. This 8-core, 16-thread chip may not be the fastest desktop CPU, but thanks to its 3D V-Cache, it reduces how often your CPU has to fall back on system RAM, drastically improving frame rates while gaming. It also has a modest power draw, making it our pick for the best gaming CPU on the market.
The CPU is paired with an AMD B650 M-ATX motherboard that offers built-in Wi-Fi 6 and Bluetooth, gigabit Ethernet, and multiple USB ports. For memory, there's 32GB of dual-channel DDR5-5600 RAM along with a 1TB SSD for storage. There are also additional M.2 and SATA ports for future storage expansion.
The CyberPowerPC desktop combines AMD's Ryzen 7 9800X3D with a Radeon RX 9070 featuring 16GB of VRAM, alongside 32GB of DDR5 memory and a 1TB SSD, making it a capable option for 1440p and 4K gaming.
Powering the graphics is AMD’s Radeon RX 9070, featuring RDNA 4 architecture, 16GB of GDDR6 VRAM, and 56 Compute Units. The GPU can handle 1440p and 4K gaming and has a rated TDP of 220W. As per our 16-game rasterization geomean, the RX 9070 averages 86.6 FPS at 1440p Ultra settings and 52.2 FPS at 4K Ultra settings.
View Original
View Original
View Original
View Original
The system is built inside a premium mid-tower chassis with dual USB Type-A and Type-C ports at the front and an RGB LED strip running across the front and side glass panels. It also includes a 360mm AIO liquid cooler, five additional ARGB case fans, an 850W power supply unit, and an RGB keyboard and mouse bundle.
At its discounted price of $1,749.99, this CyberPowerPC prebuilt offers great value for anyone looking to purchase a high-end gaming system. The deal is currently available at Best Buy, but you can also buy it for the same price on eBay.

Topic:Work
Lachlan Riddell says he has applied for hundreds of jobs over the past three years. (ABC News: Kate Nickels)
Economists are watching to see if youth unemployment rises further as the economy softens.
Young people are increasingly competing with multiple job holders for work.
University students are being encouraged to engage with the job market well before they graduate.
Young people struggling to find work can be the canary in the coal mine for the rest of the jobs market.
And a recent rise in the youth unemployment rate could be a sign of things to come.
Australian Bureau of Statistics data from September showed youth unemployment at 11.1 per cent compared with the overall unemployment rate of 4.7 per cent.
While still lower than before the pandemic, a slowing economy could mean youth unemployment rises further in the coming months.
""We tend to see younger workers affected more heavily when there is a slowdown,"" chief economist at jobs website SEEK, Blair Chapman, said.
Commonwealth Bank economist Harry Ottley said the youth unemployment rate was ""definitely rising more quickly than other cohorts"".

Lachlan Riddell has experienced what he describes as ""job hunting depression"".
""I started to fall into a bit of a slump, [asking myself], 'why am I even doing this',"" he said.
The 24-year-old estimated he had applied for hundreds of jobs over the past three years, from collecting supermarket trolleys to retail and hospitality roles.
""I feel like there is a mentality that … generation Z don't want to work, that they're very selective about what they want to do, which is simply not true,"" he said.
Lachlan Riddell says without a permanent job, he's struggling to make ends meet. (ABC News: Kate Nickels)
Mr Riddell has a Diploma of Music and works casually as an audio-visual technician, but shifts are sporadic.
He said that despite having wide-ranging work experience, he rarely received a response from employers, and most job applications were online.
He has concerns that success in Tasmania's small jobs market can depend less on what you know than who you know.
Lachlan Riddell has applied for jobs, including in hospitality. (ABC News: Sam Ikin)
As he continues his search for more permanent work, Mr Riddell said he was struggling to make ends meet on his Jobseeker payment and relied on support from his fiancee.
Graduate job ads have been trending down after post-pandemic highs, according to SEEK.
""There is anecdotal evidence that several larger firms have reduced their graduate intakes in recent years,"" Mr Chapman said.
""The big four accounting firms are a good example of this as they reduced their intakes in response to declines in demand.""
Blair Chapman says the number of graduate jobs has been trending down. (ABC News: John Gunn)
He said he suspected a slowdown in demand for graduates would continue in Australia as the economy softened.
But it is not all bad news.
The rise in youth unemployment has come alongside continued job growth for young people.
Harry Ottley says there are more young people in the labour market looking for work. (Supplied)
""The share of young people in work [the employment-to-population ratio] is 63.6 per cent, down from a peak of 66.7 per cent in November 2022, but well above its 2019 average of 59.7 per cent,"" Mr Ottley said.
He said youth participation was also high at 71.4 per cent compared with 67.7 per cent in 2019.
Blair Chapman says younger workers are often more heavily affected when there is an economic slowdown. (ABC News: Jake Grant)
There have been fears that the growing use of artificial intelligence (AI) could lead to a jobs apocalypse, particularly for young people and white-collar workers.
But a study by the Department of Employment and Workplace Relations found the technology was not yet causing broad disruption to Australia's labour market.
The study, released in July this year, also found that jobs for people aged 20 to 24 had grown slightly faster than those for people aged 25 and over since the introduction of ChatGPT, while young graduate outcomes remained resilient.
But the report did cite evidence from the US suggesting young people have experienced slower employment growth than older people since the introduction of generative AI tools, especially in occupations more exposed to AI.
Qantas last week announced 850 job cuts and plans to accelerate its use of AI.
Qantas is cutting jobs. (ABC News: Evelyn Manfield)
""We do think there'll be some job disruption at the margin, let's say, over the next five years,"" Mr Ottley said.
Federal Skills and Training Minister Andrew Giles said AI was mostly augmenting jobs, ""so changing the way in which a job is done rather than completely automating a job"".
""We've got to really focus on our skill to make sure workers are equipped to manage this transition,"" Mr Giles said.
Young people are also facing increasing competition for positions from existing job holders.
One million people in Australia have multiple jobs.
SEEK's Blair Chapman has some advice for young job seekers, particularly university graduates.

94,2026-11-14,"Health officials warn that falling numbers of recorded infections may conceal a worsening disease crisis in Yemen’s besieged Taiz.
Save
Share
Taiz, Yemen – The woman held the body of a young baby girl wrapped in a white shroud.
“Our children are dying because of the siege. We have no open road, no medics and no medicines,” the woman said.
Wearing the traditional clothing of women from Jabal Sabir, the mountain that overlooks the city of Taiz and is one of its most famous symbols, she appeared in a short video widely circulated on social media on November 10.
In her arms was the body of an infant from her family, identified as Gaza Ali Abdulmughni al-Shaqab, who had fallen ill and died after her family was unable to get her emergency medical care. The Yemeni Network for Rights and Freedoms said the vehicle carrying the baby to the hospital was attacked by Houthi forces as it travelled through mountain roads.
“She died, and we couldn’t get her emergency treatment because of the siege,” the woman said, referring to the ongoing Houthi siege of al-Shaqab, which lies on a front line between the rebel group and Yemeni government forces.
The siege of Taiz and the surrounding areas such as al-Shaqab intensified in early October, when the Houthis advanced around the city and cut off the last major supply route into government-held areas.
Fighting between the Iran-backed group and the Saudi-backed Yemeni government intensified over the summer, before the Houthis captured substantial territory along Yemen’s Red Sea coast in September and then made further advances in Taiz governorate.
Government counteroffensives have only had limited success so far, and the siege of the city of Taiz continues.
The Yemeni government has accused the Houthis of tightening their siege on nearly two million people living in Taiz for more than a month, preventing the entry of food, safe drinking water, medicines and fuel.
Human Rights Watch (HRW) said in a statement on October 29 that the siege imposed by the Houthis on civilians in Taiz “may amount to the war crime of using starvation as a method of warfare”.
The Houthis have denied responsibility for the road closures, instead blaming Yemeni government forces.
But HRW said that residents, aid workers and United Nations officials had all reported that the Houthis were to blame.
 

 
International bodies have repeatedly warned of the crisis now facing Taiz.
On October 14, the World Food Programme (WFP) said that it had been unable to reach more than 600,000 people in Taiz dependent on humanitarian assistance.
The WFP described the situation as “especially alarming”, noting that most districts in Taiz were classified as facing emergency levels of hunger.
For its part, the UN Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (OCHA) warned that health services in Taiz were failing because of a shortage of supplies.
Nearly 250,000 people had lost access to essential healthcare, including more than 186,000 children younger than five years old, the agency said.
It added that more than 1,400 cases of acute diarrhoea and suspected cholera were reported in just one week.
“Twelve hospitals are at risk of closing or have had to reduce services due to a lack of fuel and critical supplies,” OCHA added.
According to figures obtained by Al Jazeera from the Epidemiological Surveillance Department at Taiz’s Public Health and Population Office, 5,642 cases across three disease categories were recorded during the latest four-week reporting period.
The vast majority – 5,228 cases – were of acute watery diarrhoea. There were also 232 suspected dengue fever cases and 182 suspected cholera cases.
The number of watery diarrhoea cases decreased compared with the previous reporting period, but Dr Yasin Abdulmalik, the director of the department, said that was likely the result of residents finding it increasingly difficult to reach health facilities because of road closures between the city and surrounding rural areas.
“The siege has led to a shortage of petroleum products, including fuel, whose prices have risen on the black market,” Abdulmalik said. “This has created an additional difficulty in travelling to hospitals and health centres for treatment because of higher transport fares.”
“In reality, more than twice this number of cases were unable to reach [health facilities] because of the siege and its various consequences,” he said.
Taysir al-Samiei, a health communications official at Taiz’s Public Health and Population Office, told Al Jazeera that patients’ ability to reach hospitals had fallen by between 60 and 70 percent by the end of the second week of the siege.
Dr Fares al-Absi, a consultant in diabetes, endocrinology and obesity, said the reported high number of acute watery diarrhoea and cholera cases was down to the use of unsafe water sources.
Al-Absi said the situation had worsened “after the Houthis prevented safe drinking water from entering the city, except for limited quantities sold at high prices, which have risen dramatically because of the shortage of diesel”.
“Recently, access to the al-Dhabab water basin, a major source of water transported by trucks, has been restricted,” al-Absi added. “Only about 8 percent of Taiz’s water needs are currently being met, while most local wells are unfit for consumption.”
Al-Absi also explained that dengue fever was spreading because of the conditions in the city.
The disease is transmitted by mosquitoes that breed in stagnant water, while accumulated rubbish can also create conditions that favour mosquito breeding.
Al-Absi said that tonnes of waste had accumulated in Taiz’s streets because of insufficient diesel to transport the rubbish. He also accused the Houthis of preventing sanitation trucks from reaching the landfill in the west of the city.
“Both stagnant water and rubbish have increased significantly as a result of the siege, as streets and residential neighbourhoods have turned into open rubbish dumps,” he said.
